- **Score**: 7/10

### TGDPO: Harnessing Token-Level Reward Guidance for Enhancing Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14574v1)
- **Authors**: Mingkang Zhu, Xi Chen, Zhongdao Wang, Bei Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning from human feedback have shown that utilizing fine-grained token-level reward models can substantially enhance the performance of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in aligning large language models. However, it is challenging to leverage such token-level reward as guidance for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), since DPO is formulated as a sequence-level bandit problem. To address this challenge, this work decomposes the sequence-level PPO into a sequence of token-level proximal policy optimization problems and then frames the problem of token-level PPO with token-level reward guidance, from which closed-form optimal token-level policy and the corresponding token-level reward can be derived. Using the obtained reward and Bradley-Terry model, this work establishes a framework of computable loss functions with token-level reward guidance for DPO, and proposes a practical reward guidance based on the induced DPO reward. This formulation enables different tokens to exhibit varying degrees of deviation from reference policy based on their respective rewards. Experiment results demonstrate that our method achieves substantial performance improvements over DPO, with win rate gains of up to 7.5 points on MT-Bench, 6.2 points on AlpacaEval 2, and 4.3 points on Arena-Hard. Code is available at https://github.com/dvlab-research/TGDPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Token-Guided Direct Preference Optimization (TGDPO), a method for improving Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) by incorporating token-level reward guidance.  TGDPO decomposes the sequence-level PPO problem inherent in DPO into a series of token-level PPO problems. This decomposition enables the integration of token-level reward signals. The authors derive a closed-form optimal token-level policy and corresponding reward, and then use this to formulate a new loss function for DPO incorporating token-level guidance using the Bradley-Terry model. A practical reward guidance based on the induced DPO reward is also proposed. Experimental results on MT-Bench, AlpacaEval 2, and Arena-Hard demonstrate that TGDPO consistently outperforms existing DPO methods. The paper further analyzes TGDPO's convergence properties, robustness, and controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the decomposition of sequence-level DPO into a token-level optimization problem. While token-level rewards have been previously used in RLHF, their integration into DPO presents unique challenges since DPO's reward is implicitly defined through the policy itself. The derivation of a closed-form solution for the token-level policy and reward, along with the resulting TGDPO loss function, represents a significant algorithmic contribution. The theoretical justification for eliminating the partition function with the Bradley-Terry model is also notable. The practical reward guidance based on DPO is a nice addition for ease of use.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of TGDPO is significant. By leveraging token-level rewards, TGDPO could lead to more efficient and effective alignment of LLMs with human preferences. The demonstrated improvements in win rates across several benchmark datasets suggest that TGDPO is a promising approach. The analysis of convergence, robustness, and controllability further enhances the practical value of the method. The findings that TGDPO achieves convergence with satisfying policies and that the speed is controllable addresses a critical challenge to previous methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:**  The paper provides a solid theoretical foundation for TGDPO, including the derivation of the optimal token-level policy and reward.
    *   **Algorithmic Contribution:** The decomposition approach and the TGDPO loss function are novel and well-motivated.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements over existing DPO methods across multiple datasets.
    *   **Analysis:** The analysis of TGDPO's convergence properties, robustness, and controllability provides valuable insights into the method's behavior and practical utility.
    *   **Code Availability:**  The availability of code facilitates reproducibility and adoption by other researchers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The mathematical derivations might be difficult for some readers to follow. Although, they are necessary to support the contribution.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Reward Functions:** Although practical rewards were mentioned, a more extensive exploration of different token-level reward guidance mechanisms could be beneficial.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The token-level analysis will likely increase the computations in relation to the baseline, which is not mentioned.

*   **Potential Influence:** TGDPO has the potential to become a valuable tool for aligning LLMs with human preferences, particularly in situations where fine-grained control over the generated text is desired. The method's robustness and controllability could also make it more attractive for practical applications.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a novel and effective approach for incorporating token-level reward guidance into DPO. The theoretical foundation is strong, the algorithm is well-motivated, and the experimental results demonstrate compelling improvements over existing methods. The added analysis provides additional information to make the paper more attractive. The identified weaknesses are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from the overall value of the work. The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM alignment and to be adopted by practitioners in the field. Therefore, a score of 8 is warranted, reflecting the paper's substantial novelty, significance, and potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### GenerationPrograms: Fine-grained Attribution with Executable Programs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14580v1)
- **Authors**: David Wan, Eran Hirsch, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Ido Dagan, Mohit Bansal
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) achieve impressive performance in source-conditioned text generation but often fail to correctly provide fine-grained attributions for their outputs, undermining verifiability and trust. Moreover, existing attribution methods do not explain how and why models leverage the provided source documents to generate their final responses, limiting interpretability. To overcome these challenges, we introduce a modular generation framework, GenerationPrograms, inspired by recent advancements in executable "code agent" architectures. Unlike conventional generation methods that simultaneously generate outputs and attributions or rely on post-hoc attribution, GenerationPrograms decomposes the process into two distinct stages: first, creating an executable program plan composed of modular text operations (such as paraphrasing, compression, and fusion) explicitly tailored to the query, and second, executing these operations following the program's specified instructions to produce the final response. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that GenerationPrograms significantly improves attribution quality at both the document level and sentence level across two long-form question-answering tasks and a multi-document summarization task. We further demonstrate that GenerationPrograms can effectively function as a post-hoc attribution method, outperforming traditional techniques in recovering accurate attributions. In addition, the interpretable programs generated by GenerationPrograms enable localized refinement through modular-level improvements that further enhance overall attribution quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GENERATIONPROGRAMS: Fine-grained Attribution with Executable Programs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GENERATIONPROGRAMS, a novel framework for source-conditioned text generation that aims to improve the accuracy and interpretability of attributions. Unlike traditional methods that generate outputs and attributions simultaneously or rely on post-hoc attribution, GENERATIONPROGRAMS decomposes the process into two distinct stages: 1) creating an executable program plan composed of modular text operations (paraphrasing, compression, fusion, etc.) tailored to the query; and 2) executing these operations following the program's instructions to produce the final response. By explicitly tracking the source inputs used at each modular step, this framework significantly enhances attribution quality. The paper demonstrates that GENERATIONPROGRAMS improves attribution quality across various tasks, including long-form question answering and multi-document summarization. It also shows the framework's utility as a post-hoc attribution method and its ability to enable localized refinement through modular-level improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in its explicit, program-based approach to attributed generation.  While modular text generation and "code agent" architectures are becoming more prevalent, the explicit connection to *attribution* and the decomposition of the generation process into auditable and editable steps is a strong contribution.  The concept of "contributive attribution" rather than merely "corroborative" is also a significant emphasis.  The post-hoc attribution application is a clever re-framing of the problem. However, the individual modules (paraphrasing, compression, fusion) are not novel on their own.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in large language models: the lack of reliable attributions, which hinders verifiability and trust. By providing a framework that produces interpretable programs and accurate attributions, GENERATIONPROGRAMS has the potential to significantly improve the trustworthiness of generated content. The ability to perform modular-level refinement is also a valuable feature that enables targeted corrections and improvements. The gains in attribution quality, especially in comparison to direct generation methods like ALCE, are substantial. However, a limitation is the observed trade-off between answer correctness and attribution quality, requiring further investigation. The exploration of long-context summarization to mitigate this is a strong direction.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Improved Attribution:**  Demonstrated substantial improvements in attribution accuracy across various tasks and metrics.
    * **Interpretability:**  The executable program plan provides a clear and understandable explanation of the generation process.
    * **Modular Refinement:** Enables localized edits and improvements at the module level.
    * **Post-hoc Attribution:**  Can be used to attribute previously generated text, even from black-box models.
    * **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** Thoroughly evaluated on multiple datasets with strong baselines.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Trade-off with Answer Correctness:**  Observed a slight reduction in answer correctness in some settings. Further investigation is needed to understand and mitigate this trade-off. The human eval and stronger LLM metric results, however, suggest this is not as substantial as indicated by EM and Rouge-L.
    * **Reliance on LLMs:**  The framework still relies heavily on the capabilities of LLMs for program generation and module execution. While the modular design improves control and interpretability, the underlying modules are still subject to the limitations of LLMs.
    * **Limited Module Set:** While the current module set is reasonable, the framework's ultimate effectiveness hinges on the breadth and quality of available text operations.  A more extensive library of modules would further enhance its capabilities.
    * **Stylistic divergence from ALCE. The output length of GENERATIONPROGRAMS is longer, suggesting potential verbosity and further analysis is required.
    * **Scalability:** The increased computational cost, as shown in the table 9, indicates potential scalability issues.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in attributed generation, interpretable AI, and modular text processing.  The explicit program-based approach could inspire new methods for generating trustworthy and verifiable content.  The framework's adaptability to post-hoc attribution also opens up possibilities for analyzing and improving existing generation models.

**Justification for Score:**

While the individual components used by GENERATIONPROGRAMS are not completely novel, the way they are combined, the explicit focus on *attribution*, and the resulting gains in interpretability and reliability represent a significant advance in the field. It successfully addresses a key limitation of LLMs by providing a framework for generating verifiable and controllable outputs. The trade-off with answer correctness, while requiring further investigation, doesn't negate the value of the improved attribution, and the stylistic divergence and potential scalability issues still need to be examined.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Busting the Paper Ballot: Voting Meets Adversarial Machine Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14582v1)
- **Authors**: Kaleel Mahmood, Caleb Manicke, Ethan Rathbun, Aayushi Verma, Sohaib Ahmad, Nicholas Stamatakis, Laurent Michel, Benjamin Fuller
- **Abstract**: We show the security risk associated with using machine learning classifiers in United States election tabulators. The central classification task in election tabulation is deciding whether a mark does or does not appear on a bubble associated to an alternative in a contest on the ballot. Barretto et al. (E-Vote-ID 2021) reported that convolutional neural networks are a viable option in this field, as they outperform simple feature-based classifiers. Our contributions to election security can be divided into four parts. To demonstrate and analyze the hypothetical vulnerability of machine learning models on election tabulators, we first introduce four new ballot datasets. Second, we train and test a variety of different models on our new datasets. These models include support vector machines, convolutional neural networks (a basic CNN, VGG and ResNet), and vision transformers (Twins and CaiT). Third, using our new datasets and trained models, we demonstrate that traditional white box attacks are ineffective in the voting domain due to gradient masking. Our analyses further reveal that gradient masking is a product of numerical instability. We use a modified difference of logits ratio loss to overcome this issue (Croce and Hein, ICML 2020). Fourth, in the physical world, we conduct attacks with the adversarial examples generated using our new methods. In traditional adversarial machine learning, a high (50% or greater) attack success rate is ideal. However, for certain elections, even a 5% attack success rate can flip the outcome of a race. We show such an impact is possible in the physical domain. We thoroughly discuss attack realism, and the challenges and practicality associated with printing and scanning ballot adversarial examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of machine learning classifiers used in U.S. election tabulators to adversarial attacks, focusing on the binary classification task of determining whether a mark exists in a bubble on a ballot. The authors introduce four new ballot datasets and train various models (SVMs, CNNs, Transformers) on these datasets. They demonstrate that traditional white-box attacks fail due to gradient masking, which they attribute to numerical instability. They overcome this issue by modifying the difference of logits ratio (DLR) loss and conduct physical-world attacks to demonstrate the impact of adversarial examples on election outcomes, even with small attack success rates. The paper thoroughly discusses the challenges of printing and scanning ballot adversarial examples and their impact on election security.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a compelling and important topic. The application of adversarial machine learning to voting systems is novel, particularly the exploration of physical-world attacks and their potential impact on elections. Identifying and addressing gradient masking in this specific domain also shows a useful contribution. The creation of specific datasets for the voting domain is very useful.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for election security. The paper highlights a potential vulnerability that could be exploited to manipulate election outcomes, even with relatively low attack success rates. By showcasing the weaknesses of machine learning models used in tabulators and providing a method to overcome gradient masking, the authors contribute to a deeper understanding of the risks involved.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper's investigation of gradient masking and its mitigation in the context of voting systems is valuable.
    *   The physical-world experiments add a crucial layer of realism to the analysis.
    *   The creation and public release of the datasets are a strong contribution, providing a valuable resource for other researchers.
    *   The thorough discussion of the challenges associated with printing and scanning adversarial examples is insightful.
    *   The analysis of the impact of even small attack success rates on election outcomes is well-reasoned and compelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The disclaimer emphasizing that the targeted models are not currently used by any tabulator manufacturer might weaken the direct real-world impact of the work. While the concepts apply generally to such systems, concrete examples with actual systems (if possible) are needed.
    *   The reliance on COTS equipment might not be the most realistic, as tabulators may employ specialized hardware. The discussion of how this impacts the results in the paper is thorough.
    *   The work could benefit from further exploration of potential defenses against the identified attacks.

*   **Impact:** The paper will influence the discussion about the application of machine learning in election systems. It could also influence vendor and election official testing practices.

**Justification for Score:**

This paper is a valuable contribution to election security and adversarial machine learning, presenting a credible threat and method of attack on election systems. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses in this work.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### NetRoller: Interfacing General and Specialized Models for End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14589v1)
- **Authors**: Ren Xin, Hongji Liu, Xiaodong Mei, Wenru Liu, Maosheng Ye, Zhili Chen, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Integrating General Models (GMs) such as Large Language Models (LLMs), with Specialized Models (SMs) in autonomous driving tasks presents a promising approach to mitigating challenges in data diversity and model capacity of existing specialized driving models. However, this integration leads to problems of asynchronous systems, which arise from the distinct characteristics inherent in GMs and SMs. To tackle this challenge, we propose NetRoller, an adapter that incorporates a set of novel mechanisms to facilitate the seamless integration of GMs and specialized driving models. Specifically, our mechanisms for interfacing the asynchronous GMs and SMs are organized into three key stages. NetRoller first harvests semantically rich and computationally efficient representations from the reasoning processes of LLMs using an early stopping mechanism, which preserves critical insights on driving context while maintaining low overhead. It then applies learnable query embeddings, nonsensical embeddings, and positional layer embeddings to facilitate robust and efficient cross-modality translation. At last, it employs computationally efficient Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms to enhance the performance of SMs through few-epoch fine-tuning. Based on the mechanisms formalized in these three stages, NetRoller enables specialized driving models to operate at their native frequencies while maintaining situational awareness of the GM. Experiments conducted on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that integrating GM through NetRoller significantly improves human similarity and safety in planning tasks, and it also achieves noticeable precision improvements in detection and mapping tasks for end-to-end autonomous driving. The code and models are available at https://github.com/Rex-sys-hk/NetRoller .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NetRoller, an adapter designed to integrate General Models (GMs), specifically Large Language Models (LLMs), with Specialized Models (SMs) for end-to-end autonomous driving. It addresses the challenges arising from asynchronous systems due to the distinct characteristics of GMs and SMs. NetRoller employs three key mechanisms: (1) harvesting semantically rich representations from LLM reasoning using early stopping, (2) applying learnable query embeddings, nonsensical embeddings, and positional layer embeddings for robust cross-modality translation, and (3) utilizing Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms for SM performance enhancement through fine-tuning. The approach enables SMs to operate at their native frequencies while maintaining GM-derived situational awareness. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate improvements in human similarity, safety in planning, and precision in detection and mapping tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a significant challenge:** Integrating GMs with SMs is a recognized area of importance for advancing autonomous driving, particularly for improving robustness and generalizability. The paper directly tackles the asynchronous nature of GM-SM interaction, a practical hurdle for real-world deployment.
    *   **Novel adapter design:** The proposed NetRoller adapter with its three-stage mechanism appears well-engineered. The individual components like early stopping, learnable embeddings, and shift mechanisms seem thoughtfully designed to address specific aspects of the GM-SM integration problem. The inclusion of "nonsense" embeddings to maintain system robustness in the absence of GM output is a clever design choice.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experiments on the nuScenes dataset offer empirical evidence of the effectiveness of NetRoller. The use of multiple evaluation metrics (NDS, mAP, Similarity, Safety) provides a holistic view of the performance improvements in various aspects of autonomous driving. The frame-wise analysis offers insights into the stability and robustness of the approach.
    *   **Ablation studies are thorough:** The thorough ablation studies help to understand the contributions of different components in NetRoller, strengthening the claim that the adapter is well-designed and that individual elements contribute to the overall performance.
    *   **Well-written and clearly presented:** The paper is well-organized, technically sound, and presents the methodology and results clearly.  The inclusion of diagrams aids in understanding the architecture. The tables are also comprehensive and well-designed.
    *   **Open-sourced code and models:** Making the code and models available increases the impact and reproducibility of the work, encouraging further research in the area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental contribution:** While the approach is well-engineered, the novelty might be perceived as incremental rather than revolutionary. The individual components (e.g., Q-Former, fine-tuning) are based on existing techniques, and the core innovation lies in their specific configuration within the NetRoller framework. The extent to which the combination of these techniques represents a truly novel advance may be questioned.
    *   **Dependency on specific baselines:** The evaluation relies on specific baselines like VAD. While reasonable, it raises the question of how NetRoller's performance would compare when integrated with other state-of-the-art E2E-AD models. The improvement over other models with GM is still not explored here.
    *   **Limited generalization:** The evaluation is limited to the nuScenes dataset. Assessing the performance of NetRoller on other datasets with different characteristics (e.g., weather conditions, traffic density, geographical locations) would further strengthen the generalizability of the approach.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   The work provides a practical and effective solution for integrating LLMs into autonomous driving systems, which could accelerate the development of more robust and human-like driving agents.
    *   The detailed methodology and ablation studies offer valuable insights for researchers working on similar problems.
    *   The open-sourced code and models can serve as a benchmark and starting point for future research in this area.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a significant engineering effort that addresses a practical challenge in autonomous driving. While the novelty of the individual components is somewhat limited, the overall architecture of NetRoller, the thorough experimentation, and the clear presentation of results justify a positive assessment. However, the incremental nature of the contribution and the limited evaluation in terms of datasets prevent it from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14603v1)
- **Authors**: Amirmojtaba Sabour, Sanja Fidler, Karsten Kreis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion- and flow-based models have emerged as state-of-the-art generative modeling approaches, but they require many sampling steps. Consistency models can distill these models into efficient one-step generators; however, unlike flow- and diffusion-based methods, their performance inevitably degrades when increasing the number of steps, which we show both analytically and empirically. Flow maps generalize these approaches by connecting any two noise levels in a single step and remain effective across all step counts. In this paper, we introduce two new continuous-time objectives for training flow maps, along with additional novel training techniques, generalizing existing consistency and flow matching objectives. We further demonstrate that autoguidance can improve performance, using a low-quality model for guidance during distillation, and an additional boost can be achieved by adversarial finetuning, with minimal loss in sample diversity. We extensively validate our flow map models, called Align Your Flow, on challenging image generation benchmarks and achieve state-of-the-art few-step generation performance on both ImageNet 64x64 and 512x512, using small and efficient neural networks. Finally, we show text-to-image flow map models that outperform all existing non-adversarially trained few-step samplers in text-conditioned synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Align Your Flow" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation" introduces a novel approach to distill diffusion and flow-based generative models into efficient few-step samplers.  The key contribution is the development of two new continuous-time training objectives for flow maps (AYF-EMD and AYF-LMD), which generalize existing consistency and flow matching losses. The paper demonstrates that these flow maps, dubbed Align Your Flow (AYF), maintain performance across different numbers of sampling steps, unlike consistency models which degrade with more steps.  The authors also leverage autoguidance for improved performance and introduce a technique for adversarial fine-tuning that boosts quality without sacrificing sample diversity.  The paper validates AYF on ImageNet, achieving state-of-the-art few-step generation performance with small and efficient networks, and demonstrates strong results in text-to-image synthesis using a LoRA fine-tuned FLUX.1 model.  A key analytical result proves that consistency models are inherently flawed in multi-step generation due to error accumulation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers significant novelty in several areas. The analytical proof demonstrating the limitations of consistency models in multi-step generation is a valuable theoretical contribution. The two new continuous-time flow map training objectives (AYF-EMD and AYF-LMD) are a clear advance, generalizing existing loss functions and enabling robust few-step generation. The successful incorporation of autoguidance into the distillation process and the demonstration that adversarial fine-tuning can improve results with minimal impact on diversity are also novel and practical improvements. The idea of leveraging the Y sampling algorithm for stochastic multistep sampling of flow map models is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of generative modeling by addressing the computational cost of diffusion and flow-based models. Distilling these models into efficient few-step samplers is highly relevant for practical applications. The state-of-the-art results on ImageNet and the competitive performance in text-to-image synthesis demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The theoretical understanding of the limitations of consistency models provides a solid foundation for future research in this area. The approach of first demonstrating the limitations of existing models and then addressing it with new model design, sets a good precedent for new research direction.

*   **Strengths:** The paper combines strong theoretical analysis with empirical validation. The analytical proof regarding consistency models is rigorous, and the experimental results on ImageNet and text-to-image synthesis are compelling. The authors also provide detailed implementation details and ablation studies, enhancing the reproducibility and understanding of their work. The paper clearly highlights the advantages of the proposed approach over existing methods, such as consistency models and shortcut models.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper's results for *one-step* AYF are slightly worse than existing methods such as sCD/sCT. While the authors address this with a brief adversarial finetuning step, it highlights that AYF needs additional training for it to be a *drop-in* replacement. Some of the visual examples in the appendix (e.g. multi-step AYF samples) have limited zoom quality, which degrades them and could be improved.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a substantial impact on the field of generative modeling. The proposed AYF method offers a promising approach for efficiently distilling diffusion and flow-based models, enabling faster generation times and reduced computational costs. The analytical understanding of the limitations of consistency models could shift the direction of future research, prompting more exploration of flow map based approaches.

*   **Overall:** This paper is well-written and offers a novel framework. The analytical proof alone is of significant value, and the proposed modifications and experimental results support its claims well.

**Score: 8.5**

**Justification:** The paper offers a compelling advancement in distillation for generative models, demonstrating strong theoretical foundations, significant performance improvements, and valuable insights regarding multi-step sampling with different model types. The combination of analytical understanding, novel objectives, and strong empirical results makes this a significant contribution to the field, however the lack of out-of-the-box performance compared to one-step specialist models is a shortcoming of the model.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Guaranteed Guess: A Language Modeling Approach for CISC-to-RISC Transpilation with Testing Guarantees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14606v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Heakl, Sarim Hashmi, Chaimaa Abi, Celine Lee, Abdulrahman Mahmoud
- **Abstract**: The hardware ecosystem is rapidly evolving, with increasing interest in translating low-level programs across different instruction set architectures (ISAs) in a quick, flexible, and correct way to enhance the portability and longevity of existing code. A particularly challenging class of this transpilation problem is translating between complex- (CISC) and reduced- (RISC) hardware architectures, due to fundamental differences in instruction complexity, memory models, and execution paradigms. In this work, we introduce GG (Guaranteed Guess), an ISA-centric transpilation pipeline that combines the translation power of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with the rigor of established software testing constructs. Our method generates candidate translations using an LLM from one ISA to another, and embeds such translations within a software-testing framework to build quantifiable confidence in the translation. We evaluate our GG approach over two diverse datasets, enforce high code coverage (>98%) across unit tests, and achieve functional/semantic correctness of 99% on HumanEval programs and 49% on BringupBench programs, respectively. Further, we compare our approach to the state-of-the-art Rosetta 2 framework on Apple Silicon, showcasing 1.73x faster runtime performance, 1.47x better energy efficiency, and 2.41x better memory usage for our transpiled code, demonstrating the effectiveness of GG for real-world CISC-to-RISC translation tasks. We will open-source our codes, data, models, and benchmarks to establish a common foundation for ISA-level code translation research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Guaranteed Guess (GG), a novel assembly-to-assembly transpiler designed to translate x86 (CISC) binaries into efficient ARM or RISC-V (RISC) equivalents. The approach combines the power of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with a rigorous software testing framework. GG generates candidate translations using an LLM, then validates these translations within a software-testing environment to ensure quantifiable functional correctness. The method employs hardware-informed design choices, tokenizer extensions, and context-aware training to enhance LLM performance in this specialized domain. Experimental results demonstrate high functional correctness (up to 99% on HumanEval) and competitive performance compared to Apple's Rosetta 2, including faster runtime, improved energy efficiency, and reduced memory usage. The authors open-source their code, data, models, and benchmarks to foster further research in ISA-level code translation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** Combining LLMs with software testing constructs for assembly-to-assembly transpilation is a fresh approach. While LLMs have been used for high-level code translation, their application to low-level ISAs presents unique challenges that this paper addresses directly.
*   **Hardware-Aware Design:** Integrating hardware-informed design, tokenizer extensions, and context-aware training demonstrates a deep understanding of the nuances of ISA translation. This is crucial for achieving accurate and efficient transpilation.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper utilizes diverse datasets (HumanEval-C and BringUpBench) and metrics (functional correctness and code coverage) to thoroughly evaluate GG's performance. The comparison with Rosetta 2 provides real-world context and highlights the potential benefits of GG.
*   **Open-Source Contribution:** The commitment to open-source their work is a significant strength. This allows other researchers to build upon and validate their findings, accelerating progress in the field.
*   **Performance Gains:** Demonstrating a significant speedup, energy efficiency, and memory efficiency improvement over Rosetta 2 is very strong evidence supporting the claim that their transpilation method is effective.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on Test Suites:** The "guarantee" of correctness is limited by the quality and coverage of the unit tests. It is still possible that the LLM-generated code passes all the unit tests but is functionally incorrect for certain inputs that weren't part of the test suite.
*   **Performance Degradation with Optimizations:** The significant drop in accuracy under aggressive compiler optimizations (-O2) is a concern.  While acknowledged, a more in-depth investigation into the root causes and potential mitigation strategies would be valuable. It needs to generalize better for optimized code that often occurs in real world scenarios.
*   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** The paper primarily compares against Rosetta 2 and other coding LLMs which aren't specifically designed for binary translation. A comparison against other binary translation or emulation techniques (e.g., QEMU with different translation strategies, or other compilation tools) would further strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Scalability Issues:** While the authors provide reasons for the memory bottleneck (choice of model), they fail to address possible alternative solutions which makes it not fully generalizable.
*   **No analysis of errors caused by edge-case inputs:** The evaluation might not cover edge-case/malicious inputs that might lead to incorrect or non-deterministic behavior.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to address the growing software gap caused by the shift towards ARM-based architectures. GG offers a promising approach for efficiently translating legacy x86 binaries to ARM, enabling broader software portability and longevity. The work opens new avenues for research in LLM-based binary translation, particularly in the areas of hardware-aware training, test-driven validation, and optimization-invariant representations.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. While the paper presents a novel and promising approach with demonstrable performance benefits, the limitations surrounding the test-driven "guarantee", the accuracy drop under optimizations, the limited baseline comparisons, and scalability concerns prevent a higher score. The reliance on unit tests for correctness is inherently limiting, and the drop-off in performance with optimized code is a concern for real-world applicability. Despite these weaknesses, the paper offers a significant contribution to the field by showing that LLMs, when trained with hardware context and combined with rigorous testing, can effectively translate x86 assembly to other architectures. It offers a unique architecture and could influence the field of CISC-RISC translation, providing a viable alternative to virtualization.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Exploring MLLMs Perception of Network Visualization Principles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14611v1)
- **Authors**: Jacob Miller, Markus Wallinger, Ludwig Felder, Timo Brand, Henry Förster, Johannes Zink, Chunyang Chen, Stephen Kobourov
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we test whether Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can match human-subject performance in tasks involving the perception of properties in network layouts. Specifically, we replicate a human-subject experiment about perceiving quality (namely stress) in network layouts using GPT-4o and Gemini-2.5. Our experiments show that giving MLLMs exactly the same study information as trained human participants results in a similar performance to human experts and exceeds the performance of untrained non-experts. Additionally, we show that prompt engineering that deviates from the human-subject experiment can lead to better-than-human performance in some settings. Interestingly, like human subjects, the MLLMs seem to rely on visual proxies rather than computing the actual value of stress, indicating some sense or facsimile of perception. Explanations from the models provide descriptions similar to those used by the human participants (e.g., even distribution of nodes and uniform edge lengths).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), specifically GPT-4o and Gemini 2.5, to perceive network visualization principles. It replicates a human-subject experiment that gauges the ability to identify diagrams with lower stress (a measure of layout quality). The study examines how closely the MLLMs can match human performance under varying conditions (different instructions/training levels). The research reveals that MLLMs can achieve human-level accuracy and, with careful prompt engineering, potentially exceed it. Like humans, the models seem to rely on visual proxies (e.g., edge crossings, node distribution) rather than computing the stress metric directly. The paper also analyzes the MLLMs' explanations for their choices and correlates their answers with network layout properties.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic investigation of MLLM's ability to understand and evaluate network visualizations, a relatively unexplored area. While previous studies have examined LLMs' general visualization literacy, this work delves specifically into the perception of stress in network layouts, a more complex and nuanced task. Replicating a human study is a strength and helps to control the novelty of the task itself, and focus on MLLM abilities.
*   **Significance:** The findings have several potential implications:
    *   **Pilot Studies:** MLLMs could become valuable tools for piloting human-subject visualization studies, providing rapid feedback on potential design flaws, difficulty levels, and floor/ceiling effects.
    *   **Assistive Tools:** MLLMs could be incorporated into visualization tools to offer automated critiques and suggestions for layout improvements.
    *   **Limitations of MLLMs:**  The paper highlights the risk that MLLMs will potentially participate in user studies in place of humans, which is both ethically and methodologically problematic.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  Replicating a well-defined human-subject experiment offers a solid foundation for comparison. The use of bootstrapping for statistical analysis is appropriate, given the nature of the data. The paper also gives great detail on methodology, including prompts, which will aid in replicability.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The exploration of different prompt strategies (Trained, Untrained, Expert, Tuned) is valuable for understanding how to best elicit accurate responses from MLLMs.
    *   **Analysis of Explanations:**  Analyzing the models' explanations provides insights into their decision-making processes and their reliance on visual proxies.
    *   **Clear Limitations:** The authors explicitly acknowledge limitations (model choices, single perceptual task, dependence on closed-source models), which enhances the paper's credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Dependence:** The reliance on GPT-4o and Gemini 2.5, both closed-source models, limits the generalizability and replicability of the findings. The specific architectures and training data of these models influence their performance.
    *   **Narrow Task:** The focus on a single perceptual task (stress identification) restricts the scope of the conclusions.  It is not clear how well these findings generalize to other visualization tasks (e.g., identifying clusters, finding shortest paths, etc.).
    *   **Limited "Understanding":**  While the MLLMs achieve good accuracy, the paper doesn't fully demonstrate a *deep* understanding of stress. The reliance on visual proxies suggests a more superficial assessment. The paper also mentions cases where the models gave reasoning that contradicted its answer, indicating a flaw in understanding.

*   **Overall Influence:**  The study provides valuable initial insights into MLLM capabilities with network visualizations. It is a contribution towards understanding how to evaluate network visualization qualities, rather than an innovation in the development of tools or a novel type of network visualization. The research also opens avenues for future research on MLLMs in visualization and calls for caution in interpreting their responses as equivalent to genuine human understanding.
*  **Rigorous Rationale:** This paper delivers a solid experimental design and provides an in-depth analysis of how two state-of-the-art MLLMs perform on a complex perceptual task in network visualizations. It identifies strengths and limitations in the MLLMs understanding of visual quality metrics, and also shows some of the possible negative outcomes if they are used uncritically. It is not groundbreaking in terms of visualizations or new knowledge about MLLMs, but is more of a systematic study of an important topic.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper contributes to the growing field of MLLM evaluation in visualization, but its novelty is constrained by the limitations mentioned above. While the methodology is strong and the findings are insightful, the reliance on closed-source models and the narrow task scope temper the paper's significance. Despite this, it is more than just an incremental study, and provides some useful insights on MLLMs ability to understand visualizations. This makes the paper better than merely acceptable, but not an outstanding piece of work.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Probabilistic Aggregation and Targeted Embedding Optimization for Collective Moral Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14625v2)
- **Authors**: Chenchen Yuan, Zheyu Zhang, Shuo Yang, Bardh Prenkaj, Gjergji Kasneci
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown impressive moral reasoning abilities. Yet they often diverge when confronted with complex, multi-factor moral dilemmas. To address these discrepancies, we propose a framework that synthesizes multiple LLMs' moral judgments into a collectively formulated moral judgment, realigning models that deviate significantly from this consensus. Our aggregation mechanism fuses continuous moral acceptability scores (beyond binary labels) into a collective probability, weighting contributions by model reliability. For misaligned models, a targeted embedding-optimization procedure fine-tunes token embeddings for moral philosophical theories, minimizing JS divergence to the consensus while preserving semantic integrity. Experiments on a large-scale social moral dilemma dataset show our approach builds robust consensus and improves individual model fidelity. These findings highlight the value of data-driven moral alignment across multiple models and its potential for safer, more consistent AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Probabilistic Aggregation and Targeted Embedding Optimization for Collective Moral Reasoning in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of inconsistent moral reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) when faced with complex, multi-factor moral dilemmas.  It proposes a two-pronged approach: 1) a novel "truncated-normal Expectation-Maximization (EM)" method to aggregate moral judgments from multiple LLMs into a collective moral reference, weighting each model's contribution based on its reliability; and 2) a "targeted embedding-optimization" procedure to fine-tune the token embeddings related to specific moral theories for LLMs that consistently deviate from the consensus. This fine-tuning aims to improve alignment with the collective understanding while preserving semantic integrity. The approach is evaluated on a large-scale social moral dilemma dataset derived from the AITA (Am I The Asshole) subreddit, demonstrating improved model consistency and fidelity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach.  While aggregation methods have been used in crowdsourcing, adapting EM with a truncated-normal distribution to *continuous* moral acceptability scores and applying it to *LLMs* is a unique contribution.  The targeted embedding optimization, focusing specifically on moral theory tokens and validating alignment against a *model-derived* consensus (rather than human labels), also demonstrates originality. The combination of these two aspects is novel.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant challenge in the responsible development of AI: ensuring consistent and reliable moral reasoning. By moving beyond simple binary moral judgments and accounting for nuance with continuous scores, the paper provides a valuable tool for analyzing complex scenarios. The model-driven alignment process is particularly relevant, as it avoids reliance on potentially biased or limited human annotations and aims to find a *shared*, albeit model-based, moral compass. The results show meaningful improvements in inter-model agreement and alignment with the constructed consensus. This is potentially valuable for building safer and more predictable AI systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Complex Problem:** The paper tackles a real-world issue of LLM inconsistency in moral reasoning, going beyond simplistic scenarios.
    *   **Principled Methodology:** The EM-based aggregation and targeted embedding optimization are well-motivated and grounded in statistical principles.
    *   **Data-Driven Alignment:** Fine-tuning based on consensus, rather than relying solely on human labels, helps create a shared, model-derived "moral stance."
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:**  The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation on a large dataset. Including multiple LLM models helps strengthen the validity of their approach.
    *   **Intriguing Insights:** The analysis of inter-theory correlations and token embedding projections provides valuable insights into how LLMs represent and reason about moral concepts.
    *   **Code availability**: The code and data are publicly available.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Model-Driven Consensus:**  The "ground truth" is derived from other LLMs, potentially perpetuating existing biases or limitations within those models. While the authors acknowledge this, further exploration of how the consensus is affected by the composition of the LLM pool would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Model Scope:** While using a variety of LLMs, the models are primarily from a certain subset of architectures and training paradigms. Further work is needed to see if it generalizes across other types of models.
    *   **Ethical Considerations around LLMs:** LLMs are not fully understood in terms of their reasoning and may perpetuate harmful biases.
    *   **Limited Human Validation:** While a pilot human evaluation is conducted, its scope is fairly limited, and the validity might vary since humans could themselves have biased viewpoints.
    *   **Reliance on Summarization:** Using GPT-4o to summarize AITA threads may introduce other problems such as summarization errors.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research in several ways:

    *   **Model Aggregation:** Encouraging the development of more robust methods for aggregating outputs from multiple LLMs, going beyond simple averaging or majority voting.
    *   **Fine-Grained Alignment:** Promoting the use of targeted fine-tuning techniques to align LLMs on specific domains or ethical principles.
    *   **Understanding Moral Reasoning:** Inspiring further investigations into how LLMs represent and reason about complex social and ethical issues.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the reasonably novel methods, significant real-world problem being addressed, and the comprehensive evaluation, but accounting for the limitations in human validation and LLM model scope, a score of 7.5 out of 10 seems appropriate.  The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, provides a solid foundation for future research, and offers a promising approach for improving the consistency and reliability of moral reasoning in LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ACM Survey Draft on Formalising Software Requirements with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14627v1)
- **Authors**: Arshad Beg, Diarmuid O'Donoghue, Rosemary Monahan
- **Abstract**: This draft is a working document, having a summary of nighty-four (94) papers with additional sections on Traceability of Software Requirements (Section 4), Formal Methods and Its Tools (Section 5), Unifying Theories of Programming (UTP) and Theory of Institutions (Section 6). Please refer to abstract of [7,8]. Key difference of this draft from our recently anticipated ones with similar titles, i.e. AACS 2025 [7] and SAIV 2025 [8] is: [7] is a two page submission to ADAPT Annual Conference, Ireland. Submitted on 18th of March, 2025, it went through the light-weight blind review and accepted for poster presentation. Conference was held on 15th of May, 2025. [8] is a nine page paper with additional nine pages of references and summary tables, submitted to Symposium on AI Verification (SAIV 2025) on 24th of April, 2025. It went through rigorous review process. The uploaded version on arXiv.org [8] is the improved one of the submission, after addressing the specific suggestions to improve the paper.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper draft.

**Summary**

The paper is a survey draft examining the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to formalize software requirements. The authors aim to bridge the gap between the need for formal verification techniques (often required in safety-critical software) and their limited adoption in the industry due to the time and expertise required for writing formal specifications. The draft surveys existing research on using LLMs to translate natural language requirements into formal notations, exploring methodologies, emerging trends, and future research directions. It also includes sections on software requirements traceability, formal methods, and unifying theories of programming.  The authors conduct a systematic literature review and present findings organized around two primary research questions: (1) methodologies leveraging LLMs for transforming natural language to formal notations and (2) emerging trends and future research directions. The survey also aims to improve the techniques that bridge the gap between informal natural language description and rigorous formal specifications, through refinement of prompt engineering, the incorporation of chain-of-thought reasoning and the development of hybrid neuro-symbolic approaches.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of surveying the use of LLMs for formalizing software requirements is moderately novel. While individual research papers explore specific LLM applications in this area, a comprehensive survey provides valuable context and highlights potential research gaps. The value increases with how critically the existing approaches are presented and when gaps are identified in the existing literature.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this survey hinges on its ability to synthesize existing work and identify meaningful directions for future research. Formalizing software requirements is a crucial step in ensuring software reliability and safety, particularly in critical systems. LLMs offer a potentially transformative approach to automating this process. If the survey effectively guides researchers toward promising and under-explored areas, it could have a significant impact.
    The strength of this paper will depend on the survey methodology, and the depth of analysis conducted in the paper. The paper aims to improve the techniques that bridge the gap between informal natural language description and rigorous formal specifications, through refinement of prompt engineering, the incorporation of chain-of-thought reasoning and the development of hybrid neuro-symbolic approaches.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Topic:** The survey addresses a timely and relevant topic, given the increasing capabilities of LLMs and the ongoing need for robust software verification methods.
    *   **Structured Approach:** The paper outlines a structured approach to the literature review, enhancing the credibility of its findings.
    *   **Clearly Defined Research Questions:** The paper sets out with the defined research questions.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper covers formal methods and chain-of-thought reasoning

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Preliminary Nature:** The paper is explicitly labeled as a "draft," suggesting that the literature review and analysis may not be fully complete. The full impact of this work is limited because of the draft label.
    *   **Lack of Critical Analysis:** The summary tables primarily *describe* the surveyed papers. A stronger contribution would include more *critical analysis* of the strengths, weaknesses, and limitations of each approach, and a synthesis of the findings across different papers. The "discussion" sections of the paper must critically analyse the gaps between the papers presented in the study.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** The paper acknowledges potential limitations of LLMs (ambiguity resolution, verification).
    *   **Accessibility:** The paper has a short length, so the authors must be wary to not simply produce a "list of papers" in the domain, without rigorous analysis and synthesis.

*   **Potential Impact:** The survey's potential impact depends on the thoroughness and depth of its analysis. A well-synthesized survey could help researchers identify the most promising LLM techniques for formal verification, leading to more efficient and reliable software development processes. The paper should act as a valuable reference for researchers and practitioners seeking to understand the current state-of-the-art and future directions in this rapidly evolving field.

*   **Justification:** The paper touches upon an exciting area of investigation. Given the paper is a survey draft and its significance is yet to be determined based on a final version with more thorough analysis, synthesis, and discussion of identified issues within LLMs' use for formalising software requirements. At the current stage, it seems to be moderately novel.
