### CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14769v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahua Ma, Yiran Qin, Yixiong Li, Xuanqi Liao, Yulan Guo, Ruimao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Policy (DP) enables robots to learn complex behaviors by imitating expert demonstrations through action diffusion. However, in practical applications, hardware limitations often degrade data quality, while real-time constraints restrict model inference to instantaneous state and scene observations. These limitations seriously reduce the efficacy of learning from expert demonstrations, resulting in failures in object localization, grasp planning, and long-horizon task execution. To address these challenges, we propose Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), a novel transformer-based diffusion model that enhances action prediction by conditioning on historical action sequences, thereby enabling more coherent and context-aware visuomotor policy learning. To further mitigate the computational cost associated with autoregressive inference, a caching mechanism is also introduced to store attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps, substantially reducing redundant computations during execution. Extensive experiments in both simulated and real-world environments, spanning diverse 2D and 3D manipulation tasks, demonstrate that CDP uniquely leverages historical action sequences to achieve significantly higher accuracy than existing methods. Moreover, even when faced with degraded input observation quality, CDP maintains remarkable precision by reasoning through temporal continuity, which highlights its practical robustness for robotic control under realistic, imperfect conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), a novel transformer-based diffusion model for robotic visuomotor policy learning. CDP enhances action prediction by conditioning on historical action sequences, creating a context-aware and temporally coherent policy.  The authors also introduce a caching mechanism to store attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps to reduce computational cost during autoregressive inference. Extensive experiments, both simulated and real-world, demonstrate CDP's improved accuracy and robustness compared to existing methods, especially under degraded observation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the combination of an autoregressive transformer architecture with a diffusion policy framework for robotic manipulation.  Specifically, incorporating historical action sequences into the conditioning process, along with the caching mechanism, presents a practical approach to improving policy robustness and efficiency. While individual components (diffusion policies, transformers, caching) are not new, their integration in this specific manner within the visuomotor control context offers a unique contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing critical limitations in existing diffusion policy methods for real-world robotic deployments. The approach effectively tackles issues related to noisy sensor data, partial observability, and real-time computational constraints. The ability to leverage temporal context makes CDP more robust to errors and improves the ability to perform long-horizon tasks. Demonstrating improved performance under realistic, imperfect conditions directly addresses a core challenge in deploying IL-based approaches in the real world.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The experimental results are thorough and convincing, with comprehensive evaluations in simulation and real-world environments spanning diverse manipulation tasks.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of both historical action conditioning and the caching mechanism.
    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing significant practical challenges in robotic policy learning.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the integration is novel, the paper does not delve deeply into theoretical analysis.  A more rigorous mathematical justification for the effectiveness of the approach could strengthen the work.
    *   The limitations section mentions the lack of exploration of extremely long-horizon tasks and efficient fusion of historical data; these points highlight potential directions for future work but also imply a current scope limitation.
    *   The baseline models are fairly standard, and the improvement is notable. However, a more direct comparison to state-of-the-art approaches in autoregressive action prediction could better showcase the competitive advantage.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work has the potential to influence research in robotic manipulation, specifically in improving the robustness and efficiency of diffusion-based policy learning. The insights gained on the importance of historical context and efficient inference methods can inform future development in the field. It provides a strong practical solution that bridges the gap between simulation and real-world robotic control. The results encourage the development of alternative approaches that similarly take into account temporal data in visuomotor tasks.

*   **Justification:** The paper addresses a crucial aspect of real-world robot learning by focusing on robustness to imperfect data, a common problem in real-world deployments. While many components of the approach are existing methods, the specific combination and its demonstrated effectiveness are significant. The inclusion of the caching mechanism for real-time application is an added contribution. The relatively short experimental setup of 50 real world demonstrations is also notable, and provides a glimpse into a practical solution that can be quickly implemented.

### DETONATE: A Benchmark for Text-to-Image Alignment and Kernelized Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14903v1)
- **Authors**: Renjith Prasad, Abhilekh Borah, Hasnat Md Abdullah, Chathurangi Shyalika, Gurpreet Singh, Ritvik Garimella, Rajarshi Roy, Harshul Surana, Nasrin Imanpour, Suranjana Trivedy, Amit Sheth, Amitava Das
- **Abstract**: Alignment is crucial for text-to-image (T2I) models to ensure that generated images faithfully capture user intent while maintaining safety and fairness. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), prominent in large language models (LLMs), is extending its influence to T2I systems. This paper introduces DPO-Kernels for T2I models, a novel extension enhancing alignment across three dimensions: (i) Hybrid Loss, integrating embedding-based objectives with traditional probability-based loss for improved optimization; (ii) Kernelized Representations, employing Radial Basis Function (RBF), Polynomial, and Wavelet kernels for richer feature transformations and better separation between safe and unsafe inputs; and (iii) Divergence Selection, expanding beyond DPO's default Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularizer by incorporating Wasserstein and R'enyi divergences for enhanced stability and robustness. We introduce DETONATE, the first large-scale benchmark of its kind, comprising approximately 100K curated image pairs categorized as chosen and rejected. DETONATE encapsulates three axes of social bias and discrimination: Race, Gender, and Disability. Prompts are sourced from hate speech datasets, with images generated by leading T2I models including Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large, Stable Diffusion XL, and Midjourney. Additionally, we propose the Alignment Quality Index (AQI), a novel geometric measure quantifying latent-space separability of safe/unsafe image activations, revealing hidden vulnerabilities. Empirically, we demonstrate that DPO-Kernels maintain strong generalization bounds via Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR). DETONATE and complete code are publicly released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper abstract and related information:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical issue of alignment in text-to-image (T2I) models, focusing on ensuring faithfulness to user intent while maintaining safety and fairness. It introduces "DPO-Kernels," an extension of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), that improves alignment across three dimensions: a hybrid loss combining embedding-based objectives, kernelized representations using RBF, Polynomial, and Wavelet kernels, and divergence selection beyond the default Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence.  The paper also introduces DETONATE, a large-scale benchmark dataset comprising ~100K curated image pairs categorized as chosen and rejected, representing social biases (Race, Gender, Disability), and the Alignment Quality Index (AQI), a geometric measure of latent space separability of safe and unsafe image activations. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization ability of DPO-Kernels and DETONATE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of several elements that, while individually perhaps not groundbreaking, collectively form a significant contribution. The kernelization of DPO is a novel approach for T2I alignment, going beyond scalar preference optimization. This provides a geometric understanding that scalar approaches previously failed to understand. The introduction of a diverse set of kernels provides a wider range of feature transformation options, and the use of alternate divergence measures shows an attempt to improve alignment stability and robustness.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and pressing issue in generative AI: the alignment of T2I models with ethical principles and user intent. Misalignment can lead to the generation of harmful, biased, or misleading content, posing significant societal risks. The DETONATE benchmark dataset is a valuable resource for the community, as it provides a structured and challenging evaluation platform for alignment techniques, focusing on sensitive social dimensions. The AQI offers an intriguing approach to quantify alignment based on latent space geometry, moving beyond simple surface-level evaluations. The results are promising and demonstrate the improvements achieved by DPO-Kernels.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Comprehensive approach that addresses both optimization, evaluation, and benchmarking.
    *   Well-designed benchmark dataset (DETONATE) covering critical social biases.
    *   The use of various kernels and divergence measures with a sound theoretical justification.
    *   Introduction of a novel geometric evaluation metric (AQI).
    * The method goes beyond traditional alignment fixes by incorporating geometry-aware information.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The computational overhead is a significant drawback, potentially limiting its widespread adoption.
    *   While the kernels and divergence measures are well justified, more comprehensive experimentation showing when one kernel is best suited might be beneficial.
    *   The reliance on PCA and t-SNE for dimensionality reduction in AQI, while standard, is a potential point of information loss and can influence results.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The DETONATE benchmark is likely to become a standard for evaluating alignment techniques in T2I models, fostering more responsible development.
    *   The DPO-Kernels approach provides a promising direction for research on alignment, motivating further exploration of kernel methods and other geometric regularization techniques.
    *   The AQI could inspire the development of more sophisticated latent-space diagnostic tools for understanding and improving model behavior.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of responsible AI and generative modeling. While the individual components may not be entirely revolutionary, their combination is innovative and well-motivated. The work tackles a crucial problem, offers practical solutions, and provides valuable resources for the community. The weaknesses related to computational cost and the lack of more extensive experimentation are valid concerns, but the overall impact and novelty warrant a good score.

### CrEst: Credibility Estimation for Contexts in LLMs via Weak Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14912v1)
- **Authors**: Dyah Adila, Shuai Zhang, Boran Han, Bonan Min, Yuyang Wang
- **Abstract**: The integration of contextual information has significantly enhanced the performance of large language models (LLMs) on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, existing methods often overlook a critical challenge: the credibility of context documents can vary widely, potentially leading to the propagation of unreliable information. In this paper, we introduce CrEst, a novel weakly supervised framework for assessing the credibility of context documents during LLM inference--without requiring manual annotations. Our approach is grounded in the insight that credible documents tend to exhibit higher semantic coherence with other credible documents, enabling automated credibility estimation through inter-document agreement. To incorporate credibility into LLM inference, we propose two integration strategies: a black-box approach for models without access to internal weights or activations, and a white-box method that directly modifies attention mechanisms. Extensive experiments across three model architectures and five datasets demonstrate that CrEst consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving up to a 26.86% improvement in accuracy and a 3.49% increase in F1 score. Further analysis shows that CrEst maintains robust performance even under high-noise conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CrEst, a weakly supervised framework for estimating the credibility of context documents used in retrieval-augmented language models (LLMs).  CrEst leverages the insight that credible documents tend to exhibit higher semantic coherence with other credible documents.  It computes credibility scores without manual annotation by measuring the semantic similarity between documents in the latent space. The approach offers two integration strategies: a black-box method suitable for models with limited access and a white-box method for models with transparent internals, directly manipulating the attention mechanism. The authors demonstrate CrEst's effectiveness across multiple models and datasets, showing performance gains even under noisy conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging semantic coherence for assessing document credibility within the context of LLM inference is relatively novel. While weak supervision has been applied in other areas, its application to credibility estimation in RAG, particularly without relying on model-generated signals or manual labels, contributes a unique perspective.  The two integration strategies (black-box and white-box) offer practical solutions for different types of LLMs. However, the novelty is somewhat tempered by existing work on re-ranking and relevance assessment, which share similar objectives.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem: the impact of untrustworthy context on LLM performance. By effectively identifying and mitigating the influence of non-credible documents, CrEst has the potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM-generated responses in knowledge-intensive tasks. The demonstrated improvements across multiple datasets and model architectures highlight its generalizability.  The ability to maintain performance under noisy conditions further enhances its value. The fact that it doesn't require manual annotation is a significant advantage, making it more scalable and adaptable.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the CrEst framework.
    * The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering multiple models, datasets, and baseline methods.
    * The ablation studies provide insights into the impact of key parameters, such as the number of embedders.
    * The analysis of noise robustness is particularly valuable, demonstrating CrEst's resilience to corrupted contexts.
    * The absence of manual annotations enhances the applicability of the technique.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The assumption that credible documents are semantically similar can be limiting, particularly if a majority of documents are unreliable. The paper acknowledges this limitation but could benefit from more detailed analysis of failure cases under these circumstances.  The experiments are limited to a handful of datasets; broader validation across different domains would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
    * The complexity of the framework (weak supervision, embedding spaces, integration strategies) may make it difficult for practitioners to implement without significant expertise.
    * While the black-box approach avoids modifying internal model parameters, it is not entirely practical due to repeated prompting which significantly increases API costs.
    * The paper does not explicitly consider computationally complex or memory-intensive methods for scaling the method to accommodate larger datasets or document collections.

**Justification for the Score:**

CrEst provides a useful and innovative contribution to the field of retrieval-augmented language models.  It successfully addresses the issue of context credibility through a practical, scalable, and well-evaluated framework. While it builds on existing concepts like weak supervision and semantic similarity, its specific application to credibility estimation and the proposed integration strategies offer a valuable advancement. The limitations, such as reliance on dataset quality and implementation complexity, temper the overall impact. The paper is well-written and contributes significantly to the literature.

### Winter Soldier: Backdooring Language Models at Pre-Training with Indirect Data Poisoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14913v1)
- **Authors**: Wassim Bouaziz, Mathurin Videau, Nicolas Usunier, El-Mahdi El-Mhamdi
- **Abstract**: The pre-training of large language models (LLMs) relies on massive text datasets sourced from diverse and difficult-to-curate origins. Although membership inference attacks and hidden canaries have been explored to trace data usage, such methods rely on memorization of training data, which LM providers try to limit. In this work, we demonstrate that indirect data poisoning (where the targeted behavior is absent from training data) is not only feasible but also allow to effectively protect a dataset and trace its use. Using gradient-based optimization prompt-tuning, we make a model learn arbitrary secret sequences: secret responses to secret prompts that are absent from the training corpus. We validate our approach on language models pre-trained from scratch and show that less than 0.005% of poisoned tokens are sufficient to covertly make a LM learn a secret and detect it with extremely high confidence ($p < 10^{-55}$) with a theoretically certifiable scheme. Crucially, this occurs without performance degradation (on LM benchmarks) and despite secrets never appearing in the training set.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Winter Soldier: Backdooring Language Models at Pre-Training with Indirect Data Poisoning" explores a method for dataset ownership verification (DOV) in large language models (LLMs).  The authors introduce a technique called "indirect data poisoning" where the training data is subtly tampered with to make the model learn a secret behavior *without* explicitly including the trigger-response pair in the training set. This involves using gradient-based optimization to craft poisoned samples that, when included in pre-training, cause the model to generate a specific secret response when prompted with a specific secret prompt. The authors demonstrate that even a small amount of poisoned data (less than 0.005%) can be sufficient to effectively implant a secret behavior, detectable with high confidence, and without significantly impacting the model's performance on standard benchmarks. The method relies on only top-l predictions, which enables applicability to black-box models. The paper also extends theoretical guarantees from image data poisoning to text, offering a means to certify the false detection rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of indirect data poisoning is not entirely new, as it builds on existing work in image data poisoning. However, the application of this technique to *text* modalities, specifically in the context of LLM pre-training and DOV, is a significant contribution.  The paper adapts gradient-based prompt-tuning techniques to make the poisoning samples work, which requires overcoming challenges related to discrete nature of tokens. Crucially, the focus on *indirect* poisoning to avoid memorization is a clever twist that circumvents limitations of previous DOV methods that rely on memorization. Another important aspect is that method only uses the top-l predictions to detect membership, which opens the door for DOV on closed source model.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM development: ensuring dataset integrity and preventing unauthorized use.  The ability to reliably detect whether a model has been trained on a specific dataset, even when attempts have been made to obscure this fact through techniques like data deduplication, is valuable. The fact that the approach doesn't rely on explicit memorization of the data nor accessing the model's logits makes it significantly more practical and harder to circumvent than prior art. The method offers theoretical guarantees for controlling the false detection rate. The study is well-executed on diverse models (135M to 1.4B parameters) and contamination ratios. The analysis of the method's transferability between model sizes and ablations on poison parameters are valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Practical approach that works even with limited access to the model (top-l predictions).
    *   High detection accuracy with low contamination ratios.
    *   Theoretical guarantees for false detection rate.
    *   Demonstrated preservation of model performance on standard benchmarks.
    *   Circumvents reliance on memorization, making it more robust against mitigation strategies.
    *   Experiments covering transferability across models, ablation on parameters and defense mechanisms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The assumption that Alice knows Bob's model architecture and tokenizer is a limitation. While many LLMs use public tokenizers and Transformer architectures are well-known, this assumption could be restrictive in scenarios involving highly proprietary models.
    *   The compute-intensive nature of crafting the poisoned samples is a practical challenge. This could limit the applicability of the approach for protecting very large datasets or by actors with limited computational resources.
    *   The stealthiness of the crafted poisons could be improved. The current implementation is relatively easy to detect and filter using basic quality classifiers, which calls for more research in evading existing filter strategies.
    *   The method necessitates poisoning datasets *before* sharing, raising concerns about already published data.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM security and DOV. It provides a new and practical approach for detecting data usage that is robust against common mitigation strategies. It is likely to spur further research into more robust and stealthier data poisoning techniques, as well as into defenses against such attacks. The availability of a theoretically certifiable DOV is also a valuable contribution. It will likely spark interest in improving the stealthiness of the approach, as well as adapting defense to existing filtering strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the field of LLM security by introducing a practical and effective method for dataset ownership verification. While the core idea of indirect data poisoning is not entirely new, its adaptation to the text domain, specifically within the context of LLMs and with a focus on evading memorization-based defenses, is a notable advancement. The paper demonstrates high detection accuracy, theoretical guarantees, and preserved model performance, highlighting its practical value. The primary limitations are the assumptions around the model and tokenizer architectures, the compute-intensive nature of poison crafting, and the current level of stealthiness, which can be improved with more sophisticated poisoning techniques. However, these limitations do not diminish the significance of the paper's contribution, which is likely to stimulate further research and development in LLM security.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Frequency-Calibrated Membership Inference Attacks on Medical Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14919v1)
- **Authors**: Xinkai Zhao, Yuta Tokuoka, Junichiro Iwasawa, Keita Oda
- **Abstract**: The increasing use of diffusion models for image generation, especially in sensitive areas like medical imaging, has raised significant privacy concerns. Membership Inference Attack (MIA) has emerged as a potential approach to determine if a specific image was used to train a diffusion model, thus quantifying privacy risks. Existing MIA methods often rely on diffusion reconstruction errors, where member images are expected to have lower reconstruction errors than non-member images. However, applying these methods directly to medical images faces challenges. Reconstruction error is influenced by inherent image difficulty, and diffusion models struggle with high-frequency detail reconstruction. To address these issues, we propose a Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE) method for MIAs on medical image diffusion models. By focusing on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range and excluding both high-frequency (difficult to reconstruct) and low-frequency (less informative) regions, our frequency-selective approach mitigates the confounding factor of inherent image difficulty. Specifically, we analyze the reverse diffusion process, obtain the mid-frequency reconstruction error, and compute the structural similarity index score between the reconstructed and original images. Membership is determined by comparing this score to a threshold. Experiments on several medical image datasets demonstrate that our FCRE method outperforms existing MIA methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Frequency-Calibrated Membership Inference Attacks on Medical Image Diffusion Models" addresses the privacy risks associated with using diffusion models for medical image generation. The authors highlight that existing Membership Inference Attack (MIA) methods, which try to determine if a specific image was used to train a model, often fail on medical images due to their specific frequency characteristics (high-frequency details and low-frequency background). To overcome this, they propose a Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE) method. FCRE focuses on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range, excluding high and low-frequency regions. This approach mitigates the influence of inherent image difficulty, making it more reliable for determining membership. The paper demonstrates that FCRE outperforms existing MIA methods on several medical image datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application of difficulty calibration, particularly frequency-based calibration, to Membership Inference Attacks specifically targeting medical image diffusion models. While difficulty calibration itself isn't entirely new, its adaptation to the specific challenges presented by medical image frequency characteristics and diffusion models is a significant contribution. The idea to focus on the mid-frequency range to improve the attack's accuracy is insightful. While other works have used MIAs on diffusion models, they did not focus on calibrating the attack based on frequency. The specific implementation of FCRE, including the use of Laplacian scores and the combination of SSIM and L2 loss, adds further novelty.
*   **Significance:** The increasing use of diffusion models in medical imaging, combined with the sensitive nature of medical data, makes the privacy concerns very real. Identifying and quantifying these risks is crucial. Therefore, improving MIA methods for medical images is a relevant and important task. The paper's findings suggest that diffusion models can inadvertently leak training data information, underscoring the need for privacy-preserving techniques. The improvement over existing MIA methods, demonstrated through experiments on multiple medical datasets, further enhances the paper's significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Framing:** Clearly identifies a crucial privacy issue in medical image generation.
    *   **Technical Approach:** FCRE addresses the limitations of existing methods with a well-motivated frequency-based calibration technique.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on multiple datasets and include comparisons to relevant baselines.
    *   **Results:** The experimental results consistently demonstrate the superiority of FCRE over the baselines on medical image datasets.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Provides insight into the importance of frequency range selection.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Threshold Selection:** The paper acknowledges that the optimal frequency thresholds (Lmin and Lmax) vary across datasets and that the optimal values were not exhaustively searched. Adaptive methods for threshold selection could improve the method's generalizability.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses solely on one particular type of difficulty calibration (frequency-based). Exploring other forms of calibration (e.g., patch complexity) may potentially yield further improvements.
    *   **Natural Image Performance:** While the method provides good results on medical imaging datasets, results are less compelling in the natural image dataset.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to influence research in two primary areas: privacy in medical image generation and MIA methods for generative models. It provides a strong baseline for future research on improving privacy risks for medical imaging generation.

**Overall Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **7.5**.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and relevant adaptation of existing MIA methods to the specific challenges posed by medical image diffusion models. The FCRE method is well-motivated and demonstrates clear improvements over existing methods. The experimental results provide strong evidence to support the claims. However, the method's dependence on manual threshold selection and limited scope in terms of difficulty calibration prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the limited scope of the study is acknowledged in the text, exploring the performance of the method on different models and architecture would further increase confidence in the method's generalisability. If the authors investigated methods to better generalise to different image types and frequency characteristics (potentially through incorporating a learning step to threshold selections) then the score could easily rise to an 8 or higher. In summary, the contributions of the paper are novel and significant, although there are some aspects that could be improved in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### FORTRESS: Frontier Risk Evaluation for National Security and Public Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14922v1)
- **Authors**: Christina Q. Knight, Kaustubh Deshpande, Ved Sirdeshmukh, Meher Mankikar, Scale Red Team, SEAL Research Team, Julian Michael
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) introduces dual-use capabilities that could both threaten and bolster national security and public safety (NSPS). Models implement safeguards to protect against potential misuse relevant to NSPS and allow for benign users to receive helpful information. However, current benchmarks often fail to test safeguard robustness to potential NSPS risks in an objective, robust way. We introduce FORTRESS: 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts with instance-based rubrics of 4-7 binary questions for automated evaluation across 3 domains (unclassified information only): Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear and Explosive (CBRNE), Political Violence & Terrorism, and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities, with 10 total subcategories across these domains. Each prompt-rubric pair has a corresponding benign version to test for model over-refusals. This evaluation of frontier LLMs' safeguard robustness reveals varying trade-offs between potential risks and model usefulness: Claude-3.5-Sonnet demonstrates a low average risk score (ARS) (14.09 out of 100) but the highest over-refusal score (ORS) (21.8 out of 100), while Gemini 2.5 Pro shows low over-refusal (1.4) but a high average potential risk (66.29). Deepseek-R1 has the highest ARS at 78.05, but the lowest ORS at only 0.06. Models such as o1 display a more even trade-off between potential risks and over-refusals (with an ARS of 21.69 and ORS of 5.2). To provide policymakers and researchers with a clear understanding of models' potential risks, we publicly release FORTRESS at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ScaleAI/fortress_public. We also maintain a private set for evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FORTRESS, a new dataset and benchmark for evaluating the robustness of large language model (LLM) safeguards against misuse related to national security and public safety (NSPS).  The benchmark includes 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts across three domains: Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear and Explosive (CBRNE); Political Violence & Terrorism; and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities. Each prompt is paired with a benign counterpart to measure over-refusal rates.  The paper evaluates 26 state-of-the-art LLMs using FORTRESS, revealing trade-offs between potential NSPS risks and model usefulness. It includes instance-specific rubrics for each prompt, allowing for automated evaluation with LLM judges.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:**  The paper fills a significant gap in existing benchmarks by specifically targeting NSPS-related risks.  While general safety benchmarks exist, focused, rigorous, and *automated* NSPS evaluations are lacking.  The introduction of instance-specific rubrics combined with LLM judges is a good approach to scalability and precision compared to solely relying on manual human evaluation.
    *   **Relevance:** The paper addresses a critical and timely concern given the increasing capabilities of LLMs and potential for misuse. The choice of domains (CBRNE, Terrorism, Financial Crimes) are very relevant.
    *   **Methodology:** The detailed methodology is well-explained, including prompt generation, adversarial tactics, and the development of instance-specific rubrics. The use of a panel of LLM judges enhances the reliability and reduces bias in the evaluation. The inclusion of benign prompts to measure over-refusal is also a good design choice, allowing for an evaluation of the utility and safety trade-off.
    *   **Openness:** The public release of the FORTRESS dataset (albeit a smaller, public version) is a significant contribution to the research community, enabling further investigation and development of improved safeguards.
    *   **Analysis:** The comparative analysis of different LLMs and the breakdown of vulnerabilities by subdomain and adversarial tactic provide valuable insights for model developers and policymakers.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope Limitations:** While focused, the benchmark is still limited to static, single-turn prompts.  Real-world misuse often involves more complex interactions. This should have more acknowledgement in the introduction.
    *   **Model Dependence in Evaluation:** While the authors use a panel of LLM judges and compare to human evaluation, the reliance on LLMs to *judge* LLMs means there's still dependence on the alignment and biases of those judging LLMs. The human evaluation, while consistent, isn't particularly extensive. The effectiveness of the human experts seems limited.
    *   **Lack of Ground Truth Data:** While the paper uses expert-crafted rubrics, there is a potential bias in using LLMs as judges without a very extensive set of verified ground truth data. The reliance on "experienced red teamers" as human judges means a smaller, more expert panel. This limits scalability in the long run.
    *   **Limited Private Set Details:** While the authors mention maintaining a private set, there aren't specific discussions of how that set *differs* from the public one, what unique types of prompts/attacks are there, or the general plan for its maintenance.
    *   **Minor point on terminology:** The paper could perhaps be clearer in how "adversarial prompt" is intended vs. "jailbreak". These aren't *necessarily* the same, but it's possible a prompt is adversarial and yet the model refuses *appropriately*.
*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   The FORTRESS benchmark has the potential to become a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing the NSPS-related safety of LLMs.
    *   The findings can inform the development of more robust safeguards and policies to mitigate the risks associated with LLM misuse.
    *   The dataset can serve as a resource for researchers working on adversarial robustness, safety alignment, and interpretability.
    *   The instance-specific rubric approach combined with automated evaluation could be adopted in other domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution by addressing a crucial gap in LLM safety evaluation. The NSPS focus is novel, the methodology sound, and the release of the dataset promotes further research. However, limitations in scope (single-turn prompts), the reliance on LLM judges, the smaller number of human evaluations, and the dependence on "experienced red teamers" limit the overall scope and robustness of the evaluation. Despite these weaknesses, the paper represents a strong step forward in NSPS-related LLM evaluations.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vision Transformers for End-to-End Quark-Gluon Jet Classification from Calorimeter Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14934v1)
- **Authors**: Md Abrar Jahin, Shahriar Soudeep, Arian Rahman Aditta, M. F. Mridha, Nafiz Fahad, Md. Jakir Hossen
- **Abstract**: Distinguishing between quark- and gluon-initiated jets is a critical and challenging task in high-energy physics, pivotal for improving new physics searches and precision measurements at the Large Hadron Collider. While deep learning, particularly Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), has advanced jet tagging using image-based representations, the potential of Vision Transformer (ViT) architectures, renowned for modeling global contextual information, remains largely underexplored for direct calorimeter image analysis, especially under realistic detector and pileup conditions. This paper presents a systematic evaluation of ViTs and ViT-CNN hybrid models for quark-gluon jet classification using simulated 2012 CMS Open Data. We construct multi-channel jet-view images from detector-level energy deposits (ECAL, HCAL) and reconstructed tracks, enabling an end-to-end learning approach. Our comprehensive benchmarking demonstrates that ViT-based models, notably ViT+MaxViT and ViT+ConvNeXt hybrids, consistently outperform established CNN baselines in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy, highlighting the advantage of capturing long-range spatial correlations within jet substructure. This work establishes the first systematic framework and robust performance baselines for applying ViT architectures to calorimeter image-based jet classification using public collider data, alongside a structured dataset suitable for further deep learning research in this domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Vision Transformers (ViTs) and ViT-CNN hybrid architectures for quark-gluon jet classification using calorimeter images derived from the CMS Open Data.  The authors create multi-channel "jet-view" images from detector-level energy deposits (ECAL, HCAL) and reconstructed tracks, allowing for end-to-end learning.  They benchmark ViT-based models against CNN baselines, finding that the ViTs, especially the hybrid models (ViT+MaxViT, ViT+ConvNeXt), consistently outperform CNNs in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy. The paper argues that this improved performance stems from the ViTs' ability to capture long-range spatial correlations in jet substructure. The authors also conduct a thorough ablation study to analyze the impact of various hyperparameters. They make their code and dataset publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While ViTs have been applied in high-energy physics before, their direct application to calorimeter *image* data for jet classification, especially with realistic detector conditions (using CMS Open Data) is a valuable contribution.  Existing work primarily focuses on particle cloud representations. The idea of systematically benchmarking these models for jet classification using such data is relatively new. The exploration of ViT-CNN hybrid architectures adds another layer of novelty. However, the core techniques used within the ViT architectures themselves (self-attention, transformer blocks, etc.) are well-established.

*   **Significance:** Quark-gluon jet discrimination is a crucial task for improving searches for new physics at the LHC. The paper's results demonstrate that ViTs offer a promising alternative to traditional CNN approaches for this task. The availability of the code and structured dataset will facilitate further research in this area and provide baselines for comparison. The identification of ViT+MaxViT and ViT+ConvNeXt as top performers is also important. The sensitivity analysis offers practical guidance for future researchers. The use of public CMS data allows for wider impact and greater accessibility for the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper includes a thorough comparison of multiple ViT architectures, CNNs, and hybrid models.
    *   **Realistic Data:**  The use of CMS Open Data provides a realistic evaluation environment, including detector effects and pileup.
    *   **End-to-End Learning:**  The focus on direct image input avoids the need for complex manual feature engineering.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The hyperparameter sensitivity analysis is valuable for practical applications.
    *   **Code and Data Availability:** This promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Exploration:** While the CMS Open Data is a good starting point, it is a simulated dataset from 2012. Testing on more recent real data would significantly strengthen the conclusions. The lack of explanation for *why* the ViTs are better from a physics perspective is also a weakness.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The paper notes the high computational cost of ViTs, which could limit their adoption in real-time applications without further optimization.
    *   **Lack of In-depth Physics Interpretation:** The paper primarily focuses on model performance metrics without delving deeply into the physical interpretation of the learned features. The extent to which the models learn meaningful physics, rather than just statistical correlations, remains unclear.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to encourage further research on applying ViTs to calorimeter image analysis and other HEP problems. The provided dataset and code will be valuable resources for the community. The performance benchmarks established by this work will serve as a reference point for future studies.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of 7/10. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by systematically evaluating ViTs for calorimeter image-based jet classification using realistic CMS Open Data, creating a dataset, and identifying promising hybrid architectures. This constitutes a clear advance over prior work focused primarily on particle clouds or lacking extensive benchmarking on publicly accessible collider datasets. However, the dependence on a relatively old simulated dataset, the lack of in-depth physics interpretation, and the significant computational demands limit the paper's impact. While the novelty within the ViT architecture is limited, the novel *application* is solid and the findings will likely guide future research. Furthermore, the code and data availability significantly boost the contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Structured Moral Reasoning in Language Models: A Value-Grounded Evaluation Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14948v1)
- **Authors**: Mohna Chakraborty, Lu Wang, David Jurgens
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in domains requiring moral understanding, yet their reasoning often remains shallow, and misaligned with human reasoning. Unlike humans, whose moral reasoning integrates contextual trade-offs, value systems, and ethical theories, LLMs often rely on surface patterns, leading to biased decisions in morally and ethically complex scenarios. To address this gap, we present a value-grounded framework for evaluating and distilling structured moral reasoning in LLMs. We benchmark 12 open-source models across four moral datasets using a taxonomy of prompts grounded in value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies. Our evaluation is guided by four questions: (1) Does reasoning improve LLM decision-making over direct prompting? (2) Which types of value/ethical frameworks most effectively guide LLM reasoning? (3) Which cognitive reasoning strategies lead to better moral performance? (4) Can small-sized LLMs acquire moral competence through distillation? We find that prompting with explicit moral structure consistently improves accuracy and coherence, with first-principles reasoning and Schwartz's + care-ethics scaffolds yielding the strongest gains. Furthermore, our supervised distillation approach transfers moral competence from large to small models without additional inference cost. Together, our results offer a scalable path toward interpretable and value-grounded models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

This paper addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in moral reasoning. It argues that LLMs often exhibit shallow or biased moral judgements due to their reliance on surface-level patterns rather than deeper value systems and ethical theories. To overcome this, the authors introduce a value-grounded framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs.  The framework uses prompts structured around value systems (e.g., Schwartz's Value Theory, Moral Foundations Theory), ethical theories (e.g., utilitarianism, care ethics, deontology), and cognitive reasoning strategies (e.g., first-principles reasoning, stakeholder analysis). The paper benchmarks several open-source LLMs across various moral datasets, finding that such structured moral prompting improves accuracy and coherence, and that moral competence can be transferred from large to small models via reasoning-based distillation.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:* The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic approach to structuring moral reasoning prompts for LLMs. While previous work has explored prompting techniques and moral datasets, this paper distinguishes itself by:

*   **Unified Taxonomy:** Creating a comprehensive taxonomy that integrates established value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies into the prompt design. This is a structured attempt to mirror how humans approach complex moral decisions, and it is much more involved than a simple Chain-of-Thought prompt.
*   **Distillation of Moral Reasoning:**  The proposed reasoning-based distillation approach is new in the context of moral reasoning. Distillation has been applied to other tasks, but applying it specifically to transfer structured moral reasoning to smaller models is a significant contribution.

*Significance:* The significance of this work is considerable, given the increasing deployment of LLMs in areas with ethical implications (e.g., content moderation, education). By improving the quality, interpretability, and consistency of moral reasoning in LLMs, the paper addresses a crucial challenge. The key significance elements are:

*   **Improved Performance:** Demonstrating that structured prompting leads to significant improvements in accuracy and coherence across different LLMs and datasets.
*   **Scalability:** Showing that moral reasoning skills can be transferred to smaller models via distillation, which promotes broader accessibility.
*   **Normative Alignment:** The framework provides a way to shape the normative alignment of LLMs, making their judgements more culturally sensitive and less prone to bias.

*Strengths:*

*   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper provides a well-defined and rigorous framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:**  The empirical evaluation is thorough, covering a range of LLMs, datasets, and prompting strategies.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-articulated, and the experiments are designed to directly address them.
*   **Case Studies:** The inclusion of illustrative case studies adds further depth and insight.
*   **Addressing a Critical Issue:** The research tackles a timely and important issue, as LLMs are increasingly used in morally sensitive applications.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Dataset limitations:** The reliance on curated datasets, while necessary for controlled evaluation, may not fully capture the complexity and nuance of real-world moral dilemmas. Datasets can still be biased even if created with attention. It would be crucial to evaluate the model outputs on diverse scenarios in many different languages to validate.
*   **Overfitting risk:** There's potential risk of overfitting to the specific moral frameworks used in the prompts. The models might become overly reliant on these pre-defined values at the expense of context-sensitive considerations. The authors acknowledge that the moral frameworks that they use are not necessarily exhaustive. More details as to why the specific frameworks were chosen should be expanded on.
*   **Value Choice is Still Subjective:** Choosing which ethical frameworks to implement itself poses subjective and potentially biased considerations. The study does not offer a way to automate value selection.
*   **Computational Cost:** While the distillation approach helps with scalability, training the initial large teacher models is still computationally expensive.

*Potential Influence:*

*   **Guidance for Developers:** This research can serve as a practical guide for developers building LLMs for ethically sensitive applications.
*   **Further Research:** The paper opens avenues for future research in areas like automated value selection, development of more robust moral reasoning datasets, and exploration of alternative distillation techniques.
*   **Improving Interpretability:** The use of structured prompts promotes more transparent and interpretable decision-making in LLMs, which is crucial for accountability.

*Justification for Score:*

Despite the identified limitations, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field. The systematic framework, thorough empirical evaluation, and demonstration of reasoning-based distillation offer a valuable step toward building more ethically aligned and interpretable language models. The novelty of the approach and its potential impact on LLM development in ethically sensitive domains justify a high score.
