- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### From Chat to Checkup: Can Large Language Models Assist in Diabetes Prediction?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14949v1)
- **Authors**: Shadman Sakib, Oishy Fatema Akhand, Ajwad Abrar
- **Abstract**: While Machine Learning (ML) and Deep Learning (DL) models have been widely used for diabetes prediction, the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for structured numerical data is still not well explored. In this study, we test the effectiveness of LLMs in predicting diabetes using zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting methods. We conduct an empirical analysis using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD). We evaluate six LLMs, including four open-source models: Gemma-2-27B, Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Llama-3.2-2B. We also test two proprietary models: GPT-4o and Gemini Flash 2.0. In addition, we compare their performance with three traditional machine learning models: Random Forest, Logistic Regression, and Support Vector Machine (SVM). We use accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score as evaluation metrics. Our results show that proprietary LLMs perform better than open-source ones, with GPT-4o and Gemma-2-27B achieving the highest accuracy in few-shot settings. Notably, Gemma-2-27B also outperforms the traditional ML models in terms of F1-score. However, there are still issues such as performance variation across prompting strategies and the need for domain-specific fine-tuning. This study shows that LLMs can be useful for medical prediction tasks and encourages future work on prompt engineering and hybrid approaches to improve healthcare predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for diabetes prediction using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD). It compares the performance of several LLMs (both open-source and proprietary) with traditional machine learning models (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, SVM) under zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting strategies.  The study evaluates performance using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score. The results indicate that while proprietary LLMs like GPT-40 and Gemma-2-27B perform reasonably well, particularly with few-shot prompting, they generally don't surpass the accuracy of traditional ML models. The paper highlights the promise of LLMs in low-data scenarios but also points out their limitations in terms of resource demands, prompting sensitivity, and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is moderate. While ML and DL models for diabetes prediction are well-established, the exploration of LLMs *without fine-tuning* on structured medical data is relatively new. However, simply applying standard prompting techniques (zero-shot, one-shot, few-shot) to a well-known dataset does not represent a groundbreaking advance. The prompts used appear basic, lacking advanced prompt engineering techniques that could significantly improve performance.

*   **Significance:** The significance is somewhat limited by the modest performance gains.  The paper demonstrates that LLMs can function reasonably well in a zero/few-shot setting, hinting at their potential for scenarios with limited labeled data.  However, since the LLMs don't outperform traditional methods on this dataset (and the performance of Gemini Flash 2.0 in particular, seems problematic), the immediate practical impact is low. The potential is that this work *motivates* further research, especially focused on improving prompting strategies and architectures suitable for these tasks and more complex medical datasets. However, more detailed analysis would be needed of both errors made and a wider range of models compared.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper performs a comparative analysis of several LLMs against established ML baselines, providing a benchmark for future research.
    *   The use of multiple prompting strategies offers insights into the sensitivity of LLMs to context.
    *   The paper correctly identifies the limitations of current LLMs, such as the need for domain-specific fine-tuning, resource intensiveness, and lack of interpretability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The prompting strategies employed are rather basic.  More sophisticated prompt engineering (e.g., chain-of-thought prompting, more elaborate instructions, utilizing patient history, etc.) could potentially yield better results.
    *   The model selection lacks a rationale beyond readily available open source and cloud-based models. It would be helpful to discuss why these architectures were chosen.
    *   The dataset is relatively simple and well-studied. The dataset is imbalanced which may influence results with metrics that dont account for this (precision, recall).
    *   The paper lacks error analysis. Understanding *what* kind of predictions the LLMs get wrong could be valuable.
    *   The paper does not address ethical implications of using LLMs in medical diagnosis.

*   **Influence on the Field:** The paper's influence will likely be moderate. It serves as a preliminary investigation that could inspire further research into using LLMs for medical prediction, particularly focusing on prompt engineering, fine-tuning, and hybrid approaches. If future work can overcome the limitations identified in this paper, this study could be seen as an early stepping stone. The reliance on a well-known and relatively simple data set with standard methods is a hinderance. The claims of improvement and suggestions of new methods are not significant enough to be truly impactful at this time.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty is modest, and while it demonstrates some potential for LLMs in diabetes prediction without training, the limited performance gains and basic methodology constrain its significance. Its strengths lie in its comparative analysis and identification of limitations, but the lack of advanced prompting techniques, more complex medical applications and lack of ethical considerations weaken its potential influence. It is valuable exploratory work that could spur more impactful future research, but in its current form, it does not make a substantial contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Thinking in Directivity: Speech Large Language Model for Multi-Talker Directional Speech Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14973v1)
- **Authors**: Jiamin Xie, Ju Lin, Yiteng Huang, Tyler Vuong, Zhaojiang Lin, Zhaojun Yang, Peng Su, Prashant Rawat, Sangeeta Srivastava, Ming Sun, Florian Metze
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated that prompting large language models (LLM) with audio encodings enables effective speech recognition capabilities. However, the ability of Speech LLMs to comprehend and process multi-channel audio with spatial cues remains a relatively uninvestigated area of research. In this work, we present directional-SpeechLlama, a novel approach that leverages the microphone array of smart glasses to achieve directional speech recognition, source localization, and bystander cross-talk suppression. To enhance the model's ability to understand directivity, we propose two key techniques: serialized directional output training (S-DOT) and contrastive direction data augmentation (CDDA). Experimental results show that our proposed directional-SpeechLlama effectively captures the relationship between textual cues and spatial audio, yielding strong performance in both speech recognition and source localization tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Directional-SpeechLLaMA, a speech large language model designed to process multi-channel audio from microphone arrays (specifically, smart glasses) for directional speech recognition.  The key contributions are two techniques to enhance directional awareness: Serialized Directional Output Training (S-DOT) and Contrastive Direction Data Augmentation (CDDA).  The model is trained in a multi-stage framework and evaluated on tasks like multi-talker target-direction ASR, single-talker far-field ASR, and source localization. Experimental results suggest that the proposed methods effectively capture the relationship between textual cues and spatial audio, leading to improved performance in both speech recognition and source localization tasks, even with overlapping speech and unseen acoustic conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the specific adaptation of SpeechLLaMA to multi-channel directional audio, especially for the smart glasses scenario.  While SpeechLLMs and spatial audio processing are established areas, the combination and the proposed S-DOT and CDDA techniques demonstrate a non-trivial advancement. The idea of serializing directional output and contrastive direction augmentation is relatively novel within the context of speech LLMs. The smart glasses application is relevant and practical. However, some of the individual components (beamforming, adapter layers, contrastive loss) are well-established techniques; the innovation is in their specific configuration and application to the SLLM framework. Prior work exists on SLLMs for spatial audio, so the idea isn't completely groundbreaking.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the work comes from its potential to improve speech recognition in real-world scenarios where multi-channel audio and directional information are available, like smart glasses. Successfully addressing multi-talker scenarios with spatial awareness is a valuable contribution.  The paper demonstrates a plausible path towards more robust and context-aware speech recognition systems. The performance boost achieved by using S-DOT and CDDA compared to the MC-SpeechLLaMA shows the effectiveness of the proposed method.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper addresses a relevant problem with a practical application.
    * The proposed techniques (S-DOT and CDDA) are well-motivated and show measurable improvements.
    * The multi-stage training approach is sound and likely contributes to the overall performance.
    * The experiments are relatively thorough, covering different tasks, unseen directions, and acoustic conditions.
    * The paper is clearly written and well-organized.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the techniques are novel in their application, the underlying components are standard, potentially diminishing the overall impact.
    * The evaluation focuses heavily on simulated data.  Real-world performance in the smart glasses context could be significantly different due to more complex acoustic environments and hardware limitations.
    * The performance on overlapping speech remains a challenge, suggesting that further improvements are needed in this area.
    * The comparison against the Whisper baseline, while useful, might not be the most competitive benchmark for single-channel ASR. Comparing against other state-of-the-art methods within the multi-channel ASR field may strengthen the evaluation.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research on speech LLMs by highlighting the importance of spatial audio and directional information.  The S-DOT and CDDA techniques could be adopted and adapted by other researchers working on similar problems. The work provides a solid foundation for further exploration of multi-modal speech recognition using LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper deserves a score of **7**.  The work makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the feasibility of integrating directional information into speech LLMs for smart glasses applications. The proposed techniques are innovative in their application and yield measurable improvements. However, the reliance on simulated data and the use of relatively standard components somewhat limit the overall impact. Real-world testing and further refinement of the techniques, particularly for handling overlapping speech, would strengthen the work considerably. The findings are novel enough to merit interest and further investigation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hypothesis Testing for Quantifying LLM-Human Misalignment in Multiple Choice Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14997v1)
- **Authors**: Harbin Hong, Sebastian Caldas, Liu Leqi
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly appear in social science research (e.g., economics and marketing), it becomes crucial to assess how well these models replicate human behavior. In this work, using hypothesis testing, we present a quantitative framework to assess the misalignment between LLM-simulated and actual human behaviors in multiple-choice survey settings. This framework allows us to determine in a principled way whether a specific language model can effectively simulate human opinions, decision-making, and general behaviors represented through multiple-choice options. We applied this framework to a popular language model for simulating people's opinions in various public surveys and found that this model is ill-suited for simulating the tested sub-populations (e.g., across different races, ages, and incomes) for contentious questions. This raises questions about the alignment of this language model with the tested populations, highlighting the need for new practices in using LLMs for social science studies beyond naive simulations of human subjects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the critical issue of aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human opinions and behaviors, particularly in social science research contexts. The authors introduce a quantitative framework based on hypothesis testing to assess the misalignment between LLM-simulated responses and actual human responses in multiple-choice survey settings. The framework uses permutation tests and two test statistics (Pearson's chi-squared inspired and Kolmogorov-Smirnov) to determine if LLM outputs and human responses come from the same underlying distribution. The authors apply this framework to the Disagreement500 dataset and find that a common LLM (GPT-3.5-Turbo) struggles to accurately represent diverse human opinions, especially for contentious questions. The results reveal significant misalignment across demographic subgroups, indicating that the LLM fails to capture the variability and nuances of human perspectives.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its rigorous, quantitative approach to assessing LLM-human misalignment. While previous works have used distance metrics like Wasserstein Distance and KL Divergence, this paper argues that these metrics are often arbitrary and lack objective baselines. The hypothesis testing framework provides a more principled and interpretable way to determine the extent of misalignment, outputting p-values which allows for statistically significant comparison.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings are significant for the growing body of research using LLMs for social science simulations. It provides strong evidence that naive simulations of human subjects using LLMs can be misleading, especially when dealing with diverse opinions and contentious topics. This underscores the need for caution and more sophisticated approaches when employing LLMs in such studies. Highlighting limitations of current models helps refine and direct the approach of using LLMs as representative proxies of human populations.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The hypothesis testing framework provides a robust and statistically grounded approach.
    *   The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods for evaluating LLM alignment.
    *   The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the framework's ability to identify areas of misalignment.
    *   The analysis of subgroup-specific misalignment offers valuable insights into the model's biases.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study focuses on a single LLM (GPT-3.5-Turbo) and a single dataset (Disagreement500). Generalizability to other models and datasets could be further explored.
    *   The reliance on multiple-choice questions limits the complexity of human opinions that can be captured.
    *   While the paper introduces two test statistics, the rationale for choosing these specific statistics could be further elaborated. Are these the most appropriate statistics for the task, or are there alternatives that could offer additional insights?
    *   Although demographic steering prompts were used, the paper does not perform a thorough ablation study to test if this steering mechanism is effective in mitigating the biases.

* **Potential influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging more rigorous evaluation methods for LLM-based social science simulations.
    *   Raising awareness of the potential biases and limitations of LLMs in capturing diverse human opinions.
    *   Inspiring the development of new techniques for aligning LLMs with human values and perspectives.
    *   Shaping the ongoing debate about the appropriate uses and limitations of LLMs in social science.
    *   Providing a reproducible framework with statistical rigour that can be used to test future models on potentially biased datasets.

The thorough statistical approach and potential impact on the design and deployment of LLMs justifies a high, but not perfect, score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 8/10

### Memory Tokens: Large Language Models Can Generate Reversible Sentence Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15001v1)
- **Authors**: Ignacio Sastre, Aiala Rosá
- **Abstract**: In this work, we observe an interesting phenomenon: it is possible to generate reversible sentence embeddings that allow an LLM to reconstruct the original text exactly, without modifying the model's weights. This is achieved by introducing a special memory token, whose embedding is optimized through training on a fixed sequence. When prompted with this embedding, the model reconstructs the fixed sequence exactly. We evaluate this phenomenon across English and Spanish datasets, sequences of up to approximately 240 tokens, and model scales ranging from 100M to 8B parameters. Notably, Llama 3.1 8B successfully reconstructs all tested sequences. Our findings highlight an interesting capability of LLMs and suggest potential applications in memory-based retrieval, compression, and controlled text generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "memory tokens" in Large Language Models (LLMs). By training a special embedding associated with a new token on a fixed text sequence while keeping the rest of the model frozen, the authors demonstrate that LLMs can perfectly reconstruct the original sequence when prompted with this embedding. They evaluate this phenomenon across different domains, sequence lengths, languages, and model sizes, showing that larger models (especially Llama 3.1 8B) achieve perfect reconstruction. The authors suggest potential applications in memory-based retrieval, compression, controlled text generation, and discuss implications for adversarial attacks and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of training a single embedding to "memorize" a specific sequence is somewhat novel, although related to prompt tuning and prefix tuning. The paper's strength lies in demonstrating the *completeness* of the memorization – achieving perfect reconstruction. While P*-tuning methods are known, they often focus on task performance, not lossless sequence encoding. The connection to prompt compression is relevant, but the paper doesn't directly compare against state-of-the-art compression techniques. The observation regarding adversarial attacks, although interesting, is quite speculative.

*   **Significance:** The findings shed light on the representational capacity of LLMs, particularly their ability to encode and retrieve information precisely. This could have implications for how we understand information storage within LLMs and potentially lead to new memory-based retrieval systems that leverage these abilities. The perfect reconstruction result is intriguing and contributes to the understanding of the emergent properties of LLMs at scale.  However, the practicality of the proposed method is not fully explored. The computational cost of training the embedding and the limitation of the embedding encoding only one specific sequence raise concerns about its scalability and applicability in real-world scenarios. The lack of fine-tuning to adapt these embeddings for broader tasks hinders the overall significance. The paper presents a fascinating phenomenon, but its practical usefulness remains limited without further development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-written presentation.
    *   Rigorous experimental evaluation across different models and datasets.
    *   Identifies an interesting and somewhat unexpected capability of LLMs.
    *   Explores potential applications and limitations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited exploration of practical applications beyond perfect reconstruction.
    *   High computational cost and scalability concerns.
    *   Speculative discussion of adversarial attacks.
    *   The link with interpretability research is not deeply explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could inspire further research into memory-based retrieval mechanisms within LLMs and the development of more efficient encoding techniques. It might also encourage investigations into the interpretability of LLM embeddings. However, its impact will depend on whether the demonstrated phenomenon can be leveraged for practical applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** Although the results are interesting, the lack of practical usage beyond perfectly reproducing sequences limits the significance of the paper. The core idea has some novelty in demonstrating completeness but leans on previous P*-tuning techniques. There is a need for significant further development to make the technique practical.
    Therefore, while technically sound and interesting, the paper does not represent a breakthrough contribution to the field.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scaling Intelligence: Designing Data Centers for Next-Gen Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15006v1)
- **Authors**: Jesmin Jahan Tithi, Hanjiang Wu, Avishaii Abuhatzera, Fabrizio Petrini
- **Abstract**: The explosive growth of Large Language Models (LLMs) - such as GPT-4 with 1.8 trillion parameters - demands a radical rethinking of data center architecture to ensure scalability, efficiency, and cost-effectiveness. Our work provides a comprehensive co-design framework that jointly explores FLOPS, HBM bandwidth and capacity, multiple network topologies (two-tier vs. FullFlat optical), the size of the scale-out domain, and popular parallelism/optimization strategies used in LLMs. We introduce and evaluate FullFlat network architectures, which provide uniform high-bandwidth, low-latency connectivity between all nodes, and demonstrate their transformative impact on performance and scalability. Through detailed sensitivity analyses, we quantify the benefits of overlapping compute and communication, leveraging hardware-accelerated collectives, wider scale-out domains, and larger memory capacity. Our study spans both sparse (mixture of experts) and dense transformer-based LLMs, revealing how system design choices affect Model FLOPS Utilization (MFU = Model flops per token x Observed tokens per sec / Peak flops of the hardware) and overall throughput. For the co-design study, we extended and validated a performance modeling tool capable of predicting LLM runtime within 10% of real-world measurements. Our findings offer actionable insights and a practical roadmap for designing AI data centers that can efficiently support trillion-parameter models, reduce optimization complexity, and sustain the rapid evolution of AI capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Scaling Intelligence: Designing Data Centers for Next-Gen Language Models" explores the co-design of data center architectures to efficiently support the training of trillion-parameter Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-4.  It investigates the interplay between FLOPS, HBM bandwidth and capacity, network topologies (specifically two-tier and a proposed "FullFlat" optical network), scale-out domain size, and various LLM parallelism strategies. The key contribution is a comprehensive framework for evaluating these factors jointly. The paper introduces and assesses FullFlat network architectures, which provide uniform high-bandwidth, low-latency connectivity, and quantifies the benefits of overlapping compute and communication, wider scale-out domains, and larger memory capacities. The analysis covers both sparse (Mixture of Experts) and dense transformer-based LLMs.  The authors extended and validated a performance modeling tool to predict LLM runtime. The findings aim to provide actionable insights for designing AI data centers optimized for large LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to data center co-design for LLMs, specifically the detailed evaluation of FullFlat optical networks.  While previous work has examined individual aspects (e.g., network topologies, parallelism strategies), this paper attempts to integrate them into a single comprehensive framework. The application of co-design principles to next-generation data centers and the proposal of a new network topology (FullFlat) represent a significant contribution. This paper is also one of the first public investigations of FullFlat networks impact on LLM workloads. The insights regarding the diminishing returns of larger HBDs (high bandwidth domains) once MoE communication fits within them, and FullFlat's enabling of tensor parallelism beyond a node boundary, challenge current assumptions and practices.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in AI infrastructure: how to efficiently train increasingly large LLMs. The findings have practical implications for data center architects, hardware vendors, and AI researchers.  The proposed FullFlat architecture, if realized, could alleviate communication bottlenecks and improve the scalability of LLM training. The performance modeling tool and sensitivity analyses provide valuable guidance for optimizing data center designs. The explicit consideration of both sparse and dense models is also significant, given the emerging trend of distilling sparse models for faster inference.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive co-design framework considering multiple layers of the system.
    *   Detailed performance analysis and sensitivity studies.
    *   Evaluation of a promising novel network architecture (FullFlat).
    *   Consideration of both sparse (MoE) and dense LLMs.
    *   Validation with a performance modeling tool (although details are reserved for another paper).
    *   Clear identification of trade-offs and actionable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The FullFlat network analysis is based on *projected* technology (co-packaged optics by 2026/2027) rather than existing deployments. The assumptions about the performance characteristics of these future networks need to be carefully scrutinized as many challenges related to full flat networks remain to be addressed.
    *   The paper lacks detailed information on the extended performance modeling tool.  Without knowing specifics about the tool's implementation, validation dataset, and accuracy, it's difficult to fully assess the reliability of the results. While mentioned the tool's accuracy to within 10% of actual system performance (on Mistral), it would benefit from more thorough validation against GPT-3 and GPT-4 implementations.
    *   The discussion of power consumption and TCO savings related to FullFlat networks is relatively high-level.  A more quantitative analysis would strengthen the argument.
    *   The assumption of 80% communication efficiency in scale up and scale-out domains may be overly optimistic, particularly at larger scales, as demonstrated by figure 5 (lower efficiency).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings have the potential to influence the design of future AI data centers. The FullFlat network architecture could become a viable alternative to traditional two-tier designs, particularly as optical interconnect technologies mature. The co-design framework and sensitivity analyses could guide hardware and software optimizations for LLM training.
*   **Critical Justification of the Score:**  Given the paper's novelty, significance, the identified strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes valuable contributions by highlighting the importance of considering multiple factors, providing comprehensive co-design, and providing forward-looking insights into the design of AI-accelerated data centers, especially related to the role of all-optical networks. However, the reliance on projected technology, the limitations of validation, and lack of quantitative cost analysis moderate the score. The impact of this work will depend on the real-world performance and cost-effectiveness of FullFlat networks and the adoption of the proposed co-design principles in practice.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hyper-Local Deformable Transformers for Text Spotting on Historical Maps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15010v1)
- **Authors**: Yijun Lin, Yao-Yi Chiang
- **Abstract**: Text on historical maps contains valuable information providing georeferenced historical, political, and cultural contexts. However, text extraction from historical maps is challenging due to the lack of (1) effective methods and (2) training data. Previous approaches use ad-hoc steps tailored to only specific map styles. Recent machine learning-based text spotters (e.g., for scene images) have the potential to solve these challenges because of their flexibility in supporting various types of text instances. However, these methods remain challenges in extracting precise image features for predicting every sub-component (boundary points and characters) in a text instance. This is critical because map text can be lengthy and highly rotated with complex backgrounds, posing difficulties in detecting relevant image features from a rough text region. This paper proposes PALETTE, an end-to-end text spotter for scanned historical maps of a wide variety. PALETTE introduces a novel hyper-local sampling module to explicitly learn localized image features around the target boundary points and characters of a text instance for detection and recognition. PALETTE also enables hyper-local positional embeddings to learn spatial interactions between boundary points and characters within and across text instances. In addition, this paper presents a novel approach to automatically generate synthetic map images, SynthMap+, for training text spotters for historical maps. The experiment shows that PALETTE with SynthMap+ outperforms SOTA text spotters on two new benchmark datasets of historical maps, particularly for long and angled text. We have deployed PALETTE with SynthMap+ to process over 60,000 maps in the David Rumsey Historical Map collection and generated over 100 million text labels to support map searching. The project is released at https://github.com/kartta-foundation/mapkurator-palette-doc.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PALETTE, a novel end-to-end text spotter designed explicitly for scanned historical maps.  PALETTE addresses the challenges of extracting text from these maps, which often suffer from variations in style, length, orientation, and complex backgrounds. The key innovations are: (1) a "hyper-local sampling" module that learns localized image features around individual boundary points and characters instead of relying on a single reference point for an entire text instance, and (2) "hyper-local positional embeddings" to capture spatial relationships between sub-components of the text.  The paper also presents SYNTHMAP+, a synthetic dataset of historical map images created automatically to address the lack of training data. The results show that PALETTE, trained with SYNTHMAP+, outperforms state-of-the-art text spotters on two new historical map datasets, especially for long and angled text. The system has been deployed to process a large collection of historical maps, generating text labels for search.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The hyper-local sampling and positional embedding approach is the most significant novelty.  Traditional object detection techniques rely on single reference points, which are inadequate for long, curved text with varying character spacing. PALETTE's sub-component level feature extraction is a clear advance. The SYNTHMAP+ dataset is also a valuable contribution given the scarcity of labeled data in this domain. While synthetic data generation is not entirely new, tailoring it to historical map styles adds value.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical and important problem.  Historical maps are valuable resources, but their content is difficult to access without automated text extraction.  Improved text spotting directly enables map searching, georeferencing, and further analysis. The scale of the deployment (over 60,000 maps) highlights the real-world impact. Also, creating large text labelled historic maps datasets is extremely important for future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of text spotting on historical maps.
    *   **Technical Innovation:** The hyper-local sampling and positional embedding are well-motivated and appear effective.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough experimentation with state-of-the-art baselines and ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed components.
    *   **Real-World Impact:** The deployment and large-scale processing of maps demonstrate the practicality of the approach.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The SYNTHMAP+ dataset will likely be a valuable resource for the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** While quantitative results are presented, more qualitative analysis showing failure cases of other methods and how PALETTE succeeds would strengthen the paper.  Examples of the learned positional embeddings and their effect on performance would be insightful. The visual examples of the final result (Figure 6) could be improved.
    *   **Reliance on Deformable DETR:**  The paper builds on the Deformable DETR architecture. While this is a solid foundation, the novelty is primarily in the sampling and embedding strategy, not the underlying architecture.
    *   **Future Work:** The paper could have provided more in depth direction of potential future research directions.

*   **Potential Influence:** PALETTE's hyper-local sampling strategy could be applicable to other document analysis tasks involving complex layouts or variable text styles. The SYNTHMAP+ dataset will likely be adopted by other researchers working on historical map processing.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated approach to a challenging and important problem. The hyper-local sampling strategy addresses a critical limitation of existing text spotting methods. The creation and open-sourcing of the SYNTHMAP+ dataset are also significant contributions. However, the paper builds on Deformable DETR, and the qualitative analysis could be expanded. The work represents a strong, incremental advance in the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SFT-GO: Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15021v1)
- **Authors**: Gyuhak Kim, Sumiran Singh Thakur, Su Min Park, Wei Wei, Yujia Bao
- **Abstract**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become an essential step in tailoring large language models (LLMs) to align with human expectations and specific downstream tasks. However, existing SFT methods typically treat each training instance as a uniform sequence, giving equal importance to all tokens regardless of their relevance. This overlooks the fact that only a subset of tokens often contains critical, task-specific information. To address this limitation, we introduce Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization (SFT-GO), a novel approach that treats groups of tokens differently based on their importance.SFT-GO groups tokens in each sample based on their importance values and optimizes the LLM using a weighted combination of the worst-group loss and the standard cross-entropy loss. This mechanism adaptively emphasizes the most challenging token groups and guides the model to better handle different group distributions, thereby improving overall learning dynamics. We provide a theoretical analysis of SFT-GO's convergence rate, demonstrating its efficiency. Empirically, we apply SFT-GO with three different token grouping strategies and show that models trained with SFT-GO consistently outperform baseline approaches across popular LLM benchmarks. These improvements hold across various datasets and base models, demonstrating the robustness and the effectiveness of our method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SFT-GO (Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization), a novel approach to supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for large language models (LLMs). SFT-GO addresses the limitation of existing SFT methods that treat all tokens in a training instance as equally important, even though some tokens are more critical to task-specific information. SFT-GO groups tokens based on their importance values (using TF-IDF, LLMLingua-2, or a loss-based method like Rho-1) and optimizes the LLM using a weighted combination of the worst-group loss and standard cross-entropy loss.  This allows the model to focus on difficult token groups, enhancing learning dynamics. The paper provides theoretical analysis, demonstrating SFT-GO's convergence efficiency. Empirical results across various datasets (LIMA, Alpaca) and base models (Llama 3.2-3B, Llama 3.1-8B) show that SFT-GO consistently outperforms baseline SFT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of grouping tokens by importance and optimizing based on the "worst-group loss" is novel in the context of SFT for LLMs. The paper's strength is formalizing this intuition into a concrete framework with theoretical backing. However, the individual components used for grouping (TF-IDF, LLMLingua-2, Rho-1) are not new. The novelty lies in their specific application *within* the SFT-GO framework.  The reformulation of Rho-1 as a special case is a minor but welcome contribution. The theoretical analysis helps formalize the work.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically relevant problem: making SFT more efficient and effective by focusing on the most informative parts of the data.  The consistent empirical improvements across different datasets and base models suggest that SFT-GO is a robust technique. The analysis of different grouping strategies is insightful and provides guidance for applying SFT-GO in different scenarios. The convergence rate analysis also strengthens the theoretical validity of this approach. The gains are noticeable but not groundbreaking, which should be expected.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined SFT-GO framework.
    *   Theoretical analysis of convergence.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple datasets, base models, and grouping strategies.
    *   Insightful analysis of the impact of different grouping functions.
    *   Provides practical guidance on applying the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual token grouping techniques are not novel.
    *   The improvements, while consistent, are not transformational. The results are solid but not "revolutionary."
    *   The convexity assumption in the theoretical analysis is a potential limitation, given that LLMs are non-convex.
    *   The computational overhead of grouping tokens and computing the worst-group loss is not explicitly discussed. This could be an important practical consideration, especially for very large models and datasets, and is worth mentioning.
    * While the paper highlights the potential broader impacts concerning fairness and bias mitigation, it would be improved by suggesting concrete ways these concerns can be addressed within the SFT-GO framework.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SFT-GO is likely to be adopted as a refinement technique in existing SFT pipelines. The framework's modularity allows for incorporating new and potentially more sophisticated token grouping strategies. By providing a formal basis and solid empirical evidence, the paper can encourage further research on token-level optimization for LLMs. The theoretical guarantee is a value added that is not common in the field.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing a practical and theoretically grounded framework for improving SFT of LLMs. While not groundbreaking, SFT-GO offers a simple yet effective way to enhance learning dynamics by focusing on critical tokens. The empirical results support the robustness of the approach, and the analysis of different grouping strategies provides valuable insights. A more thorough discussion of practical implementation costs, or explicit addressing for fairness & bias would increase the real-world value.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the novelty of combining existing techniques into a unified framework. It is a significant improvement and addresses a clear problem. The results are robust and promising, along with a theoretical foundation that justifies the approach. Some weaknesses (computational overhead, and limited scope of mitigation for fairness/bias) lower the score.

### Optimal Embedding Learning Rate in LLMs: The Effect of Vocabulary Size
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15025v1)
- **Authors**: Soufiane Hayou, Liyuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Pretraining large language models is a costly process. To make this process more efficient, several methods have been proposed to optimize model architecture/parametrization and hardware use. On the parametrization side, $\mu P$ (Maximal Update Parametrization) parametrizes model weights and learning rate (LR) in a way that makes hyperparameters (HPs) transferable with width (embedding dimension): HPs can be tuned for a small model and used for larger models without additional tuning. While $\mu$P showed impressive results in practice, recent empirical studies have reported conflicting observations when applied to LLMs. One limitation of the theory behind $\mu$P is the fact that input dimension (vocabulary size in LLMs) is considered fixed when taking the width to infinity. This is unrealistic since vocabulary size is generally much larger than width in practice. In this work, we provide a theoretical analysis of the effect of vocabulary size on training dynamics, and subsequently show that as vocabulary size increases, the training dynamics \emph{interpolate between the $\mu$P regime and another regime that we call Large Vocab (LV) Regime}, where optimal scaling rules are different from those predicted by $\mu$P. Our analysis reveals that in the LV regime, the optimal embedding LR to hidden LR ratio should roughly scale as $\Theta(\sqrt{width})$, surprisingly close to the empirical findings previously reported in the literature, and different from the $\Theta(width)$ ratio predicted by $\mu$P. We conduct several experiments to validate our theory, and pretrain a 1B model from scratch to show the benefit of our suggested scaling rule for the embedding LR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the impact of vocabulary size on the optimal learning rate for the embedding layer in large language models (LLMs) during pretraining. The authors argue that the standard µP (Maximal Update Parametrization) theory, which suggests a constant learning rate for the embedding layer regardless of model width, is inadequate because it assumes a fixed vocabulary size, which is unrealistic in practice.  They theoretically demonstrate the existence of a "Large Vocab" (LV) regime where the optimal ratio between the embedding layer's learning rate and the hidden layers' learning rate scales with the square root of the model width (√d). They validate this theory through experiments, including pretraining a 1B model, showing that their suggested scaling rule improves performance compared to µP and standard parametrization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly addressing the influence of vocabulary size on the learning rate scaling rules derived from µP. While previous empirical studies have hinted at discrepancies between µP's predictions and observed LLM behavior, this work provides a theoretical framework explaining why µP might fail in the context of LLMs, where vocabulary size is often much larger than model width. The identification of the "Large Vocab" (LV) regime and the corresponding √d scaling rule for the embedding layer's learning rate are significant contributions.

*   **Significance:** The work has practical significance as it provides a more informed approach to hyperparameter tuning for LLM pretraining. The finding that embedding learning rates need to be adapted differently when vocabulary size grows can lead to more efficient training and improved model performance. This has the potential to reduce the computational cost associated with pretraining large models. Moreover, the theoretical analysis provides a better understanding of the dynamics of feature learning in LLMs, which can guide future research on scaling laws and parameter optimization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper presents a clear and rigorous theoretical analysis that explains the observed empirical phenomena. The framework is based on simplifying assumptions (linear networks, SignSGD), but it captures the key dynamics relevant to the vocabulary size impact.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The theoretical findings are supported by extensive experimental results. The experiments, ranging from small models to a 1B model, demonstrate the benefits of the suggested scaling rule for different model sizes and datasets.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and the arguments are easy to follow. The notations are well-defined and the key concepts are explained clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on simplifying assumptions, such as linear networks and SignSGD. While these assumptions allow for a tractable analysis, they may not fully capture the complexity of modern LLMs with attention mechanisms and Adam optimizers. The authors acknowledge this limitation and discuss the potential relevance of their findings to full transformer architectures based on the residual stream.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on the embedding layer's learning rate. While this is a critical component, other hyperparameters (e.g., initialization schemes) might also be affected by vocabulary size. Exploring the interplay between vocabulary size and other hyperparameters would further enhance the understanding of LLM training dynamics.
    *   **Variance around the √d line:** There is significant variance around the optimal value of embedding learning rates from the experiments on smaller models. This indicates that there are potentially more fundamental limitations for the parametrization to fully capture the impact of vocab size on embedding learning rate.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's insights have the potential to influence the design and training of future LLMs. The √d scaling rule provides a more informed starting point for hyperparameter tuning, potentially saving significant computational resources. The theoretical framework can also inspire further research on the interaction between model architecture, vocabulary size, and learning dynamics.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by theoretically and empirically demonstrating the impact of vocabulary size on embedding learning rate scaling in LLMs, addressing a gap in the understanding provided by standard µP theory. The proposed √d scaling rule is practical and shown to improve training efficiency. While the theoretical analysis relies on simplifying assumptions, the core insights are well-supported by experimental evidence. Overall, this work constitutes a significant step forward in understanding and optimizing LLM pretraining.

### Identifying economic narratives in large text corpora -- An integrated approach using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15041v1)
- **Authors**: Tobias Schmidt, Kai-Robin Lange, Matthias Reccius, Henrik Müller, Michael Roos, Carsten Jentsch
- **Abstract**: As interest in economic narratives has grown in recent years, so has the number of pipelines dedicated to extracting such narratives from texts. Pipelines often employ a mix of state-of-the-art natural language processing techniques, such as BERT, to tackle this task. While effective on foundational linguistic operations essential for narrative extraction, such models lack the deeper semantic understanding required to distinguish extracting economic narratives from merely conducting classic tasks like Semantic Role Labeling. Instead of relying on complex model pipelines, we evaluate the benefits of Large Language Models (LLMs) by analyzing a corpus of Wall Street Journal and New York Times newspaper articles about inflation. We apply a rigorous narrative definition and compare GPT-4o outputs to gold-standard narratives produced by expert annotators. Our results suggests that GPT-4o is capable of extracting valid economic narratives in a structured format, but still falls short of expert-level performance when handling complex documents and narratives. Given the novelty of LLMs in economic research, we also provide guidance for future work in economics and the social sciences that employs LLMs to pursue similar objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, focusing on novelty, significance, and a justified score:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for extracting economic narratives from text.  It argues that traditional NLP methods, while scalable, lack the semantic understanding needed to accurately identify and extract nuanced economic narratives.  The authors evaluate the performance of GPT-4o on a corpus of Wall Street Journal and New York Times articles about inflation, comparing its output to a gold-standard dataset created by expert annotators. They find that GPT-4o is capable of extracting valid economic narratives in a structured format but falls short of expert-level performance, particularly when dealing with complex documents and narratives. The paper also discusses prompt engineering strategies and provides guidance for future research using LLMs in economics and social sciences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in applying state-of-the-art LLMs, specifically GPT-4o, to the task of economic narrative extraction, a field that has traditionally relied on more rule-based or smaller-scale machine learning approaches. While other works have started to explore LLMs for content analysis, this paper provides a focused evaluation within the context of economic narratives, operationalizing a rigorous narrative definition and comparing LLM performance against expert annotations. The emphasis on prompt engineering and the identification of challenges related to causal chain identification contributes some valuable insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: efficiently and accurately extracting economic narratives from the vast amount of textual data available.  Economic narratives are increasingly recognized as crucial drivers of economic behavior, and the ability to quantify and analyze them at scale could provide valuable insights for policymakers and researchers.  The paper's findings, while indicating limitations of current LLMs, highlight the potential of this approach and offer practical guidance for future research. The effort to construct a gold standard and compare to human annotation is a significant contribution. The demonstration of narrative extraction's inherent subjectivity adds to the value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Rigorous methodology:  The use of a gold-standard dataset created by expert annotators provides a strong benchmark for evaluating LLM performance.
    *   Detailed analysis of prompt engineering strategies: The paper explores different prompting approaches and identifies effective strategies for maximizing LLM performance.
    *   Identification of limitations:  The authors acknowledge the limitations of current LLMs and highlight challenges related to narrative density bias and the identification of complex causal structures.
    *   Clear articulation of the research problem and its importance: The paper effectively motivates the need for efficient narrative extraction methods and clearly outlines the potential benefits of using LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited Generalizability: The evaluation focuses solely on inflation narratives within a specific corpus (WSJ and NYT). This limits the generalizability of the findings to other economic topics or textual sources.
    *   Lack of Comparison to more traditional methods: While the paper emphasizes the limitations of traditional NLP approaches, it lacks a direct comparison to a state-of-the-art traditional method (e.g., a well-tuned BERT pipeline) on the same dataset. This makes it difficult to definitively assess the improvement offered by LLMs.
    *   The paper's post-processing pipeline, while acknowledged, is somewhat vaguely defined. A more detailed explanation and justification of the pipeline's steps would strengthen the analysis. The reliance on GPT-4o also introduces reproducibility concerns.
    * Limited use of other recent literature and techniques: In the current economic literature on text analysis there is a large literature on using embeddings in novel ways or to construct novel dictionaries. This could be leveraged in future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in economics and social sciences by demonstrating the potential and limitations of LLMs for narrative extraction. It provides a valuable framework for future studies, including the use of gold-standard datasets, detailed analysis of prompt engineering strategies, and careful consideration of the limitations of LLMs. Given the rapid advancements in LLMs, this paper can encourage further exploration of these models and the post-processing for economic text analysis.

**Score: 7.0**

**Justification:**
The paper demonstrates a valuable early effort in using LLMs for a complex economic task. While the novelty is not groundbreaking—other fields have been using LLMs in a similar fashion—the paper makes a significant contribution within the field of economics by evaluating LLMs' capabilities for economic narrative extraction and provides a solid benchmark for future research. The limitations (corpus specificity, lack of direct comparison, reliance on a proprietary model) prevent a higher score. It's a well-executed application of existing techniques in a relatively new domain, offering a solid foundation for future studies, while exhibiting an insightful analysis and benchmarked approach. While the influence is likely to be solid, the scope is somewhat limited.

### Mapping Caregiver Needs to AI Chatbot Design: Strengths and Gaps in Mental Health Support for Alzheimer's and Dementia Caregivers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15047v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayue Melissa Shi, Dong Whi Yoo, Keran Wang, Violeta J. Rodriguez, Ravi Karkar, Koustuv Saha
- **Abstract**: Family caregivers of individuals with Alzheimer's Disease and Related Dementia (AD/ADRD) face significant emotional and logistical challenges that place them at heightened risk for stress, anxiety, and depression. Although recent advances in generative AI -- particularly large language models (LLMs) -- offer new opportunities to support mental health, little is known about how caregivers perceive and engage with such technologies. To address this gap, we developed Carey, a GPT-4o-based chatbot designed to provide informational and emotional support to AD/ADRD caregivers. Using Carey as a technology probe, we conducted semi-structured interviews with 16 family caregivers following scenario-driven interactions grounded in common caregiving stressors. Through inductive coding and reflexive thematic analysis, we surface a systemic understanding of caregiver needs and expectations across six themes -- on-demand information access, emotional support, safe space for disclosure, crisis management, personalization, and data privacy. For each of these themes, we also identified the nuanced tensions in the caregivers' desires and concerns. We present a mapping of caregiver needs, AI chatbot's strengths, gaps, and design recommendations. Our findings offer theoretical and practical insights to inform the design of proactive, trustworthy, and caregiver-centered AI systems that better support the evolving mental health needs of AD/ADRD caregivers.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and a score between 1 and 10.

**Summary:**

The paper "Mapping Caregiver Needs to AI Chatbot Design: Strengths and Gaps in Mental Health Support for Alzheimer's and Dementia Caregivers" investigates the needs and expectations of family caregivers of individuals with Alzheimer's Disease and Related Dementias (AD/ADRD) regarding AI chatbots designed to provide mental health support. The authors developed a GPT-4o-based chatbot named Carey and conducted semi-structured interviews with 16 AD/ADRD caregivers, who interacted with Carey through scenario-based simulations of common caregiving stressors. The study identified key themes related to caregiver needs, the strengths and limitations of the AI chatbot in meeting those needs, and design recommendations for future AI-driven support systems. The themes include on-demand information access, emotional support, safe space for disclosure, crisis management, personalization capabilities, and data privacy. The research highlights the tension between the benefits of AI, such as accessibility and nonjudgmental support, and the concerns about trustworthiness, lack of personalization, and ethical considerations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on a Critical Area:** The paper addresses an important and growing problem - the mental health challenges faced by AD/ADRD caregivers. This is a significant area of concern with substantial social and economic impact.
    *   **User-Centered Approach:** The study uses a user-centered approach, interviewing caregivers and having them interact with a prototype chatbot. This allows for the elicitation of realistic needs and expectations from the target population, which is essential for designing effective solutions.
    *   **Comprehensive Thematic Analysis:** The paper's thematic analysis appears rigorous and provides a rich understanding of the multifaceted needs and concerns of caregivers. The identification of six main themes and associated tensions demonstrates a nuanced understanding of the problem space.
    *   **Actionable Design Recommendations:** The design recommendations are concrete and actionable, offering a clear path for future development efforts. The recommendations go beyond simply stating the problems and provide practical suggestions for addressing them.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper addresses important ethical considerations around data privacy, trust, and the potential for AI to replace human interaction. This is crucial given the vulnerable nature of the target population.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The study is grounded in established frameworks like Pearlin's Stress Process Model, which strengthens the theoretical foundation and provides a valuable context for interpreting the findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Small Sample Size:** The sample size of 16 caregivers is relatively small and might not capture the full diversity of experiences within the AD/ADRD caregiver population. While the sample demographics appear reasonably balanced, larger studies may be needed to confirm the findings.
    *   **Prototype Limitations:** The chatbot used, while GPT-4o-based, is still a prototype. Its capabilities and limitations might not fully represent the potential of future AI systems. The paper acknowledges these limitations.
    *   **Lack of Clinical Validation:** While the study elicits valuable user feedback, it lacks clinical validation. There is no direct assessment of the impact of the chatbot on caregiver mental health outcomes such as anxiety, depression, or burden.
    *   **Scenario-Based Interactions:** While using scenarios helps to standardize the interactions and research, it may not fully capture the nuances and complexities of real-world caregiving situations.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Existing Solutions:** While the paper references existing work, it could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of how Carey differentiates itself from other existing chatbot solutions in the mental health or caregiving space.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The study provides a comprehensive exploration of caregivers' needs and expectations in the context of AI-driven mental health support, which is a relatively unexplored area.
    *   The detailed mapping of caregiver needs, chatbot strengths and gaps, and design recommendations offers a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working on AI-based solutions for this population.
    *   The identification of nuanced tensions surrounding AI-driven support, such as trust, personalization, and privacy, contributes to the broader literature on human-AI interaction in sensitive domains.
    *   The research fills a gap in understanding the specific requirements of AD/ADRD caregivers regarding AI mental health support.

**Justification for the Score:**

Overall, this paper makes a valuable contribution to the field. It is well-motivated, uses a sound research methodology, provides a thorough analysis of the data, and offers actionable design recommendations. While the limitations related to sample size and prototype capabilities somewhat temper its impact, the paper's focus on a critical problem area and its comprehensive exploration of caregiver needs make it a significant contribution. The insights on ethics and trustworthy design are particularly valuable. There is room for improving it with quantitative validation and better comparative analysis.

### Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15050v1)
- **Authors**: Tiantian Fan, Lingjun Liu, Yu Yue, Jiaze Chen, Chengyi Wang, Qiying Yu, Chi Zhang, Zhiqi Lin, Ruofei Zhu, Yufeng Yuan, Xiaochen Zuo, Bole Ma, Mofan Zhang, Gaohong Liu, Ru Zhang, Haotian Zhou, Cong Xie, Ruidong Zhu, Zhi Zhang, Xin Liu, Mingxuan Wang, Lin Yan, Yonghui Wu
- **Abstract**: Recently, test-time scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional reasoning capabilities across scientific and professional tasks by generating long chains-of-thought (CoT). As a crucial component for developing these reasoning models, reinforcement learning (RL), exemplified by Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and its variants, allows models to learn through trial and error. However, PPO can be time-consuming due to its inherent on-policy nature, which is further exacerbated by increasing response lengths. In this work, we propose Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization (T-PPO), a novel extension to PPO that improves training efficiency by streamlining policy update and length-restricted response generation. T-PPO mitigates the issue of low hardware utilization, an inherent drawback of fully synchronized long-generation procedures, where resources often sit idle during the waiting periods for complete rollouts. Our contributions are two-folds. First, we propose Extended Generalized Advantage Estimation (EGAE) for advantage estimation derived from incomplete responses while maintaining the integrity of policy learning. Second, we devise a computationally optimized mechanism that allows for the independent optimization of the policy and value models. By selectively filtering prompt and truncated tokens, this mechanism reduces redundant computations and accelerates the training process without sacrificing convergence performance. We demonstrate the effectiveness and efficacy of T-PPO on AIME 2024 with a 32B base model. The experimental results show that T-PPO improves the training efficiency of reasoning LLMs by up to 2.5x and outperforms its existing competitors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization (T-PPO), a novel extension to the Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) algorithm designed to improve training efficiency for reinforcement learning (RL) tasks with Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in reasoning tasks that involve long chains-of-thought (CoT). T-PPO addresses the inherent inefficiencies of PPO due to its on-policy nature and the computational overhead associated with long CoT trajectories. The key innovations include: (1) Extended Generalized Advantage Estimation (EGAE) to handle partially generated responses, enabling progressive policy updates and improved resource utilization. (2) A token-level optimization strategy with selective filtering to reduce redundant computations. Experimental results on the AIME 2024 benchmark demonstrate that T-PPO achieves higher training efficiency and outperforms existing competitors without compromising model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper proposes a useful enhancement to PPO specifically geared towards LLM reasoning tasks. The EGAE and the token-level optimization strategy are presented as novel components. However, the core idea of truncating trajectories and adapting advantage estimation has been explored in related contexts (e.g., using partial rollouts, off-policy learning by reusing a large chunk of previous trajectories when sampling, thus avoiding the cost of regenerating new trajectories from scratch). The novelty lies in the specific combination and adaptation of these ideas for the LLM CoT setting and the detailed implementation.
* **Significance:** Improving the training efficiency of RL for LLMs is a significant problem. The experimental results on the AIME benchmark showing 2.5x speedup compared to the state-of-the-art PPO algorithms with strong performance are compelling. If validated by other benchmarks and models, this could have a practical impact on the development and deployment of reasoning-capable LLMs. The ablation study and Roofline analysis provide insights into the performance gains.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem statement and motivation
    * Well-explained algorithm and implementation details.
    * Strong experimental results on a challenging benchmark (AIME).
    * Detailed efficiency analysis and comparison with baselines.
    * Offers architectural insights from roofline analysis.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The theoretical grounding of EGAE could be stronger. While it claims to maintain the integrity of policy learning, a more rigorous analysis of its impact on convergence guarantees would be beneficial.
    * The novelty is incremental, building upon existing techniques for handling partial trajectories and advantage estimation in RL.
    * The AIME benchmark, while relevant, is limited in scope. Evaluation on a broader range of tasks would strengthen the claims of general applicability.
    * The writing can be somewhat dense and could benefit from further clarification, especially regarding the technical details of EGAE.
* **Potential Influence:** T-PPO has the potential to influence the development of more efficient RL algorithms for LLMs, particularly in tasks that require complex reasoning and long CoT generation. The EGAE and token-level optimization strategy could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

T-PPO presents a relevant and potentially impactful improvement to PPO for LLM reasoning tasks. The experimental results are promising, and the efficiency analysis provides valuable insights. However, the novelty is incremental, and the theoretical analysis could be strengthened. Also, it suffers from weaknesses that impact the breadth of the impact on the research field.

### HEAL: An Empirical Study on Hallucinations in Embodied Agents Driven by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15065v1)
- **Authors**: Trishna Chakraborty, Udita Ghosh, Xiaopan Zhang, Fahim Faisal Niloy, Yue Dong, Jiachen Li, Amit K. Roy-Chowdhury, Chengyu Song
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being adopted as the cognitive core of embodied agents. However, inherited hallucinations, which stem from failures to ground user instructions in the observed physical environment, can lead to navigation errors, such as searching for a refrigerator that does not exist. In this paper, we present the first systematic study of hallucinations in LLM-based embodied agents performing long-horizon tasks under scene-task inconsistencies. Our goal is to understand to what extent hallucinations occur, what types of inconsistencies trigger them, and how current models respond. To achieve these goals, we construct a hallucination probing set by building on an existing benchmark, capable of inducing hallucination rates up to 40x higher than base prompts. Evaluating 12 models across two simulation environments, we find that while models exhibit reasoning, they fail to resolve scene-task inconsistencies-highlighting fundamental limitations in handling infeasible tasks. We also provide actionable insights on ideal model behavior for each scenario, offering guidance for developing more robust and reliable planning strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HEAL: An Empirical Study on Hallucinations in Embodied Agents Driven by Large Language Models" systematically investigates hallucinations in LLM-driven embodied agents performing long-horizon tasks, focusing on scene-task inconsistencies.  The authors create a new hallucination probing set, building upon an existing benchmark, and evaluate 12 models across two simulation environments (VirtualHome and BEHAVIOR).  They control for different types of inconsistencies, including distractor injection, task-relevant object removal, synonymous object substitution, and scene-task contradictions.  The study aims to understand the extent of hallucinations, identify triggers, and analyze model responses. The key findings are that models struggle to resolve scene-task inconsistencies, especially when asked to perform infeasible tasks.  The paper also explores mitigation strategies and discusses the impact of cross-modal information (vision + text).  The authors emphasize the importance of robust planning strategies and providing guidance on ideal model behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-designed, systematic empirical study of a critical problem: hallucinations in embodied agents. Prior works mostly focused on general LLM hallucinations or incidental cases in embodied AI.  The explicit targeting and quantification of different *types* of scene-task inconsistencies is a significant contribution.  Creating a probing set specifically designed to induce hallucinations in this context is also novel.

*   **Significance:** Hallucinations in embodied agents can lead to serious consequences (damage, safety hazards). Understanding and mitigating these issues is crucial for the reliable deployment of these agents.  The paper's findings highlight fundamental limitations in how LLMs ground instructions in physical environments and handle infeasible tasks.  The actionable insights regarding ideal model behavior provide valuable guidance for future research. The focus on long-horizon tasks adds to the value, as it pushes the problem beyond simple QA scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic methodology with controlled experiments.
    *   Clear definition and quantification of hallucinations in the embodied agent context.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across multiple models and simulation environments.
    *   Analysis of different types of scene-task inconsistencies.
    *   Exploration of mitigation strategies and cross-modal effects.
    *   Provides actionable insights into model behavior and failure modes.
    * The use of two datasets is also a benefit for generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study is primarily empirical, focusing on observing and quantifying hallucinations.  While the authors offer explanations, there is a lack of deep theoretical analysis of *why* these failures occur. (This is acceptable considering it's more of an empirical probing paper).
    *   The mitigation strategies explored are somewhat basic (self-correction), and their effectiveness is limited, especially under scene-task contradiction. This indicates that the underlying problem is more complex than a simple self-correction mechanism can resolve.
    *   The cross-modal experiments are limited to the DistInj setting and are conducted at a small scale. Expanding these experiments to other inconsistency types and a larger dataset would strengthen the conclusions.
    *  The paper focuses on symbolic goal generation (LTL) which may not be representative of all embodied agents. A more diverse set of outputs from the LLMs could be insightful.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could have a substantial impact on the field. It provides a clear framework for studying hallucinations in embodied agents, identifies key failure modes, and offers valuable insights for developing more robust and reliable planning strategies. It can guide future research in areas such as improved grounding techniques, task infeasibility detection, and cross-modal reasoning. This rigorous study can serve as a benchmark for evaluating progress in mitigating hallucinations in embodied AI systems.

**Overall:** This is a well-executed and significant empirical study that sheds light on a critical problem in LLM-driven embodied agents. The systematic methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and actionable insights make it a valuable contribution to the field. While the paper could benefit from more theoretical analysis and exploration of more advanced mitigation strategies, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses.

### ChatModel: Automating Reference Model Design and Verification with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15066v1)
- **Authors**: Jianmin Ye, Tianyang Liu, Qi Tian, Shengchu Su, Zhe Jiang, Xi Wang
- **Abstract**: As the complexity of integrated circuit designs continues to escalate, the functional verification becomes increasingly challenging. Reference models, critical for accelerating the verification process, are themselves becoming more intricate and time-consuming to develop. Despite the promise shown by large language models (LLMs) in code programming, effectively generating complex reference models remains a significant hurdle. To address these challenges, we introduce ChatModel, the first LLM-aided agile reference model generation and verification platform. ChatModel streamlines the transition from design specifications to fully functional reference models by integrating design standardization and hierarchical agile modeling. Employing a building-block generation strategy, it not only enhances the design capabilities of LLMs for reference models but also significantly boosts verification efficiency. We evaluated ChatModel on 300 designs of varying complexity, demonstrating substantial improvements in both efficiency and quality of reference model generation. ChatModel achieved a peak performance improvement of 55.02% compared to alternative methods, with notable enhancements in generation stability, and delivered a 9.18x increase in its capacity to produce reference model designs. Furthermore, it accelerated the iterative process of reference model design and validation by an average of 5.90x compared to traditional approaches. These results highlight the potential of ChatModel to significantly advance the automation of reference model generation and validation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ChatModel: Automating Reference Model Design and Verification with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChatModel, a novel LLM-aided platform designed to automate the generation and verification of reference models for integrated circuit (IC) designs.  ChatModel addresses the increasing complexity of reference model development, a critical bottleneck in agile hardware verification.  It leverages a multi-agent LLM system with two key groups: one that standardizes design specifications using a new domain-specific language called Design IR, and another that automates reference model generation and validation through a Hierarchical Agile Modeling (HAM) flow. HAM uses a building-block approach, iteratively generating and verifying models from module to system level.  The paper presents experimental results comparing ChatModel against other LLM-based and traditional methods, demonstrating significant improvements in efficiency, stability, and generation scale across a diverse set of designs. It also includes ablation studies to highlight the contribution of each component of ChatModel. The authors show it can generate more complex designs and speeds up model generation compared to experienced engineers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:
