### ProtoReasoning: Prototypes as the Foundation for Generalizable Reasoning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15211v1)
- **Authors**: Feng He, Zijun Chen, Xinnian Liang, Tingting Ma, Yunqi Qiu, Shuangzhi Wu, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) trained with Long Chain-of-Thought (Long CoT) reasoning have demonstrated remarkable cross-domain generalization capabilities. However, the underlying mechanisms supporting such transfer remain poorly understood. We hypothesize that cross-domain generalization arises from shared abstract reasoning prototypes -- fundamental reasoning patterns that capture the essence of problems across domains. These prototypes minimize the nuances of the representation, revealing that seemingly diverse tasks are grounded in shared reasoning structures.Based on this hypothesis, we propose ProtoReasoning, a framework that enhances the reasoning ability of LLMs by leveraging scalable and verifiable prototypical representations (Prolog for logical reasoning, PDDL for planning).ProtoReasoning features: (1) an automated prototype construction pipeline that transforms problems into corresponding prototype representations; (2) a comprehensive verification system providing reliable feedback through Prolog/PDDL interpreters; (3) the scalability to synthesize problems arbitrarily within prototype space while ensuring correctness. Extensive experiments show that ProtoReasoning achieves 4.7% improvement over baseline models on logical reasoning (Enigmata-Eval), 6.3% improvement on planning tasks, 4.0% improvement on general reasoning (MMLU) and 1.0% on mathematics (AIME24). Significantly, our ablation studies confirm that learning in prototype space also demonstrates enhanced generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language representations, validating our hypothesis that reasoning prototypes serve as the foundation for generalizable reasoning in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ProtoReasoning: Prototypes as the Foundation for Generalizable Reasoning in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "ProtoReasoning," a framework that leverages abstract reasoning prototypes to improve the generalization capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is that cross-domain generalization arises from shared, abstract reasoning patterns. ProtoReasoning involves: (1) automated conversion of problems into prototype representations (Prolog for logical reasoning, PDDL for planning); (2) a verification system using interpreters for these languages; and (3) scalable problem synthesis within the prototype space.  The authors demonstrate improvements on several benchmarks, including Enigmata-Eval, planning tasks, MMLU, and AIME24. Ablation studies suggest that training in the prototype space enhances generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using abstract, formal representations to improve LLM reasoning isn't entirely new. Neuro-symbolic approaches and program synthesis have explored similar directions. However, the *specific* framing of these representations as "prototypes" that capture fundamental reasoning patterns is a worthwhile contribution. The automated pipeline for generating verifiable training data in these prototype spaces (particularly the focus on scalability and automatic difficulty stratification) gives it some good originality. Also, the explicit attempt to bridge logical reasoning and planning under a common framework is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to address the "black box" nature of LLM reasoning and improve their generalization capabilities. If LLMs can learn to reason effectively in these simplified, abstract spaces, it could lead to more robust and reliable reasoning across different domains. The empirical results, while not earth-shattering in terms of absolute gains, are consistent across several benchmarks, supporting the effectiveness of the approach. The ablation studies offer some initial evidence that the gains are due to learning the *structure* of reasoning, rather than just memorizing patterns in natural language. The work helps us in understanding the underlying mechanism of cross-domain generalizability in large language models and tries to improve it.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Hypothesis:**  The paper presents a clearly articulated hypothesis about the importance of abstract reasoning prototypes.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** ProtoReasoning is a well-defined framework with distinct components (prototype construction, verification).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The approach is validated with experiments on multiple benchmarks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of prototype-based training.
    *   **Scalability potential**: Focus on automation for synthetic data generation and automated assessment contributes to its broader adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Prototypes:** The paper focuses primarily on Prolog and PDDL. While these are good choices for logical reasoning and planning, the framework's generalizability to other types of reasoning (e.g., common-sense reasoning, spatial reasoning) is unclear.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The performance improvements, while consistent, are relatively modest.  It would be helpful to see larger gains or a more in-depth analysis of the types of problems where ProtoReasoning is most effective.
    *   **Reliance on Strong Base Models:** The approach relies on fine-tuning strong pre-trained language models (DeepSeek-R1). It's unclear how well ProtoReasoning would work with weaker models.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical justification for why these specific prototypes are effective. While the hypothesis is plausible, more theoretical work could strengthen the claims. Specifically, the notion of "reasoning prototypes" should be formally defined.
    *   **Over-claiming**: Some of the claims in the paper are stronger than what is justified by the provided results.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach can be scaled and extended to other domains, ProtoReasoning could have a significant impact on the development of more robust and generalizable reasoning systems. It also provides a blueprint for researchers to explore similar ideas with other formal representations.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving LLM reasoning by leveraging abstract reasoning prototypes. The empirical results are promising, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the limited scope of prototypes, relatively modest performance improvements, and lack of strong theoretical justification prevent it from receiving a higher score. The scalability and automation potential of the framework are significant strengths, but further research is needed to demonstrate its broad applicability and impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM vs. SAST: A Technical Analysis on Detecting Coding Bugs of GPT4-Advanced Data Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15212v1)
- **Authors**: Madjid G. Tehrani, Eldar Sultanow, William J. Buchanan, Mahkame Houmani, Christel H. Djaha Fodja
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancements in Natural Language Processing (NLP), large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 have gained significant traction in diverse applications, including security vulnerability scanning. This paper investigates the efficacy of GPT-4 in identifying software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. Drawing from an array of security mistakes, our analysis underscores the potent capabilities of GPT-4 in LLM-enhanced vulnerability scanning. We unveiled that GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) outperforms SAST by an accuracy of 94% in detecting 32 types of exploitable vulnerabilities. This study also addresses the potential security concerns surrounding LLMs, emphasising the imperative of security by design/default and other security best practices for AI.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LLM vs. SAST: A Technical Analysis on Detecting Coding Bugs of GPT4-Advanced Data Analysis" investigates the effectiveness of GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) in identifying software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. The authors selected 32 known security pitfalls and tested GPT-4 and two SAST tools (SonarQube and Cloud Defence). They found that GPT-4 outperformed SAST in detecting these vulnerabilities with higher accuracy (94%). The paper also discusses potential security concerns related to using LLMs for security vulnerability scanning and emphasizes the importance of security by design and default, as well as other security best practices for AI. It explores the use cases where large language models (LLMs) can be integrated into software development environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Timely Topic:** The paper addresses a relevant and rapidly evolving area: the use of LLMs in cybersecurity. Given the increasing adoption of LLMs, understanding their potential for vulnerability detection is important.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology, involving a specific set of vulnerabilities and a direct comparison with SAST tools, provides a basis for quantitative analysis.  The choice of the Chi-Squared Test for Independence is appropriate for the data and research questions.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations, particularly the moving target nature of LLM and SAST technologies, and mentions the focus limited to a specific set of CWEs and SAST tools. It identifies the asymmetry that emerges when attackers also leverage LLMs.
    *   **Comprehensive coverage of Security Risks and Concerns:** The paper takes into account CISA's Security by Design. The authors delve into the attack surface of LLMs, including issues such as poisoning attacks, backdoor attacks, supply chain attacks, model stealing, prompt injection, and privacy breaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of SAST Tools:** While the paper mentions two SAST tools (SonarQube and Cloud Defence), the breadth of the SAST landscape is not fully captured. Different SAST tools have varying capabilities, so generalizing the findings to *all* SAST tools is not justified. More tools and ideally a mix of commercial and open source options would enhance the results.
    *   **Lack of Context:** The vulnerabilities assessed are not described beyond the common description provided. A specific piece of code would allow reproducibility and better insight into the results.
    *   **Generalization of LLM API.** Since it is unclear which LLM endpoint (8K context or other) was used in the experiment, it is not possible to extrapolate the experiment.
    *   **Potential Bias in Vulnerability Selection:** The choice of 32 specific vulnerabilities could introduce bias. The set might not be representative of the overall distribution of vulnerabilities found in real-world applications. The reasons for selecting those specific vulnerabilities and not others is unclear. A wider range and more diverse sets would allow better evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Rigorous Evaluation of LLM's Understanding:** Although the paper mentions that LLMs can lack interpretability, there isn't a deep dive into *why* GPT-4 is detecting certain vulnerabilities.  Is it truly understanding the underlying security principles, or is it simply recognizing patterns from its training data? This is a critical question for assessing its long-term reliability.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies in directly comparing GPT-4 to existing SAST tools in a security context, offering quantitative insights into its performance.
    *   The significance is moderate. It's a worthwhile early exploration, but the limitations outlined above prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution. The findings are encouraging but should be viewed as preliminary.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could stimulate further research into the application of LLMs for vulnerability detection.
    *   It highlights the need for caution and thorough evaluation when adopting LLMs in security-critical contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, I'm assigning a score of **6**. While the paper explores an important and timely topic with a clear methodology, several limitations prevent it from being considered a highly significant contribution. The limited scope of SAST tools, the potential bias in vulnerability selection, the shallow analysis of LLM understanding, all detract from the impact. However, the discussion of LLM security concerns and the encouragement of further research make it a valuable, if preliminary, contribution.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15215v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Fan, Yating Wang, Guandong Wang, Jie Zhai, Jingping Liu, Qi Ye, Tong Ruan
- **Abstract**: Open-ended question answering (QA) is a key task for evaluating the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Compared to closed-ended QA, it demands longer answer statements, more nuanced reasoning processes, and diverse expressions, making refined and interpretable automatic evaluation both crucial and challenging. Traditional metrics like ROUGE and BERTScore struggle to capture semantic similarities due to different patterns between model responses and reference answers. Current LLM-based evaluation approaches, such as pairwise or listwise comparisons of candidate answers, lack intuitive interpretability. While pointwise scoring of each response provides some descriptions, it fails to adapt across different question contents. Most notably, existing methods overlook the distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MinosEval}, a novel evaluation method that first distinguishes open-ended questions and then ranks candidate answers using different evaluation strategies. For factoid questions, it applies an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, while for non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking strategy. Experiments on multiple open-ended QA datasets, including self-built ones with more candidate responses to complement community resources, show that MinosEval better aligns with human annotations and offers more interpretable results.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MinosEval, a novel two-stage evaluation method for open-ended question answering (QA) that aims to improve upon existing LLM-based evaluation techniques. MinosEval distinguishes between factoid and non-factoid questions and applies tailored evaluation strategies for each type. For factoid questions, it uses an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, extracting key points from a reference answer and comparing model responses for entailment using a natural language inference (NLI) model. For non-factoid questions, it applies an instance-aware listwise ranking approach, generating silver answer instances to enhance the LLM's ranking performance. The authors conduct experiments on multiple open-ended QA datasets, demonstrating MinosEval's improved alignment with human annotations and providing more interpretable results. They also contribute two new datasets with a higher number of candidate responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in explicitly addressing the distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions in open-ended QA evaluation. While existing approaches often treat all questions uniformly, MinosEval leverages the distinct characteristics of each type to refine the evaluation process. The adaptive key-point scoring and instance-aware listwise ranking are clever implementations of this core idea. The addition of new datasets is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** Open-ended QA evaluation is crucial for assessing the capabilities of LLMs, particularly in understanding nuanced language, reasoning, and generating diverse responses. MinosEval tackles the limitations of traditional metrics and existing LLM-based methods by providing a more tailored and interpretable evaluation framework. This has the potential to advance the development of more accurate and reliable evaluation benchmarks, ultimately driving improvements in LLMs themselves.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The paper provides a clear rationale for distinguishing between factoid and non-factoid questions and designs specific evaluation strategies accordingly.
    *   **Interpretability:** MinosEval offers greater interpretability through key-point scoring and silver answer instances, making the evaluation process more transparent and easier to understand for human evaluators.
    *   **Adaptability:** The methods are adaptable to specific questions, avoiding predefined criteria that might not be universally relevant.
    *   **Automated:** The process is fully automated, reducing reliance on manual labor and improving scalability.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The authors present thorough experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of MinosEval compared to existing baselines.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** MinosEval, like many LLM-based evaluation methods, relies on the performance and biases of the underlying LLMs used for fact detection, key-point extraction, NLI, and listwise ranking. The paper acknowledges the potential for cascading errors.
    *   **Factoid/Non-Factoid Ambiguity:** While the distinction is valuable, the boundary between factoid and non-factoid questions can sometimes be ambiguous, potentially leading to misclassification errors.
    *   **Lack of Explicit Error Analysis:** While some error analysis is presented, a more detailed examination of specific failure cases and limitations of each component of MinosEval would be beneficial.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper mentions a focus on "cost-effectiveness," but detailed computational cost analysis relative to baseline methods beyond just the presented visualization would solidify the claim.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**. This paper presents a significant and well-executed contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. The explicit distinction between factoid and non-factoid QA, combined with the tailored evaluation strategies, demonstrates a clear understanding of the challenges and limitations of existing methods. The experimental results provide strong evidence of improved performance and interpretability.

The weaknesses, while acknowledged by the authors, temper the score slightly. The inherent dependency on LLMs and the potential for classification errors are important limitations to consider. A deeper dive into error analysis and computational cost, as suggested above, could further strengthen the paper and justify a higher score in future iterations.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15218v1)
- **Authors**: Dan He, Weisheng Li, Guofen Wang, Yuping Huang, Shiqiang Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF) extracts the most meaningful information from multiple source images, enabling a more comprehensive and accurate diagnosis. Achieving high-quality fusion results requires a careful balance of brightness, color, contrast, and detail; this ensures that the fused images effectively display relevant anatomical structures and reflect the functional status of the tissues. However, existing MMIF methods have limited capacity to capture detailed features during conventional training and suffer from insufficient cross-modal feature interaction, leading to suboptimal fused image quality. To address these issues, this study proposes a two-stage diffusion model-based fusion network (DM-FNet) to achieve unified MMIF. In Stage I, a diffusion process trains UNet for image reconstruction. UNet captures detailed information through progressive denoising and represents multilevel data, providing a rich set of feature representations for the subsequent fusion network. In Stage II, noisy images at various steps are input into the fusion network to enhance the model's feature recognition capability. Three key fusion modules are also integrated to process medical images from different modalities adaptively. Ultimately, the robust network structure and a hybrid loss function are integrated to harmonize the fused image's brightness, color, contrast, and detail, enhancing its quality and information density. The experimental results across various medical image types demonstrate that the proposed method performs exceptionally well regarding objective evaluation metrics. The fused image preserves appropriate brightness, a comprehensive distribution of radioactive tracers, rich textures, and clear edges. The code is available at https://github.com/HeDan-11/DM-FNet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DM-FNet, a novel two-stage diffusion model-based network for unified multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF). Stage I trains a UNet via a diffusion process for image reconstruction, capturing detailed features. Stage II uses a fusion network that incorporates three key modules: multimodal feature fusion (AMFF), multiscale feature fusion (MSFF) and feature fusion of noised images for adaptive processing of different modalities.  A hybrid loss function balances luminance, color, contrast, and detail. The authors demonstrate DM-FNet's performance on various medical image types, showing superior objective evaluation metrics and visual results compared to existing methods. The approach is extended to PC-GFP and IVIF datasets, showcasing generalizability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of medical image fusion. The use of a diffusion model for feature extraction is a notable attempt to improve detail preservation, and the two-stage approach is designed to promote generalizability across different MMIF tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Architecture:** Integrating a diffusion model into a two-stage fusion network is a promising direction, potentially addressing detail loss common in traditional methods.
    *   **Unified Framework:** The goal of a single set of parameters for different MMIF tasks (MRI-CT, MRI-PET, MRI-SPECT) is appealing, although the reported extensions suggest that fine-tuning is required for non-MMIF fusion tasks.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation with multiple objective metrics and visual comparisons, demonstrating superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.  The inclusion of PC-GFP and IVIF adds to the validation.
    *   **Adaptive Fusion:** The AMFF and MSFF modules aim to adaptively process features from different modalities, potentially alleviating the need for task-specific fusion rules.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** Diffusion models are computationally intensive. While the paper mentions that DM-FNet outperforms some methods in terms of fusion time, it is unlikely to be competitive with all lightweight approaches. The paper needs a more detailed discussion of computational cost trade-offs. The model needs more analysis regarding the impact on training time, especially considering two-stage training and different parameters for different modalities.
    *   **Limited Unified Fusion Evidence:** While aiming for unified fusion, there is the requirement for fine-tuning. In the objective analysis section, the parameter settings for various medical image combinations should be highlighted.
    *   **Marginal gains:** In many cases, the gains over the state-of-the-art are not dramatic (see Table V and Table II), suggesting that gains are incremental and not breakthrough. The limited gains make the overhead due to complexity a significant concern. More analysis is needed to demonstrate a clear use-case and trade-offs with SOTA methods.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Analysis:** The ablation study (Table IV) only examines AMFF and MSFF. An important factor for the improvement should be ablating Feature fusion of different noisy images from Stage II and investigating if this component offers any significant advantages.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The application of diffusion models in MMIF is moderately novel, although other diffusion-based fusion methods exist. The main contribution lies in the architecture of the two-stage network and the specific design of the AMFF and MSFF modules. The impact on the field is likely to be moderate, as the improvements over existing methods are incremental and come at the cost of increased complexity. While the goal of unifying the MMIF tasks is a valuable research direction, there is need for the model to support fine-tuning when used across different modalities.

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a solid understanding of the field and proposes a novel architecture. The experiments are well-designed and the results are promising. However, the incremental gains over state-of-the-art methods, the computational complexity, and the unified fusion aspects are a significant concern. More focus should be placed on unified fusion and the model's efficiency, which would greatly enhance the significance of this work.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### video-SALMONN 2: Captioning-Enhanced Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15220v1)
- **Authors**: Changli Tang, Yixuan Li, Yudong Yang, Jimin Zhuang, Guangzhi Sun, Wei Li, Zejun Ma, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Videos contain a wealth of information, and generating detailed and accurate descriptions in natural language is a key aspect of video understanding. In this paper, we present video-SALMONN 2, an advanced audio-visual large language model (LLM) with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) designed for enhanced video (with paired audio) captioning through directed preference optimisation (DPO). We propose new metrics to evaluate the completeness and accuracy of video descriptions, which are optimised using DPO. To further improve training, we propose a novel multi-round DPO (MrDPO) approach, which involves periodically updating the DPO reference model, merging and re-initialising the LoRA module as a proxy for parameter updates after each training round (1,000 steps), and incorporating guidance from ground-truth video captions to stabilise the process. Experimental results show that MrDPO significantly enhances video-SALMONN 2's captioning accuracy, reducing the captioning error rates by 28\%. The final video-SALMONN 2 model, with just 7 billion parameters, surpasses leading models such as GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks, while maintaining highly competitive performance to the state-of-the-art on widely used video question-answering benchmarks among models of similar size. Codes are available at \href{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "video-SALMONN 2: Captioning-Enhanced Audio-Visual Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces video-SALMONN 2, an audio-visual large language model (LLM) designed to improve video captioning accuracy, particularly by focusing on detail and minimizing hallucinations. It builds upon a pre-trained visual LLM and incorporates audio processing capabilities. Key contributions include: (1) a multi-round DPO (MrDPO) training approach which involves periodic updates to the DPO reference model by merging and reinitializing the LoRA proxy. (2) a novel metric to quantitatively evaluate caption completeness and accuracy of audio-visual event descriptions by evaluating missing and hallucinated events. Experiments show video-SALMONN 2, with only 7 billion parameters, outperforming larger commercial models like GPT-4o and Gemini 1.5 Pro in video captioning while maintaining competitive performance on video QA benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of enhancing video captioning through better training and metrics isn't entirely new. Prior work has tackled both aspects, but SALMONN 2 introduces a specific combination that yields strong results.
    *   The most significant novelty lies in the MrDPO (Multi-round DPO) approach. Periodically updating the reference model, merging LoRA modules, and incorporating ground-truth guidance are novel techniques for stabilizing DPO training and preventing it from getting stuck in local optima. This is a valuable contribution to the field of reinforcement learning for LLMs.
    *   The metric to evaluate caption quality based on atomic events is also a significant contribution, especially the way it's implemented through automated AI-driven feedback for both missing and hallucinated events. This is a practical approach for evaluating and improving video captions in an automated way.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper demonstrates tangible improvements in video captioning accuracy, exceeding the performance of larger commercial models with a relatively smaller model size. This is significant in terms of efficiency and accessibility. Smaller, high-performing models are easier to deploy and fine-tune.
    *   The improved metrics and the MrDPO training strategy could be valuable for other research groups working on video understanding and multimodal LLMs. The automated nature of the metrics allows researchers to test iterations more quickly.
    *   The competitive results on video QA benchmarks, while not the primary focus, indicate that the improved captioning doesn't come at the expense of other visual understanding capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong experimental results and ablation studies that support the claims made in the paper.
    *   The explanation of the MrDPO method is clear and concise, along with the justification for the design choices (e.g., reference model updates, LoRA merging, use of guided DPO).
    *   The metrics for completeness and accuracy of captions is well-defined and justified.
    *   The open-source release of the code and data contributes significantly to the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The choice of the visual base model seems arbitrary. A stronger, open-source base LLM would increase impact and allow for easier reproduction of the experiments by others.
    *   The paper admits that video QA performance does not improve over the visual base model, indicating an area for further work and implying that improving caption quality does not always improve performance on other tasks.
    *   Limited discussion on the ethical implications of improved video captioning, such as potential misuse for surveillance or manipulation. This should be considered.
    *   In MrDPO, the design choices related to LORA merging and reinitialization in each training round are not completely justified. More explanation as to why this approach yields the best results would strengthen the work.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence research in video understanding, multimodal learning, and reinforcement learning for LLMs.
    *   The MrDPO training strategy could be adopted by others working on similar tasks to stabilize and improve DPO training.
    *   The captioning metrics could become a standard for evaluating video captioning models.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to video understanding and multimodal LLMs, but is also not entirely groundbreaking. The key contributions - the MrDPO training strategy and the new metrics for caption quality - show a significant improvement in video captioning accuracy and are well-supported by experimental results. The use of a 7B parameter model to beat larger, closed-source commercial models highlights the importance of the methodological improvements made in this work. The open-source nature further increases its potential impact. However, the arbitrary choice of visual base LLM, the lack of improved video QA performance, and lack of ethical consideration prevents the paper from reaching a higher score. A more thorough justification and analysis of the various components in MrDPO is needed.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Unit Testing: A Systematic Literature Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15227v1)
- **Authors**: Quanjun Zhang, Chunrong Fang, Siqi Gu, Ye Shang, Zhenyu Chen, Liang Xiao
- **Abstract**: Unit testing is a fundamental practice in modern software engineering, with the aim of ensuring the correctness, maintainability, and reliability of individual software components. Very recently, with the advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), a rapidly growing body of research has leveraged LLMs to automate various unit testing tasks, demonstrating remarkable performance and significantly reducing manual effort. However, due to ongoing explorations in the LLM-based unit testing field, it is challenging for researchers to understand existing achievements, open challenges, and future opportunities. This paper presents the first systematic literature review on the application of LLMs in unit testing until March 2025. We analyze \numpaper{} relevant papers from the perspectives of both unit testing and LLMs. We first categorize existing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs, e.g., test generation and oracle generation. We then discuss several critical aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing research, including model usage, adaptation strategies, and hybrid approaches. We further summarize key challenges that remain unresolved and outline promising directions to guide future research in this area. Overall, our paper provides a systematic overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, helping researchers gain a comprehensive understanding of achievements and promote future research. Our artifacts are publicly available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/iSEngLab/AwesomeLLM4UT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic literature review of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in unit testing.  It analyzes 105 relevant papers published up to March 2025.  The review categorizes existing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs (e.g., test generation, oracle generation) and discusses critical aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing research, including model usage, adaptation strategies (fine-tuning, prompting), and hybrid approaches (combining LLMs with traditional techniques). The authors also identify key challenges, such as testing complex units, detecting real-world bugs, and developing unit-testing oriented LLMs, and outline promising research directions. The paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview of the research landscape to promote further research in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While other surveys exist on LLMs in software engineering or on traditional unit testing, this paper is the first systematic review that specifically focuses on the application of LLMs *in unit testing*. This specific focus is a significant contribution as it addresses a rapidly growing area that lacks a consolidated overview.  The authors explicitly acknowledge existing surveys and clearly differentiate their work.  The timeliness of the review (covering up to March 2025) is also a strength, capturing recent developments.

* **Significance:** Unit testing is a fundamental software engineering practice. The potential of LLMs to automate and improve unit testing processes is substantial. This review provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners by organizing and synthesizing existing work. Identifying challenges and outlining research directions helps to focus future efforts. The analysis of LLM adoption strategies (fine-tuning vs. prompting) is particularly useful for researchers considering which approaches to pursue. The emphasis on the limitations of using commercial LLMs in security-sensitive settings is a practical and crucial point.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic Approach:** The paper uses a well-defined and rigorous methodology for literature search and selection ("Quasi-Gold Standard" strategy, quality assessment).
    * **Comprehensive Coverage:**  Analyzing 105 papers offers a broad perspective on the topic.
    * **Clear Categorization and Taxonomy:** The categorization of unit testing tasks and LLM utilization strategies is well-structured and aids understanding.
    * **Critical Analysis:** The paper doesn't simply present the literature; it critically evaluates the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches, and identifies key challenges.
    * **Identification of Future Research Directions:** The discussion of opportunities points to areas ripe for further investigation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Depth in Specific Areas:**  Due to the breadth of the review, the discussion of specific techniques within individual unit testing tasks (e.g., test generation) may lack depth. Readers interested in a specific area would need to consult the original papers.
    * **Potential for Bias:**  Although the methodology is rigorous, the authors' choices regarding inclusion/exclusion criteria and quality assessment are inherently subjective.
    * **Rapid Evolution:** The field is rapidly evolving. While the review is current as of March 2025, new research will inevitably emerge, potentially rendering some aspects of the review outdated relatively quickly. This is an inherent limitation of all literature reviews in fast-paced fields.

* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the unit testing research community. By providing a structured overview and identifying key challenges and opportunities, it can help researchers to:
    * Gain a comprehensive understanding of the current state of the field.
    * Identify promising research directions.
    * Avoid duplication of effort.
    * Build upon existing work more effectively.

The open availability of artifacts (GitHub repository) enhances the paper's accessibility and impact.

**Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a timely and systematic review of the application of LLMs to unit testing. Its comprehensive coverage, clear categorization, critical analysis, and identification of future research directions make it a valuable resource for both researchers and practitioners. While the breadth of the review limits depth in some areas, and the field is rapidly evolving, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses.

### Lost in Variation? Evaluating NLI Performance in Basque and Spanish Geographical Variants
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15239v1)
- **Authors**: Jaione Bengoetxea, Itziar Gonzalez-Dios, Rodrigo Agerri
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we evaluate the capacity of current language technologies to understand Basque and Spanish language varieties. We use Natural Language Inference (NLI) as a pivot task and introduce a novel, manually-curated parallel dataset in Basque and Spanish, along with their respective variants. Our empirical analysis of crosslingual and in-context learning experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based Large Language Models (LLMs) shows a performance drop when handling linguistic variation, especially in Basque. Error analysis suggests that this decline is not due to lexical overlap, but rather to the linguistic variation itself. Further ablation experiments indicate that encoder-only models particularly struggle with Western Basque, which aligns with linguistic theory that identifies peripheral dialects (e.g., Western) as more distant from the standard. All data and code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how well current natural language processing (NLP) technologies, specifically large language models (LLMs), handle language variation in Basque and Spanish. The authors created a new, manually-curated dataset, XNLIvar, consisting of Natural Language Inference (NLI) examples in standard Basque and Spanish, alongside their geographical variants. They conducted crosslingual and in-context learning experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based LLMs. Their results indicate a significant performance drop when dealing with linguistic variation, particularly in Basque. Further analysis suggests this isn't primarily due to lexical overlap but rather inherent linguistic differences. Ablation experiments highlight that encoder-only models struggle particularly with Western Basque, aligning with linguistic theories about peripheral dialects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper introduces a novel, manually-curated dataset, XNLIvar, which is a significant contribution in itself. Manually curated datasets specifically addressing language variation, especially for a low-resource language like Basque, are rare. The focus on geographical variations using NLI as a pivot task provides a structured evaluation framework.  The work expands on existing NLI datasets like XNLI by including variations and native examples.

**Significance:**

The finding that LLMs perform worse when processing language variations, particularly in Basque, is significant. It reinforces the understanding that current NLP technology is still biased towards standard languages and struggles with linguistic diversity. The analysis of specific error types (e.g., related to orthographic changes in Spanish, and Western Basque dialect) provides valuable insights for future research. The implications of the work are relevant to addressing potential language-based discrimination in NLP systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Dataset Creation:** The creation and public availability of XNLIvar are the primary strength. Manually curated datasets for language variation are rare, and this resource fills a gap, particularly for Basque.
*   **Rigorous Experiments:** The paper employs a thorough experimental design, exploring both discriminative and generative models, along with different learning paradigms (transfer learning, in-context learning).
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The error analysis delves into the reasons for performance drops, examining lexical overlap, dialect distances, and orthographic variations. This level of detail is essential for guiding future research.
*   **Theoretical Grounding:** The discussion connecting the performance drop with linguistic theory (peripheral dialects, historical divergence) adds depth to the analysis.
*   **Clear Reporting:** The paper clearly presents the experimental setup, results, and analysis.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scale of Variation:** While the dataset is valuable, the number of speakers contributing to the variations (12 for Basque, 6 for Spanish) is relatively small. This limits the scope of the captured variation and might not fully represent the diversity within those languages.
*   **Speaker Background:** The fact that all speakers contributing variations have linguistic or NLP backgrounds is a limitation. It introduces a potential bias, as laypeople might produce different types of variations.
*   **Geographic Focus:**  While geography is a key aspect of language variation, other forms of variation (social, stylistic) are not explicitly addressed.  The NLI task, while broad, doesn't target specific nuances of regional language use.
*   **Underrepresentation of Navarrese dialect:** As stated in the paper, there are only 7% of samples representative of the Navarrese dialect. Therefore, any conclusions drawn about its accuracy might be skewed due to the lack of data to draw conclusions from.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper's findings are likely to encourage further research into:

*   Developing NLP models that are more robust to language variation.
*   Creating more diverse and representative language resources.
*   Investigating the social implications of biased NLP systems.
*   Applying similar methodologies to other low-resource languages and their variations.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the XNLIvar dataset and the detailed analysis of LLM performance on geographical language variations. The thorough experimental methodology and connection to linguistic theory contribute significantly to its value. However, the limited scale of variation, speaker background, and geographic focus are notable weaknesses. While impactful, the paper's significance is limited by these factors. The paper presents a solid contribution, which can be further built upon and generalized in future research. The score reflects its solid contributions, balanced with certain limitations. The core finding is valuable, but broader validation on larger, more diverse datasets is needed.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15241v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Fan, Zhang Qi, Xing Wenqian, Liu Chang, Liu Liu
- **Abstract**: This article addresses domain knowledge gaps in general large language models for historical text analysis in the context of computational humanities and AIGC technology. We propose the Graph RAG framework, combining chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a The First Four Histories character relationship dataset with minimal manual annotation. This dataset supports automated historical knowledge extraction, reducing labor costs. In the graph-augmented generation phase, we introduce a collaborative mechanism between knowledge graphs and retrieval-augmented generation, improving the alignment of general models with historical knowledge. Experiments show that the domain-specific model Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B, with Simplified Chinese input and chain-of-thought prompting, achieves optimal performance in relation extraction (F1 = 0.68). The DeepSeek model integrated with GraphRAG improves F1 by 11% (0.08-0.19) on the open-domain C-CLUE relation extraction dataset, surpassing the F1 value of Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B (0.12), effectively alleviating hallucinations phenomenon, and improving interpretability. This framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of knowledge gaps in general large language models (LLMs) for historical text analysis. It proposes a Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) framework that combines chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a character relationship dataset from "The First Four Histories".  The approach aims to improve the alignment of general LLMs with historical knowledge, reduce "hallucinations," and enhance interpretability.  Experiments demonstrate improved performance in relation extraction using a domain-specific model, Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B, and show that the GraphRAG framework enhances the DeepSeek model's performance on a relation extraction dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of GraphRAG to the specific domain of historical text analysis and the creation of a labeled dataset for "The First Four Histories". While GraphRAG itself is not entirely novel, its adaptation and optimization for this niche is. The combination of chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction, and process supervision also contributes to the novelty of the approach. Further, addressing the specific issues of historical text analysis (e.g., entity ambiguity, lack of labeled data, sensitivity to Simplified vs. Traditional Chinese) increases its appeal. The approach of combining domain-specific LLMs, and more general models within a GraphRAG structure to take advantage of both is well conceived.

*   **Significance:** The research has the potential to significantly impact the field of computational humanities and digital preservation of historical texts. The ability to automate knowledge extraction from ancient texts with minimal manual annotation is a crucial step towards building accessible historical knowledge services. The demonstration that GraphRAG can enhance general LLMs' performance in this domain is significant. It effectively addresses the limitations of LLMs lacking specialized historical context. Providing more interpretable, and less hallucinated, answers in the context of an LLM in a low-resource setting is a positive contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of knowledge gaps in LLMs for historical text analysis.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The GraphRAG framework addresses multiple aspects of the problem, including dataset creation, knowledge graph construction, and retrieval-augmented generation.
    *   **Experimental validation:** The paper presents thorough experimental results, comparing different models and prompting strategies.
    *   **Clear exposition:** The paper is well-written and explains the methodology and results in a clear and concise manner.
    *   The work addresses a real problem (hallucinations in LLMs) in a very domain-specific and useful way.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited corpus:** The corpus is confined to "The First Four Histories." Expanding it to other classical texts would enhance the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Dependence on domain-specific models:** The reliance on Xunzi models may limit the accessibility of the approach to researchers who do not have access to or resources to train such models.
    *   **Computational resources:** The paper acknowledges the computational resource requirements of fine-tuning LLMs. Exploring model compression techniques could improve accessibility.
    *   The novelty of the core components is somewhat limited, and the degree to which the specific combination is novel isn't clearly articulated and compared to other approaches in the RAG literature. A better comparison to other recent RAG architectures is warranted.
    *   More discussion of the specific characteristics of historical texts that make this application of GraphRAG particularly useful would add to its significance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several areas:

    *   Development of automated knowledge extraction methods for historical texts.
    *   Application of GraphRAG to other domains with limited labeled data.
    *   Creation of accessible historical knowledge services based on LLMs.
    *   Enhancing LLM interpretability and reducing hallucinations in knowledge-intensive tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by effectively applying GraphRAG to the challenging problem of historical text analysis. The creation of a specific dataset, combined with improvements in knowledge extraction using domain-specific models, positions it well for application in low-resource scenarios. While many elements are not wholly novel, the combination of techniques and targeted optimizations for the characteristics of historical texts results in a framework which is highly relevant to the problem domain. Further evaluation and comparison to other state-of-the-art RAG architectures would strengthen its position, as would addressing the potential generalizability concerns posed by the use of the "The First Four Histories" corpus. Overall, it's a well-executed and significant contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Post-hoc Dataset Inference with Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15271v1)
- **Authors**: Bihe Zhao, Pratyush Maini, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: The remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) can be mainly attributed to their massive training datasets, which are often scraped from the internet without respecting data owners' intellectual property rights. Dataset Inference (DI) offers a potential remedy by identifying whether a suspect dataset was used in training, thereby enabling data owners to verify unauthorized use. However, existing DI methods require a private set-known to be absent from training-that closely matches the compromised dataset's distribution. Such in-distribution, held-out data is rarely available in practice, severely limiting the applicability of DI. In this work, we address this challenge by synthetically generating the required held-out set. Our approach tackles two key obstacles: (1) creating high-quality, diverse synthetic data that accurately reflects the original distribution, which we achieve via a data generator trained on a carefully designed suffix-based completion task, and (2) bridging likelihood gaps between real and synthetic data, which is realized through post-hoc calibration. Extensive experiments on diverse text datasets show that using our generated data as a held-out set enables DI to detect the original training sets with high confidence, while maintaining a low false positive rate. This result empowers copyright owners to make legitimate claims on data usage and demonstrates our method's reliability for real-world litigations. Our code is available at https://github.com/sprintml/PostHocDatasetInference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the practical limitation of Dataset Inference (DI) methods, which require an in-distribution held-out set to determine if a suspect dataset was used to train a Large Language Model (LLM). The authors propose a method to synthetically generate such a held-out set using a fine-tuned language model on a suffix completion task, followed by a post-hoc calibration step to mitigate distribution shifts between the real and synthetic data. The calibration uses a dual-classifier approach, one focused on text only and the other incorporating membership inference features, to disentangle the membership signal from distributional artifacts. Experiments on diverse datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach in detecting unauthorized data usage while maintaining a low false positive rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of generating a synthetic held-out set for DI is novel and tackles a significant obstacle in the widespread adoption of DI. While the use of synthetic data and calibration techniques are not entirely new in machine learning, their application in this specific context, along with the dual classifier calibration strategy, constitutes a significant advance. The suffix completion task for training the generator appears tailored well for this application.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a pressing concern regarding intellectual property rights and transparency in the use of training data for LLMs. A reliable DI method, even with synthetic held-out data, can empower data owners to verify unauthorized use of their content and potentially pursue legal recourse. This has the potential to influence how LLMs are trained and deployed, encouraging more ethical and legally compliant practices. The work could significantly benefit data creators by providing a usable mechanism to check for unauthorized usage of their data in AI model training, thereby advancing data privacy and data protection measures.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper identifies a clear and important problem.
    *   The proposed solution is technically sound and well-motivated.
    *   The use of a suffix-completion task seems well-suited for the generation task.
    *   The dual-classifier calibration is a clever method for mitigating distribution shifts.
    *   The experiments are extensive and cover a variety of datasets.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The synthetic data generation, while improved by suffix completion, still may not perfectly capture the complexity and nuances of real-world data. The paper's reliance on specific architectures (e.g., GPT2 for the classifiers, LoRA for the generator) might limit its generalizability to other model types or tasks.
    *   The evaluation metrics, while appropriate, could be strengthened by including more real-world scenarios or attack settings, to demonstrate the robustness of the defense.
    * The method still requires significant computational resources for training the generator and classifiers, which could be a barrier for some users.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has a strong potential to impact the field by making DI more practical and accessible. It can contribute to the ongoing debate about copyright and data governance in AI, and potentially shape future legal frameworks. The proposed framework is designed with a practical intention, aimed at facilitating reliable post-hoc dataset inference for copyright ownership determination, therefore having high potential influence in real-world copyright disputes.
*   **Critical Observations:** The paper is strong, yet I observe there are still subtle distribution shifts that may cause issues. Despite the dual classification, there is a potential to not catch a very skilled copyright infringer who injects small, but subtle distribution shifts. The method also may be complex for data owners to implement and use and requires knowledge of machine learning skills, potentially limiting its accessibility.

**Justification:**

The paper offers a significant step towards making DI a viable tool for copyright protection. The proposed approach is well-designed, technically sound, and supported by thorough experiments. While challenges remain in perfectly replicating real-world data distributions and addressing computational costs, the paper represents a substantial contribution to the field and has the potential to empower data owners in the age of LLMs. Therefore, despite the limitations mentioned above, the positive impact on the reliability and efficiency of copyright litigations is high.

### Human Motion Capture from Loose and Sparse Inertial Sensors with Garment-aware Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15290v1)
- **Authors**: Andela Ilic, Jiaxi Jiang, Paul Streli, Xintong Liu, Christian Holz
- **Abstract**: Motion capture using sparse inertial sensors has shown great promise due to its portability and lack of occlusion issues compared to camera-based tracking. Existing approaches typically assume that IMU sensors are tightly attached to the human body. However, this assumption often does not hold in real-world scenarios. In this paper, we present a new task of full-body human pose estimation using sparse, loosely attached IMU sensors. To solve this task, we simulate IMU recordings from an existing garment-aware human motion dataset. We developed transformer-based diffusion models to synthesize loose IMU data and estimate human poses based on this challenging loose IMU data. In addition, we show that incorporating garment-related parameters while training the model on simulated loose data effectively maintains expressiveness and enhances the ability to capture variations introduced by looser or tighter garments. Experiments show that our proposed diffusion methods trained on simulated and synthetic data outperformed the state-of-the-art methods quantitatively and qualitatively, opening up a promising direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of full-body human pose estimation from sparse inertial measurement units (IMUs) that are *loosely* attached to clothing.  This is a more realistic and comfortable scenario than the traditional assumption of tightly strapped IMUs. The authors propose a method based on conditional diffusion models, trained on a combination of simulated, synthetic, and real-world data. A key contribution is the incorporation of garment-related parameters (height, BMI, and a shape parameter) to improve the model's robustness to variations in clothing looseness and fit. They demonstrate that their approach outperforms state-of-the-art methods, particularly in scenarios with looser garments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   **Positive:** The focus on loosely attached IMUs for full-body pose estimation is a valuable contribution, addressing a more practical and user-friendly setup than previous works that primarily focused on tightly attached IMUs. Integrating garment parameters into the pose estimation process to account for clothing effects is also a novel and insightful approach. Leveraging diffusion models for this task, especially with the proposed training strategy, is a solid contribution. Addressing the gap in real-world data by synthetically generating data tailored to more relaxed garment conditions represents a significant step forward.
    *   **Negative:** While the combination of diffusion models and garment parameters is new, the individual components are well-established. The overall architecture is fairly standard for diffusion models, with a Transformer-based autoencoder. The idea of simulating IMU data is not new, but the specific method tailored for loose garments adds incremental novelty. LIP [Zuo et al., 2024] already explores the loose IMU setup. This paper presents an extension of that idea by incorporating garment-related parameters and tackling the full-body pose.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Positive:**  The paper's significance lies in its practical applicability. Loosely attached IMUs make motion capture more accessible and comfortable, potentially broadening its use in various applications (healthcare, sports, AR/VR).  The improved accuracy compared to existing methods, particularly in realistic clothing scenarios, is a meaningful advancement.
    *   **Negative:** The paper may not significantly revolutionize the core of pose estimation research. The gains demonstrated, while valuable, may not be viewed as transformative by all researchers in the field.  The results could be considered incremental rather than a radical breakthrough.  The datasets are a mix of existing and simulated, raising questions about the generalization to completely unseen real-world data with different garment types. The focus on male models for garment simulations may limit the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Clarity and Evaluation:**
    *   **Positive:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly presents the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results. The experimental section is thorough, with comparisons to existing methods and ablation studies. The inclusion of qualitative results adds to the understanding of the model's behavior. The ablation studies are quite strong as they attempt to isolate the contributions of the different components proposed.
    *   **Negative:** While the data simulation component is novel, the garment parameters may have a limited range. Experiments could be performed with a much broader variety of clothing styles.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research in IMU-based motion capture, particularly towards more practical and user-friendly setups. The garment-aware modeling approach could inspire further investigation into incorporating contextual information for improved pose estimation. The multi-source training strategy, combining simulated, synthetic, and real data, is a valuable technique that could be adopted by other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of IMU-based motion capture by addressing the practical issue of loosely attached sensors and incorporating garment-related parameters. The results demonstrate improved accuracy compared to existing methods, and the multi-source training strategy is a valuable contribution. However, the overall architecture is relatively standard, and the gains, while significant, may be considered incremental rather than a radical breakthrough. While the focus on loose-fitting clothing is crucial for practicality, the dataset and clothing parameter limitations suggest room for improvement.

### MEGC2025: Micro-Expression Grand Challenge on Spot Then Recognize and Visual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15298v1)
- **Authors**: Xinqi Fan, Jingting Li, John See, Moi Hoon Yap, Wen-Huang Cheng, Xiaobai Li, Xiaopeng Hong, Su-Jing Wang, Adrian K. Davision
- **Abstract**: Facial micro-expressions (MEs) are involuntary movements of the face that occur spontaneously when a person experiences an emotion but attempts to suppress or repress the facial expression, typically found in a high-stakes environment. In recent years, substantial advancements have been made in the areas of ME recognition, spotting, and generation. However, conventional approaches that treat spotting and recognition as separate tasks are suboptimal, particularly for analyzing long-duration videos in realistic settings. Concurrently, the emergence of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and large vision-language models (LVLMs) offers promising new avenues for enhancing ME analysis through their powerful multimodal reasoning capabilities. The ME grand challenge (MEGC) 2025 introduces two tasks that reflect these evolving research directions: (1) ME spot-then-recognize (ME-STR), which integrates ME spotting and subsequent recognition in a unified sequential pipeline; and (2) ME visual question answering (ME-VQA), which explores ME understanding through visual question answering, leveraging MLLMs or LVLMs to address diverse question types related to MEs. All participating algorithms are required to run on this test set and submit their results on a leaderboard. More details are available at https://megc2025.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the MEGC2025, the 8th Micro-Expression Grand Challenge, held in conjunction with ACM Multimedia 2025.  The challenge comprises two tasks: (1) ME spot-then-recognize (ME-STR), which integrates ME spotting and recognition in a unified pipeline; and (2) ME visual question answering (ME-VQA), which uses multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and large vision-language models (LVLMs) to understand and reason about MEs through visual question answering. Baseline methods, datasets, and evaluation metrics for both tasks are described.  For ME-STR, a MEAN network is used as a baseline.  For ME-VQA, the Qwen2.5VL-3B model is used with both zero-shot and fine-tuned performance reported using different types of video inputs (equally spaced frames, onset/apex/offset frames, and optical flow). The paper highlights the continued challenges in ME spotting and the potential of MLLMs/LVLMs for ME understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the introduction of the ME-VQA task.  While ME-STR is a continuation from a previous challenge, the ME-VQA task brings a fresh perspective by leveraging the power of large language and vision-language models to understand and reason about micro-expressions in a more human-interpretable way. The formulation of ME analysis as a visual question answering problem opens up new avenues for research.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in the push for more realistic and practical ME analysis. Treating spotting and recognition as separate tasks is often unrealistic, and the ME-STR task addresses this by integrating the two.  The ME-VQA task is significant because it aims to go beyond simple classification and towards deeper understanding and interpretation of MEs, bringing the field closer to real-world applications where understanding the context and nuances of emotions is crucial.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the two tasks and provides baseline methods.
    *   It clearly explains the motivation for both tasks, highlighting the limitations of previous approaches.
    *   The use of MLLMs/LVLMs for ME-VQA is a timely and promising direction.
    *   The provision of a curated ME-VQA dataset is valuable for the research community.
    *   The choice of evaluation metrics is appropriate for both tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The baseline results for ME-STR are quite low, indicating that the task remains very challenging. This limits the current impact of ME-STR task.
    *   The baseline results for ME-VQA also leave room for significant improvement.
    *   The description of the ME-VQA dataset creation is brief. More details on the QA pairs and the curation process would be beneficial.
    *   The paper mainly focuses on the tasks and baselines but does not explore or propose novel techniques to address challenges in the two tasks.

*   **Impact:** The challenge will likely encourage researchers to explore novel approaches to ME spotting, recognition, and visual understanding. The ME-VQA task has the potential to drive innovation in the use of MLLMs/LVLMs for affective computing. However, the current low baseline results suggest that significant work remains to be done. The availability of the curated ME-VQA dataset will be helpful for the community.

Considering the introduction of a novel task (ME-VQA), the extension of ME-STR, and the use of modern MLLMs/LVLMs, the paper represents a moderate advancement for the field. The relatively low baseline performance currently limits the immediate impact.
