- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### SecFwT: Efficient Privacy-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models Using Forward-Only Passes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15307v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Luo, Zhuo Zhang, Yehong Zhang, Shiyu Liu, Ye Dong, Xun Zhou, Hui Wang, Yue Yu, Zenglin Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have transformed numerous fields, yet their adaptation to specialized tasks in privacy-sensitive domains, such as healthcare and finance, is constrained by the scarcity of accessible training data due to stringent privacy requirements. Secure multi-party computation (MPC)-based privacy-preserving machine learning offers a powerful approach to protect both model parameters and user data, but its application to LLMs has been largely limited to inference, as fine-tuning introduces significant computational challenges, particularly in privacy-preserving backward propagation and optimizer operations. This paper identifies two primary obstacles to MPC-based privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs: (1) the substantial computational overhead of backward and optimizer processes, and (2) the inefficiency of softmax-based attention mechanisms in MPC settings. To address these challenges, we propose SecFwT, the first MPC-based framework designed for efficient, privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning. SecFwT introduces a forward-only tuning paradigm to eliminate backward and optimizer computations and employs MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention to approximate softmax attention, significantly reducing costly non-linear operations and computational complexity. Experimental results demonstrate that SecFwT delivers substantial improvements in efficiency and privacy preservation, enabling scalable and secure fine-tuning of LLMs for privacy-critical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SECFWT: Efficient Privacy-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models Using Forward-Only Passes" introduces a new framework for privacy-preserving fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) within a Secure Multi-Party Computation (MPC) environment.  It addresses the computational challenges associated with fine-tuning LLMs in MPC, specifically the overhead of backward propagation and optimizer operations, and the inefficiency of softmax-based attention. SECFWT utilizes a forward-only tuning paradigm based on Gradient-Free Optimization (GFO) to eliminate backward passes and leverages MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention (RFA) to approximate softmax, reducing computational complexity. The authors demonstrate through experiments that SECFWT improves efficiency and privacy preservation, enabling scalable and secure fine-tuning of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs.  While MPC-based privacy-preserving machine learning is established, its application to fine-tuning *large* LLMs is relatively unexplored due to the prohibitive computational costs. The key novelty lies in the combined approach: 1) Forward-Only Tuning (FoT) using GFO to bypass backpropagation's computational expense in an MPC context, and 2) the strategic adoption and adaptation of Random Feature Attention (RFA) for efficient MPC computation. The customization of RFA to be MPC-friendly, specifically addressing the cosine function's inefficiency through trigonometric identities, adds to the paper's innovative elements.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from the growing need for privacy-preserving techniques in LLM applications involving sensitive data (healthcare, finance).  The ability to fine-tune LLMs without exposing either the data or the model parameters is a crucial step toward responsible AI deployment. The paper attempts to overcome a key bottleneck in applying MPC to a practical LLM fine-tuning scenario. If the performance degradation (3%) is genuinely acceptable for many sensitive applications, then the resulting 10x speedup, coupled with a 14x reduction in communication cost, would mark a substantial advancement. The claim to being the *first* comprehensive MPC-based framework is a bold one but seems supported by the reviewed literature. The inclusion of code for public availability is an asset.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly identifies a relevant and significant problem: MPC-based LLM fine-tuning's computational burden.
    *   Proposes a coherent and well-justified solution combining GFO-based FoT and MPC-friendly RFA.
    *   Provides experimental evidence demonstrating efficiency improvements in time and communication cost.
    *   Addresses practical concerns related to privacy leakage and nonlinearity in MPC environments.
    *   Provides a security analysis grounded in composable security principles and semi-honest adversary models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is primarily on RoBERTa-large. While RoBERTa-large is a substantial model, extending to larger models with billions or hundreds of billions of parameters is essential to truly demonstrate the framework's scalability.
    *   The performance degradation of ~3% on GLUE needs careful consideration.  While acceptable in some scenarios, it may be a limiting factor in high-accuracy applications. A deeper exploration of the trade-offs between efficiency and accuracy is warranted.
    *   The analysis of WAN performance and comparison to the LAN setting provides insight; however, the WAN bandwidths considered may not represent the highest-end capabilities of modern infrastructure.
    *   The paper admits in the Limitation section (Section 5.1) that it does not yet handle secure handling of other potentially costly operations inside an LLM architecture, like GeLU and LayerNorm. These future efforts should focus on these limitations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research in privacy-preserving LLM training and applications.  It presents a practical framework that could be adopted and extended by others, potentially leading to the development of more efficient and robust privacy-preserving methods.  It could encourage exploration of alternative attention mechanisms and optimization techniques suitable for MPC settings. The open-sourcing of the code will promote adoption and modification of the techniques described in the paper.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** SECFWT introduces a demonstrably novel and significant contribution to the field by providing a *comprehensive* framework for MPC-based LLM *fine-tuning*, and not just inference. The combination of GFO-based FoT and MPC-friendly RFA is innovative and provides practical efficiency gains while maintaining acceptable performance degradation. The paper's strengths lie in its clear problem definition, well-justified solutions, and experimental validation.  However, the limited scope of the experimental evaluation (RoBERTa-large), the performance degradation, the bandwidth of the WAN settings, and the admitted limitations in handling other expensive operations (e.g., GeLU, LayerNorm) temper the assessment of its impact. Further work is needed to extend the framework to larger models and datasets, address the performance accuracy trade-off, consider more expansive WAN conditions, and to address previously raised limitations. Therefore, the paper is well above average but falls short of being a game-changer that would warrant a 9 or 10. The influence will depend on adoption and extension.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### One-shot Face Sketch Synthesis in the Wild via Generative Diffusion Prior and Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15312v1)
- **Authors**: Han Wu, Junyao Li, Kangbo Zhao, Sen Zhang, Yukai Shi, Liang Lin
- **Abstract**: Face sketch synthesis is a technique aimed at converting face photos into sketches. Existing face sketch synthesis research mainly relies on training with numerous photo-sketch sample pairs from existing datasets. However, these large-scale discriminative learning methods will have to face problems such as data scarcity and high human labor costs. Once the training data becomes scarce, their generative performance significantly degrades. In this paper, we propose a one-shot face sketch synthesis method based on diffusion models. We optimize text instructions on a diffusion model using face photo-sketch image pairs. Then, the instructions derived through gradient-based optimization are used for inference. To simulate real-world scenarios more accurately and evaluate method effectiveness more comprehensively, we introduce a new benchmark named One-shot Face Sketch Dataset (OS-Sketch). The benchmark consists of 400 pairs of face photo-sketch images, including sketches with different styles and photos with different backgrounds, ages, sexes, expressions, illumination, etc. For a solid out-of-distribution evaluation, we select only one pair of images for training at each time, with the rest used for inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed method can convert various photos into realistic and highly consistent sketches in a one-shot context. Compared to other methods, our approach offers greater convenience and broader applicability. The dataset will be available at: https://github.com/HanWu3125/OS-Sketch
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "One-shot Face Sketch Synthesis in the Wild via Generative Diffusion Prior and Instruction Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel one-shot face sketch synthesis method that aims to convert face photos into sketches using only a single photo-sketch pair for training. It leverages diffusion models and instruction tuning, optimizing text instructions on the diffusion model using the available photo-sketch pair. These optimized instructions are then used for inference on other face photos. To improve evaluation, the authors also introduce a new benchmark dataset, OS-Sketch, consisting of 400 diverse photo-sketch pairs exhibiting variations in style, background, age, expression, illumination, and sex. The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can generate realistic and consistent sketches from various photos within the one-shot learning context. The authors emphasize the convenience and broad applicability of their approach compared to existing methods that rely on large datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in a few key aspects:
    * **One-shot learning for face sketch synthesis:**  Prior research largely relies on training with substantial datasets. The proposed method addresses the data scarcity problem by only using one photo-sketch pair, a significant departure from traditional approaches.
    * **Instruction tuning with diffusion models:** The use of instruction tuning, particularly for guiding diffusion models in this specific task, appears to be novel. It addresses a core challenge: how to make diffusion models, typically reliant on extensive data or fragile text prompts, work with limited data in a visually nuanced task.
    * **OS-Sketch Dataset:**  The creation of the OS-Sketch dataset appears valuable, providing a more diverse and challenging benchmark for face sketch synthesis, particularly in the one-shot context. It directly addresses the recognized limitation in existing datasets.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to:
    * **Reduce data and labor costs:**  The one-shot learning paradigm can significantly reduce the reliance on large, manually curated datasets, which are expensive to acquire and annotate. This makes face sketch synthesis more accessible.
    * **Improve generalization:** The model appears to demonstrate improved generalization to out-of-distribution data.
    * **Advance diffusion models:**  The paper contributes to the broader understanding and application of diffusion models, showing how they can be effectively adapted for tasks where data is scarce and visual fidelity is crucial.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses a significant problem:**  Data scarcity is a major obstacle in many computer vision tasks. The paper directly tackles this challenge in the context of face sketch synthesis.
    * **Well-defined methodology:** The methodology is clear, and the paper provides sufficient detail for reproducibility.
    * **Strong experimental results:**  Both qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, outperforming several existing state-of-the-art approaches.
    * **New benchmark dataset:**  The OS-Sketch dataset contributes significantly by enabling more comprehensive evaluation of face sketch synthesis methods, particularly those operating in one-shot or few-shot scenarios.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on pre-trained models:**  The method relies on a pre-trained diffusion model and CLIP, which may limit its flexibility. Performance is contingent on the quality and suitability of these pre-trained components.  The paper doesn't delve deeply into the impact of different pre-trained models.
    * **Text prompt limitations:** Even with instruction tuning, the reliance on text prompts from the diffusion model can pose challenges.  The prompts may be imprecise, especially when representing nuanced artistic styles or complex facial features. This is acknowledged in the conclusion but warrants further investigation.
    * **Limited handling of extreme variations:** The paper shows good results across a range of variations.  However, it's unclear how robust the method is to extremely rare cases (e.g., unusual lighting, very stylized sketches).

* **Influence:** The paper could influence future research in the following ways:
    * **Encourage one-shot learning approaches:**  It could motivate researchers to explore one-shot or few-shot learning techniques for other computer vision tasks where data is limited.
    * **Promote instruction tuning:**  It could inspire further investigation of instruction tuning strategies to better control diffusion models, especially for visually nuanced tasks.
    * **Utilize the OS-Sketch dataset:** The dataset will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating face sketch synthesis methods, especially in one-shot learning scenarios.

* **Score Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with clear novelty and potential for significant impact. It effectively tackles the problem of data scarcity in face sketch synthesis through a creative and well-executed methodology. While the approach relies on pre-trained models and has some limitations regarding text prompts and extreme variations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The introduction of the OS-Sketch dataset enhances the significance of the paper. I believe it warrants a score of 7.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### When and How Unlabeled Data Provably Improve In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15329v1)
- **Authors**: Yingcong Li, Xiangyu Chang, Muti Kara, Xiaofeng Liu, Amit Roy-Chowdhury, Samet Oymak
- **Abstract**: Recent research shows that in-context learning (ICL) can be effective even when demonstrations have missing or incorrect labels. To shed light on this capability, we examine a canonical setting where the demonstrations are drawn according to a binary Gaussian mixture model (GMM) and a certain fraction of the demonstrations have missing labels. We provide a comprehensive theoretical study to show that: (1) The loss landscape of one-layer linear attention models recover the optimal fully-supervised estimator but completely fail to exploit unlabeled data; (2) In contrast, multilayer or looped transformers can effectively leverage unlabeled data by implicitly constructing estimators of the form $\sum_{i\ge 0} a_i (X^\top X)^iX^\top y$ with $X$ and $y$ denoting features and partially-observed labels (with missing entries set to zero). We characterize the class of polynomials that can be expressed as a function of depth and draw connections to Expectation Maximization, an iterative pseudo-labeling algorithm commonly used in semi-supervised learning. Importantly, the leading polynomial power is exponential in depth, so mild amount of depth/looping suffices. As an application of theory, we propose looping off-the-shelf tabular foundation models to enhance their semi-supervision capabilities. Extensive evaluations on real-world datasets show that our method significantly improves the semisupervised tabular learning performance over the standard single pass inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates when and how unlabeled data can improve in-context learning (ICL) in transformers, particularly in a semi-supervised setting with Gaussian mixture models (GMMs). The authors theoretically analyze linear attention models of varying depths, demonstrating that single-layer linear attention fails to leverage unlabeled data, while deeper or looped transformers can emulate semi-supervised learning algorithms by constructing polynomial estimators. They connect these findings to Expectation Maximization (EM) and propose looping off-the-shelf tabular foundation models to enhance their semi-supervision capabilities. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets validate that their method improves semi-supervised tabular learning performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Novelty:** The paper provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding the role of depth in transformers for semi-supervised ICL. The analysis of single-layer vs. multi-layer attention and their connection to polynomial estimators is novel and insightful. The link to EM is well-established.

    *   **Practical Significance:** The proposed looping strategy for tabular foundation models (TabFMs) offers a practical way to improve their performance in scenarios with limited labeled data. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements on real-world datasets, underscoring the method's practical value.

    *   **Connections to SSL:** The work provides the first theoretical characterization of semi-supervised in-context learning and connects the transformer's ability to propagate XTX into deeper layers, demonstrating that it can act as an implicit semi-supervised learning paradigm.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Architecture:** The theoretical analysis primarily focuses on linear attention models, which are a simplified version of full transformer architectures. While this allows for tractability, it limits the generalizability of the results to more complex models, and the use of softmax attention is limited. The findings may not fully capture the behavior of transformers in practice, as other attention mechanisms and architectural components could influence the results.

    *   **Specific Data Distribution:** The theoretical analysis is grounded in the GMM assumption, which may not hold for all real-world datasets. The extent to which the theoretical insights translate to other data distributions needs further investigation.

    *   **Looping Strategy Specificity:** The proposed looping strategy is tailored to TabFMs. While it is empirically effective, the adaptation of this strategy to other models and modalities may not be straightforward.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to a growing body of work on the theoretical understanding of transformers and ICL, addressing the gap in knowledge regarding the role of unlabeled data.

    *   It provides a practical approach to improving the performance of TabFMs, which have gained attention for their ability to handle small data sets.

    *   The work highlights the importance of depth and implicitly learning semi-supervised paradigms to improve ICL performance, and may spur further research into the connections between attention and traditional machine learning algorithms.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a solid theoretical analysis of semi-supervised ICL with linear attention models and proposes a practical looping strategy for TabFMs. While the theoretical scope is limited to linear attention and GMMs, the results provide valuable insights into the role of depth and offer a pathway for improving the performance of ICL with limited labeled data. The connection to Expectation-Maximization is particularly significant. The practical evaluation on real-world datasets adds to the paper's credibility.

Given the limitations discussed above, the assigned score is as follows:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v1)
- **Authors**: Camila Zurdo Tagliabue, Heloisa Oss Boll, Aykut Erdem, Erkut Erdem, Iacer Calixto
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in clinical decision support, yet current evaluation methods often fail to distinguish genuine medical reasoning from superficial patterns. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework for probing fine-grained clinical understanding. We construct a dataset of ICU discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, generating both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) versions with controlled single-variable counterfactuals targeting demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate five LLMs spanning general-purpose and medically fine-tuned variants, under both zero-shot and fine-tuned settings. We assess model behavior via (1) input-level sensitivity - how counterfactuals alter the likelihood of a note; and (2) downstream reasoning - how they affect predicted hospital length-of-stay. Our results show that zero-shot models exhibit more coherent counterfactual reasoning patterns, while fine-tuned models tend to be more stable yet less responsive to clinically meaningful changes. Notably, demographic factors subtly but consistently influence outputs, emphasizing the importance of fairness-aware evaluation. This work highlights the utility of behavioral testing in exposing the reasoning strategies of clinical LLMs and informing the design of safer, more transparent medical AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeVisE, a behavioral testing framework for evaluating the fine-grained clinical understanding of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the medical domain. DeVisE uses a dataset of ICU discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, generating both raw and template-based versions with controlled counterfactuals across demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes.  The study evaluates several LLMs (general-purpose and medically fine-tuned) under zero-shot and fine-tuned settings, assessing input-level sensitivity (how counterfactuals alter note likelihood) and downstream reasoning (how they affect predicted hospital length-of-stay). The results reveal differences in model behavior, with zero-shot models exhibiting more coherent counterfactual reasoning patterns and fine-tuned models being more stable but less responsive to clinically meaningful changes.  The study also highlights the subtle yet consistent influence of demographic factors, emphasizing the importance of fairness-aware evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has some novelty. While behavioral testing and counterfactual analysis are not entirely new in NLP, their application to the medical domain, particularly focusing on both raw and template-based clinical notes with controlled vital sign and demographic counterfactuals, is a worthwhile contribution. The focus on nuanced clinical variables rather than simply overall accuracy metrics is also a plus. The comparison between fine-tuned vs. zero-shot *in this context* is valuable.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant problem: the limitations of traditional evaluation metrics in assessing true medical reasoning in LLMs. The DeVisE framework provides a more granular way to probe model behavior and expose potential biases or spurious correlations.  The findings, such as the differing responses of fine-tuned versus zero-shot models and the influence of demographic factors, offer actionable insights for developing safer and more transparent medical AI systems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined framework:** DeVisE provides a clear and structured methodology for behavioral testing of medical LLMs.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** The creation of both raw and template-based notes, along with validated counterfactuals, is a valuable resource.
    *   **Insightful analysis:**  The paper identifies important behavioral differences between different types of LLMs (fine-tuned vs. zero-shot, medical vs. general-purpose).
    *   **Focus on fairness:** The explicit consideration of demographic biases is a crucial and timely aspect of the evaluation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope:**  The study is limited to ICU discharge summaries from a single hospital (MIMIC-IV), which may limit the generalizability of the findings. The paper acknowledges this. The study focused only on admission data, though that is justified given the objective.
    *   **Downstream task selection:** While LOS prediction is clinically relevant, it may not fully capture the range of reasoning capabilities required in medical applications. Other potential downstream tasks might be considered in future work.
    *   **Limited model choices:** A broader range of LLMs could be considered in further studies.
    *   **Severity Score Definition**: It isn't totally clear if the severity scores themselves could bias the models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The DeVisE framework has the potential to influence the way medical LLMs are evaluated, shifting the focus from simple accuracy to more nuanced measures of reasoning and fairness. It could also inform the development of more robust and transparent medical AI systems.  The insights into fine-tuning versus zero-shot performance are valuable for guiding model development strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of medical NLP by providing a behavioral testing framework that addresses the limitations of traditional evaluation metrics. The DeVisE framework offers a more granular approach to probing the reasoning capabilities and biases of LLMs in the medical domain. While the study has some limitations in terms of scope and model choices, the insights gained from the analysis are actionable and have the potential to influence future development and evaluation practices.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Acoustic Waveform Inversion with Image-to-Image Schrödinger Bridges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15346v1)
- **Authors**: A. S. Stankevich, I. B. Petrov
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in application of deep learning models to acoustic Full Waveform Inversion (FWI) are marked by the use of diffusion models as prior distributions for Bayesian-like inference procedures. The advantage of these methods is the ability to generate high-resolution samples, which are otherwise unattainable with classical inversion methods or other deep learning-based solutions. However, the iterative and stochastic nature of sampling from diffusion models along with heuristic nature of output control remain limiting factors for their applicability. For instance, an optimal way to include the approximate velocity model into diffusion-based inversion scheme remains unclear, even though it is considered an essential part of FWI pipeline. We address the issue by employing a Schr\"odinger Bridge that interpolates between the distributions of ground truth and smoothed velocity models. To facilitate the learning of nonlinear drifts that transfer samples between distributions we extend the concept of Image-to-Image Schr\"odinger Bridge ($\text{I}^2\text{SB}$) to conditional sampling, resulting in a conditional Image-to-Image Schr\"odinger Bridge (c$\text{I}^2\text{SB}$) framework. To validate our method, we assess its effectiveness in reconstructing the reference velocity model from its smoothed approximation, coupled with the observed seismic signal of fixed shape. Our experiments demonstrate that the proposed solution outperforms our reimplementation of conditional diffusion model suggested in earlier works, while requiring only a few neural function evaluations (NFEs) to achieve sample fidelity superior to that attained with supervised learning-based approach. The supplementary code implementing the algorithms described in this paper can be found in the repository https://github.com/stankevich-mipt/seismic_inversion_via_I2SB.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel approach to acoustic full waveform inversion (FWI) using a conditional Image-to-Image Schrödinger Bridge (cI2SB) framework. The method aims to improve the conditioning of the FWI problem by incorporating smoothed velocity models within a diffusion-based inversion scheme. The cI2SB interpolates between distributions of ground truth and smoothed velocity models, ensuring the inversion process converges to a sample from the reference velocity model distribution. The authors adapt the I2SB framework to conditional sampling and demonstrate its effectiveness in reconstructing reference velocity models from smoothed approximations and observed seismic signals. Experiments on the OpenFWI dataset show that the proposed solution outperforms conditional diffusion models while requiring fewer neural function evaluations (NFEs). A key aspect is the training scheme that allows the model to learn nonlinear drifts for both conditional and unconditional settings, trading off diversity and fidelity via a mixing weight parameter.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel application of Schrödinger Bridges to the acoustic FWI problem. While diffusion models and score-based generative modeling have been explored for seismic inversion, the introduction of a *conditional* image-to-image framework based on Schrödinger Bridges and the end-to-end training procedure differentiating between conditional and unconditional is a distinct contribution. The adaptation of I2SB to *conditional* seismic inversion using cI2SB is the primary novel component.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in addressing the limitations of existing diffusion-based FWI methods. Specifically, it provides a theoretically sound approach to incorporate approximate velocity models as prior information, a crucial aspect of FWI. The demonstrated reduction in NFEs is also significant, as it directly impacts the computational cost and practicality of diffusion-based inversion. Furthermore, enabling better control over the fidelity-diversity tradeoff is a practical benefit.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Solid theoretical grounding in Schrödinger Bridges and optimal transport.
    *   Clear articulation of the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on a standard benchmark (OpenFWI).
    *   Demonstrated performance improvements over other diffusion-based and supervised learning methods.
    *   Demonstrated reduction in NFEs required.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The distortion operator construction is based on generic Gaussian smoothing and has no real-world basis. While the method works, there is no theoretical reason to believe it is near-optimal for seismic velocity fields.
    *   The reliance on the shape of conditioning vector.
    *   Limited exploration of real-world datasets.
    *   The ablation studies have limited scope.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a definite contribution to the field by adapting the Image-to-Image Schrödinger Bridge framework to the problem of acoustic waveform inversion. It addresses key limitations of diffusion-based approaches, namely the lack of theoretical grounding for conditioning and high computational cost. While there is room for improvement in aspects such as the design of the distortion operator and evaluation on real data, the paper presents a sound methodology, solid experiments, and promising results. The impact is potentially substantial because the theoretical grounding opens the door to better conditioning schemes.

- **Classification**: cs.LG

### SANSKRITI: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating Language Models' Knowledge of Indian Culture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15355v1)
- **Authors**: Arijit Maji, Raghvendra Kumar, Akash Ghosh, Anushka, Sriparna Saha
- **Abstract**: Language Models (LMs) are indispensable tools shaping modern workflows, but their global effectiveness depends on understanding local socio-cultural contexts. To address this, we introduce SANSKRITI, a benchmark designed to evaluate language models' comprehension of India's rich cultural diversity. Comprising 21,853 meticulously curated question-answer pairs spanning 28 states and 8 union territories, SANSKRITI is the largest dataset for testing Indian cultural knowledge. It covers sixteen key attributes of Indian culture: rituals and ceremonies, history, tourism, cuisine, dance and music, costume, language, art, festivals, religion, medicine, transport, sports, nightlife, and personalities, providing a comprehensive representation of India's cultural tapestry. We evaluate SANSKRITI on leading Large Language Models (LLMs), Indic Language Models (ILMs), and Small Language Models (SLMs), revealing significant disparities in their ability to handle culturally nuanced queries, with many models struggling in region-specific contexts. By offering an extensive, culturally rich, and diverse dataset, SANSKRITI sets a new standard for assessing and improving the cultural understanding of LMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SANSKRITI, a new benchmark designed to evaluate language models' understanding of Indian culture.  The dataset consists of 21,853 question-answer pairs covering 16 key attributes of Indian culture across all 28 states and 8 union territories. The authors evaluate various large language models (LLMs), Indic Language Models (ILMs), and small language models (SLMs) on SANSKRITI, revealing disparities in their ability to handle culturally nuanced queries. The authors emphasize that the benchmark aims to improve cultural understanding in LMs and promote inclusivity in AI. They make the dataset publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper explicitly tackles a critical gap in the evaluation of language models: their understanding of culturally-specific knowledge, especially within the diverse Indian context. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on multilingualism rather than cultural depth, so this is a pertinent addition.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The scale and breadth of the SANSKRITI dataset, covering all Indian states/UTs and a wide range of cultural attributes, is a significant strength. It offers a more thorough assessment than smaller, regionally-limited datasets.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The annotation process, involving a team of forty annotators with expertise in linguistics and Indian culture, including measures for quality assurance, cultural sensitivity, and cross-validation, strengthens the reliability of the dataset.
    *   **Public Availability:** Making the dataset publicly available fosters further research and development of culturally-aware AI systems.
    *   **Zero Shot Evaluation:** The choice of Zero shot Evaluation is commendable to assess the inherent capacity of existing LLMs to handle Indian cultural questions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Question Type Limitations:** The exclusive use of multiple-choice, fact-based questions is a limitation. This type of question might not fully capture deeper reasoning capabilities or the ability to apply cultural understanding in more complex scenarios. While efficient for evaluation, it can oversimplify the assessment.
    *   **Lack of State-Specific Multilingual Queries:** The absence of state-specific multilingual question-answer pairs is a noteworthy weakness. In India, a state's cultural context is also heavily influenced by regional dialects and languages.
    *   **VQA's Absence:** The absence of visual data might ignore important cultural aspects that are better conveyed through pictures and videos.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While the error analysis pinpoints specific challenges, it could be more detailed, providing insights into why certain types of questions were more difficult for particular models. The given explanation is too short for any conclusive insights.
    *   **Comparision Metric Limited:** The paper chooses to use only the accuracy score. In particular, precision/recall scores can provide better insights into how a model is performing on the given datasets.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The creation of SANSKRITI is itself a novel contribution.
    *   The benchmarking of prominent LLMs, ILMs, and SLMs provides valuable insights into their current capabilities and limitations regarding Indian cultural understanding. This information can guide future model development.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a comprehensive benchmark that addresses a critical gap in language model evaluation. The comprehensiveness, careful annotation process, and public availability make it a valuable resource for the AI community. However, the limitations regarding question types, the absence of visual data, or more fine-grained state-specific multilingual evaluations constrain its immediate impact. Further enhancements addressing these limitations would significantly increase the dataset's long-term influence on the field.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Sampling 3D Molecular Conformers with Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15378v1)
- **Authors**: J. Thorben Frank, Winfried Ripken, Gregor Lied, Klaus-Robert Müller, Oliver T. Unke, Stefan Chmiela
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have demonstrated strong performance in generative modeling, particularly in image synthesis, making them a compelling choice for molecular conformer generation. However, applying DiTs to molecules introduces novel challenges, such as integrating discrete molecular graph information with continuous 3D geometry, handling Euclidean symmetries, and designing conditioning mechanisms that generalize across molecules of varying sizes and structures. We propose DiTMC, a framework that adapts DiTs to address these challenges through a modular architecture that separates the processing of 3D coordinates from conditioning on atomic connectivity. To this end, we introduce two complementary graph-based conditioning strategies that integrate seamlessly with the DiT architecture. These are combined with different attention mechanisms, including both standard non-equivariant and SO(3)-equivariant formulations, enabling flexible control over the trade-off between between accuracy and computational efficiency. Experiments on standard conformer generation benchmarks (GEOM-QM9, -DRUGS, -XL) demonstrate that DiTMC achieves state-of-the-art precision and physical validity. Our results highlight how architectural choices and symmetry priors affect sample quality and efficiency, suggesting promising directions for large-scale generative modeling of molecular structures. Code available at https://github.com/ML4MolSim/dit_mc.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiTMC, a novel framework for generating 3D molecular conformers using Diffusion Transformers (DiTs). Recognizing the challenges of applying DiTs to molecules – such as integrating graph data with 3D geometry, handling symmetries, and generalizing across varying molecular structures – DiTMC employs a modular architecture that separates the processing of 3D coordinates from conditioning on atomic connectivity. The authors propose two complementary graph-based conditioning strategies seamlessly integrated with the DiT architecture. They experiment with different attention mechanisms, including both non-equivariant and SO(3)-equivariant formulations, to analyze the trade-offs between accuracy and computational efficiency. Experimental results on standard conformer generation benchmarks (GEOM-QM9, -DRUGS, -XL) demonstrate that DiTMC achieves state-of-the-art precision and physical validity.  The authors highlight the importance of architectural choices and symmetry priors on sample quality and efficiency, suggesting pathways for large-scale generative modeling of molecular structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's core novelty lies in adapting the diffusion transformer architecture (DiT), primarily used in image synthesis, to the problem of 3D molecular conformer generation. While diffusion models and transformers have individually been used in molecular generation previously, their combination within a modular framework specifically tailored to address the unique challenges of molecular geometry is relatively novel. The conditioning strategies and systematic investigation of attention mechanisms, including SO(3)-equivariant variants, are also notable contributions.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance rests on several factors:

    * **Performance:** Achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) results on established benchmarks indicates that DiTMC offers a practical improvement over existing methods in terms of precision and physical validity. This is important for applications like drug discovery and materials design, where accurate conformer generation is crucial.
    * **Systematic Analysis:**  The thorough ablation studies, particularly the investigation of different attention mechanisms and symmetry priors, provide valuable insights into the design choices that affect sample quality and computational cost. This helps the community understand the trade-offs and potentially build more efficient and accurate models in the future.
    * **Modular Design:** DiTMC's modular architecture is itself a significant aspect. It allows for the easy substitution and comparison of different components (e.g., attention mechanisms, conditioning strategies), facilitating future research and development.
    * **Scalability Potential:** The paper suggests that DiTMC is a promising candidate for large-scale conformer generation, though this claim is primarily based on computational efficiency rather than a demonstration on very large molecules.

* **Strengths:**

    * Strong empirical results on standard benchmarks.
    * Thorough ablation studies and analysis of design choices.
    * Clear and well-structured presentation.
    * Modular architecture promoting future research.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * The scalability to truly large molecules is only suggested, not explicitly demonstrated. The experiments are limited to the GEOM datasets, which have limitations in molecule size.
    * The computational cost of SO(3)-equivariant attention remains a significant hurdle. While the paper acknowledges this and explores trade-offs, a more efficient implementation or alternative approach would be valuable.
    * The improvement in recall metrics compared to some other methods is not as significant as the improvement in precision. In certain applications, recall (ground truth coverage) is equally important, if not more.
    * While the authors analyze ensemble properties, further investigation into other chemically relevant properties and out-of-distribution performance on diverse molecular datasets would strengthen the claims of physical validity.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    * It encourages the application of DiT architectures to other geometric deep learning problems beyond image synthesis.
    * It provides a framework for systematically exploring architectural choices in molecular generation models.
    * It highlights the importance of symmetry priors and trade-offs between accuracy and computational cost.
    * It motivates the development of more efficient equivariant neural networks.

Given the combination of novelty (DiT adaptation and modular design), significant empirical results, systematic analysis, and potential for future influence, but acknowledging the limitations in demonstrating scalability and certain aspects of physical validity, a score of 7 is warranted.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Model Knowledge meets Diffusion Model: Diffusion-assisted Data-free Image Synthesis with Alignment of Domain and Class
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15381v1)
- **Authors**: Yujin Kim, Hyunsoo Kim, Hyunwoo J. Kim, Suhyun Kim
- **Abstract**: Open-source pre-trained models hold great potential for diverse applications, but their utility declines when their training data is unavailable. Data-Free Image Synthesis (DFIS) aims to generate images that approximate the learned data distribution of a pre-trained model without accessing the original data. However, existing DFIS meth ods produce samples that deviate from the training data distribution due to the lack of prior knowl edge about natural images. To overcome this limitation, we propose DDIS, the first Diffusion-assisted Data-free Image Synthesis method that leverages a text-to-image diffusion model as a powerful image prior, improving synthetic image quality. DDIS extracts knowledge about the learned distribution from the given model and uses it to guide the diffusion model, enabling the generation of images that accurately align with the training data distribution. To achieve this, we introduce Domain Alignment Guidance (DAG) that aligns the synthetic data domain with the training data domain during the diffusion sampling process. Furthermore, we optimize a single Class Alignment Token (CAT) embedding to effectively capture class-specific attributes in the training dataset. Experiments on PACS and Ima geNet demonstrate that DDIS outperforms prior DFIS methods by generating samples that better reflect the training data distribution, achieving SOTA performance in data-free applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diffusion-Assisted Data-Free Image Synthesis (DDIS), a novel approach to generate synthetic images that mimic the data distribution learned by a pre-trained model when the original training data is unavailable. DDIS leverages a text-to-image diffusion model as a strong image prior to guide the synthesis process, addressing the limitations of existing Data-Free Image Synthesis (DFIS) methods that often produce unnatural and deviated samples. The key innovations include Domain Alignment Guidance (DAG), which aligns the domain of synthetic data with the training data domain using batch normalization statistics, and a Class Alignment Token (CAT) that encodes class-specific attributes not explicitly defined in class labels. Experimental results on PACS and ImageNet demonstrate that DDIS outperforms prior DFIS methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance in data-free applications like knowledge distillation and pruning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using a text-to-image diffusion model as an image prior in DFIS is a significant step forward. Previous DFIS methods often struggle to generate realistic images due to the vast search space and lack of prior knowledge. DAG and CAT further refine the synthesis process, improving domain and class alignment. The application of batch normalization statistics for domain alignment is an interesting and well-motivated approach. However, the general idea of guiding image synthesis with prior knowledge isn't entirely new, and the novelty lies in the specific implementation with diffusion models and the DAG/CAT mechanisms.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important problem: leveraging pre-trained models when training data is inaccessible.  Successfully generating high-quality synthetic data unlocks various applications like knowledge distillation, model pruning, and domain adaptation.  The demonstrated improvements in these applications showcase the potential impact of DDIS. The results across multiple datasets, including PACS and ImageNet, are strong.  Furthermore, the paper carefully examines different design choices through ablation studies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the limitations of existing DFIS methods and motivates the need for a stronger image prior.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The use of a diffusion model and the specific DAG/CAT mechanisms are well-justified and grounded in relevant literature.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results demonstrating the superiority of DDIS over existing methods.
*   **Ablation Studies:**  Thorough ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of each component (DAG, CAT) and validate design choices.
*   **Practical Applications:** The demonstration of DDIS in data-free knowledge distillation and pruning highlights the practical utility of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Sketch Domain Limitations:** The acknowledged limitations in the sketch domain suggest that the method may not generalize well to all types of data distributions. This is a concern for the general applicability of the technique, and the paper provides limited discussion on how to mitigate such issues.
*   **Dependency on Batch Normalization:** The reliance on batch normalization statistics in DAG limits the applicability to models that do not employ batch normalization layers. This somewhat restricts its generality.
*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper discusses computational efficiency compared to older DFIS methods, the diffusion process itself is computationally intensive. Further analysis of the trade-off between synthesis quality and computational cost would be beneficial.
*   **Limited comparison with more recent techniques:** The paper could have benefited from comparisons with newer DFIS techniques, which have emerged in the rapidly evolving landscape of data-free learning.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** While the techniques introduced are innovative, the overall contribution could be considered somewhat incremental. This is because it builds upon well-established techniques like diffusion models and batch normalization statistics, rather than introducing entirely novel paradigms.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of data-free learning. By demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models as image priors, it opens up new avenues for research and development in DFIS. The DAG and CAT mechanisms provide valuable insights into how to align synthetic data with specific domains and classes. The practical applications demonstrated in the paper are likely to inspire further research on leveraging DDIS for various tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to data-free image synthesis. The combination of a text-to-image diffusion model with DAG and CAT addresses a significant limitation of existing DFIS methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance. The strong empirical results, ablation studies, and practical applications demonstrate the potential impact of the work. The limitations concerning the sketch domain, dependency on Batch Normalization, some recent DFIS methods and potential incremental nature of contribution slightly temper the overall impact. Nonetheless, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field and is likely to stimulate further research in this area.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Provable Maximum Entropy Manifold Exploration via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15385v1)
- **Authors**: Riccardo De Santi, Marin Vlastelica, Ya-Ping Hsieh, Zebang Shen, Niao He, Andreas Krause
- **Abstract**: Exploration is critical for solving real-world decision-making problems such as scientific discovery, where the objective is to generate truly novel designs rather than mimic existing data distributions. In this work, we address the challenge of leveraging the representational power of generative models for exploration without relying on explicit uncertainty quantification. We introduce a novel framework that casts exploration as entropy maximization over the approximate data manifold implicitly defined by a pre-trained diffusion model. Then, we present a novel principle for exploration based on density estimation, a problem well-known to be challenging in practice. To overcome this issue and render this method truly scalable, we leverage a fundamental connection between the entropy of the density induced by a diffusion model and its score function. Building on this, we develop an algorithm based on mirror descent that solves the exploration problem as sequential fine-tuning of a pre-trained diffusion model. We prove its convergence to the optimal exploratory diffusion model under realistic assumptions by leveraging recent understanding of mirror flows. Finally, we empirically evaluate our approach on both synthetic and high-dimensional text-to-image diffusion, demonstrating promising results.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and can provide a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of exploration using generative diffusion models.  The authors propose a novel framework that formulates exploration as entropy maximization over the approximate data manifold implicitly defined by a pre-trained diffusion model. They introduce a new principle for exploration grounded in density estimation, which is made scalable by leveraging the connection between a diffusion model's density and its score function. An algorithm based on mirror descent is developed, which fine-tunes the pre-trained diffusion model to solve the exploration problem. The paper provides theoretical convergence guarantees and demonstrates the approach's effectiveness through synthetic and high-dimensional text-to-image diffusion experiments. The core idea is to use the inherent representational power of diffusion models, specifically their ability to capture complex manifolds, to guide exploration without relying on explicit uncertainty quantification.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel elements. The formulation of exploration as maximum entropy over a learned manifold is a key contribution. The use of the score function to bypass direct density estimation is also a clever and potentially impactful technique. The convergence guarantees, although relying on specific assumptions, add to the theoretical rigor.  The use of mirror flows for this purpose appears novel in the context of diffusion model fine-tuning.

*   **Significance:** Exploration is a crucial problem in many fields, especially scientific discovery. Leveraging generative models for exploration is an active area of research, and this paper offers a promising approach that tackles a key challenge: scaling exploration to high-dimensional spaces. The provided framework can potentially lead to new algorithms and methodologies for various exploration tasks using generative models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The paper provides theoretical guarantees for the convergence of the proposed algorithm, which is a strong point.
    *   **Scalability:** By leveraging the score function, the method avoids direct density estimation, which makes it scalable to high-dimensional problems.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The empirical results on both synthetic and text-to-image diffusion demonstrate the practical potential of the approach.
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper provides a clear and well-defined problem setting for exploration with diffusion models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical convergence guarantees rely on certain assumptions (e.g., exact score estimation, exact optimization, support compatibility, precompactness) which may not always hold in practice.  The gap between these assumptions and the practical implementation needs careful consideration. The reliance on the perfect knowledge of the initial dataset also restricts the methods adaptability.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** It is unclear how the method compares to other existing exploration techniques for generative models, such as those using uncertainty quantification.
    *   **Proxy metrics:** The evaluation of diversity and originality are based on proxy metrics, that do not accurately represent the "entropy."
    *   **Evaluation:** The evaluation section lacks a solid, quantitative validation to properly showcase the strengths of the proposed algorithm.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the method can be further developed and validated with more rigorous empirical studies, it has the potential to become a valuable tool for exploration using generative models in various domains. The connection between entropy and score function could also inspire new research directions.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel and theoretically grounded framework for exploration with diffusion models. The use of the score function for scalable density estimation is a key innovation. While the theoretical results rely on certain assumptions, and the empirical validation could be more extensive and in comparison with other exploration techniques, the paper offers a promising approach that has the potential to advance the field. The work also includes additional materials and implementation that could make it helpful for those to develop similar algorithms.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Targeted Lexical Injection: Unlocking Latent Cross-Lingual Alignment in Lugha-Llama via Early-Layer LoRA Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15415v1)
- **Authors**: Stanley Ngugi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, yet their performance in low-resource languages (LRLs), such as Swahili, often lags due to data scarcity and underrepresentation in pre-training. A key challenge is achieving robust cross-lingual lexical alignment, crucial for tasks like translation and cross-lingual information retrieval. This paper introduces Targeted Lexical Injection (TLI), a novel and efficient fine-tuning approach. We first demonstrate that Lugha-Llama-8B-wura, a Swahili-centric LLM, exhibits strong, near-perfect lexical alignment for Swahili-English word pairs in its early internal layers (specifically Layer 2, with ~0.99998 average cosine similarity based on a pilot study), a capability not fully reflected in its final output representations (baseline ~0.32 similarity on our evaluation set). TLI leverages this insight by using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and a contrastive learning objective to fine-tune the model, specifically targeting embeddings from this empirically identified optimal early layer. Our experiments show that TLI significantly improves the output-level lexical alignment for 623 trained Swahili-English word pairs, increasing average cosine similarity from 0.3211 to 0.4113 (+28.08%, p < 1.33 x 10^-240). More importantly, these improvements generalize remarkably well to 63 unseen control word pairs, with similarity increasing from 0.3143 to 0.4033 (+28.32%, p < 7.17 x 10^-27). These findings suggest TLI enhances the model's ability to preserve and propagate its inherent early-layer cross-lingual knowledge, offering a parameter-efficient and effective strategy for improving lexical alignment in LRL-focused LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Targeted Lexical Injection (TLI), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique (using LoRA) to improve cross-lingual lexical alignment in Lugha-Llama, a Swahili-centric LLM. The key idea is to exploit the observation that strong, near-perfect lexical alignment exists in the early layers (Layer 2) of Lugha-Llama but is not effectively propagated to the final output layer. TLI leverages Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and contrastive learning to fine-tune the model, specifically targeting embeddings from this optimal early layer. Experiments demonstrate significant improvements in output-level lexical alignment for both trained and unseen Swahili-English word pairs, suggesting that TLI enhances the model's ability to preserve and propagate its inherent cross-lingual knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty of the Approach:** The core idea of identifying an optimal internal layer with strong pre-existing alignment and then targeting it with LoRA and contrastive learning is a genuinely novel approach. It moves beyond typical fine-tuning by explicitly exploiting and amplifying latent capabilities within the model.  The identification of the *specific layer* exhibiting this alignment is also a valuable contribution.
    * **Parameter Efficiency:** LoRA allows for effective fine-tuning with minimal additional trainable parameters, making the approach practical for LLMs.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The quantitative results are compelling, demonstrating statistically significant improvements in lexical alignment on both trained and unseen word pairs.  The generalization to unseen vocabulary is particularly significant.
    * **Clear and Well-Written:**  The paper is clearly written and logically structured. The methodology is well-defined, and the results are presented effectively.
    * **Addresses an Important Problem:**  The issue of cross-lingual lexical alignment in low-resource languages is a crucial bottleneck in multilingual NLP, making the paper's focus relevant.
    * **Qualitative Analysis:**  The t-SNE visualizations (though not as strong as the quantitative results) complement the numerical findings and offer some insights into how TLI reorganizes the embedding space.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to Lugha-Llama and the Swahili-English language pair.  While the principles may be generalizable, this requires further investigation.  The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    * **Dataset Size:** While the generalization to the control set is a significant finding, the absolute size of both the training and control sets is relatively small. A larger dataset would increase confidence in the robustness of the results.
    * **Focus on Lexical Alignment:** The evaluation primarily focuses on isolated lexical pair similarity.  The impact on higher-level semantic understanding or downstream tasks is not explored.
    * **Interpretability:** While the paper hypothesizes about improved information flow, the exact neural mechanisms underlying TLI remain unclear. Deeper interpretability analysis is needed.
    * **Ablation Studies:**  There is no ablation study varying the layers used.

* **Significance:**
    * The paper offers a promising parameter-efficient strategy for improving lexical understanding in LLMs for low-resource languages.
    * The finding of strong latent alignment in early layers is a significant insight into the internal workings of LLMs, which may influence future research directions.
    *  The paper contributes a novel method that can potentially be adapted and applied to other LLMs and language pairs to improve cross-lingual performance.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * This research could stimulate further investigations into layer-specific fine-tuning techniques for improving cross-lingual capabilities.
    * The findings may influence the development of more effective and efficient methods for adapting LLMs to low-resource languages.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper's strength lies in its novel approach and the compelling empirical results, especially the generalization finding. It offers a practical and parameter-efficient solution to a real-world problem in multilingual NLP. However, the limitations regarding language pair specificity, dataset size, and the lack of in-depth interpretability temper the overall impact. It addresses a relatively narrow focus on improving lexical understanding and leaves higher-level semantic implications unaddressed. Therefore, while being a solid and promising paper, it does not meet the criteria for the highest scores.

### Understanding GUI Agent Localization Biases through Logit Sharpness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15425v1)
- **Authors**: Xingjian Tao, Yiwei Wang, Yujun Cai, Zhicheng Yang, Jing Tang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled GUI agents to interact with operating systems by grounding language into spatial actions. Despite their promising performance, these models frequently exhibit hallucinations-systematic localization errors that compromise reliability. We propose a fine-grained evaluation framework that categorizes model predictions into four distinct types, revealing nuanced failure modes beyond traditional accuracy metrics. To better quantify model uncertainty, we introduce the Peak Sharpness Score (PSS), a metric that evaluates the alignment between semantic continuity and logits distribution in coordinate prediction. Building on this insight, we further propose Context-Aware Cropping, a training-free technique that improves model performance by adaptively refining input context. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our framework and methods provide actionable insights and enhance the interpretability and robustness of GUI agent behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Understanding GUI Agent Localization Biases through Logit Sharpness":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucinations (localization errors) in GUI agents, which are systems that can interact with graphical user interfaces. The authors propose a fine-grained evaluation framework that categorizes model predictions into correct responses, biased hallucinations, misleading hallucinations, and confusion hallucinations. They introduce a new metric called the Peak Sharpness Score (PSS) to quantify model uncertainty by assessing the alignment between semantic continuity and logit distribution in coordinate predictions.  Based on their analysis, they propose a training-free technique called Context-Aware Cropping to improve model performance by adaptively refining the input context. The paper presents experiments demonstrating the effectiveness of their framework and the cropping technique.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. The fine-grained classification of hallucination types provides a more nuanced understanding of errors in GUI agents than simple accuracy metrics. The Peak Sharpness Score (PSS) is a novel metric for quantifying uncertainty specifically tailored to the characteristics of coordinate prediction tasks, which is distinct from general uncertainty measures used in language models. Context-Aware Cropping is also a sensible and simple contribution.
* **Significance:** The problem of hallucinations in GUI agents is significant, as it directly impacts their reliability and usability in real-world applications. The proposed evaluation framework and PSS metric can help researchers better understand and diagnose the failure modes of GUI agents. The Context-Aware Cropping technique provides a practical approach for improving model performance without requiring retraining.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper presents a systematic analysis of hallucination behaviors in GUI agents, providing valuable insights into their failure modes.
    * **Novel Metrics:** The PSS metric is well-motivated and tailored to the specific characteristics of GUI localization tasks.
    * **Practical Solution:** The Context-Aware Cropping technique is simple, training-free, and demonstrably effective.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the proposed framework, metric, and technique.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Scope:**  The experimental evaluation primarily focuses on two GUI agent models (ShowUI-2B and UGround-V1-2B) trained on the Qwen2-VL framework. The generalizability of the findings to other GUI agent architectures and training paradigms could be further explored.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the Context-Aware Cropping technique improves accuracy, the magnitude of the improvement is not drastic. The improvement, while consistent, might not be revolutionary.
    * **Dependency on Image Quality:** The proposed methodology hinges on the quality and clarity of the visual representations presented to the GUI agent, particularly the clarity of the icon set that is being utilized. There's a potential limitation in generalizing the approach to scenarios that deal with noisy, incomplete, or abstract visual information. This aspect isn't significantly addressed in the paper.

**Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of GUI agents by providing a more nuanced understanding of hallucination behaviors and proposing practical methods for improving model performance. While the magnitude of the accuracy improvement with cropping is not huge, it has the advantages of being free from re-training, simple and effective. The evaluation framework and PSS are useful tools for diagnosing model failures. The paper fills a gap in the research by providing specific metrics and improvement strategies for GUI agent localisation.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper's novelty stems from the tailored approach to evaluating and mitigating hallucinations in GUI agents. The PSS metric is a well-motivated contribution, and the context-aware cropping offers a simple yet effective solution. The significance lies in the potential impact on improving the reliability and usability of these agents. The weaknesses are primarily related to the incremental nature of the performance improvements and that its dependent on the underlying quality of images presented to the model. While the contributions aren't paradigm-shifting, they represent a solid advance in the field and provide valuable tools and insights for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncovering Intention through LLM-Driven Code Snippet Description Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15453v1)
- **Authors**: Yusuf Sulistyo Nugroho, Farah Danisha Salam, Brittany Reid, Raula Gaikovina Kula, Kazumasa Shimari, Kenichi Matsumoto
- **Abstract**: Documenting code snippets is essential to pinpoint key areas where both developers and users should pay attention. Examples include usage examples and other Application Programming Interfaces (APIs), which are especially important for third-party libraries. With the rise of Large Language Models (LLMs), the key goal is to investigate the kinds of description developers commonly use and evaluate how well an LLM, in this case Llama, can support description generation. We use NPM Code Snippets, consisting of 185,412 packages with 1,024,579 code snippets. From there, we use 400 code snippets (and their descriptions) as samples. First, our manual classification found that the majority of original descriptions (55.5%) highlight example-based usage. This finding emphasizes the importance of clear documentation, as some descriptions lacked sufficient detail to convey intent. Second, the LLM correctly identified the majority of original descriptions as "Example" (79.75%), which is identical to our manual finding, showing a propensity for generalization. Third, compared to the originals, the produced description had an average similarity score of 0.7173, suggesting relevance but room for improvement. Scores below 0.9 indicate some irrelevance. Our results show that depending on the task of the code snippet, the intention of the document may differ from being instructions for usage, installations, or descriptive learning examples for any user of a library.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper and provide a critical evaluation, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Uncovering Intention through LLM-Driven Code Snippet Description Generation" explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating descriptions for code snippets found in README files, focusing on NPM packages. The authors manually classify the types of descriptions developers typically use (Instruction, Example, Unclear), then evaluate how well an LLM (Llama3) can classify existing descriptions and generate new ones. They find that developers often provide example-based descriptions. The LLM generally identifies the types correctly but leans towards generalization, favoring the "Example" category. While LLM-generated descriptions show moderate similarity to originals, discrepancies in classification suggest potential challenges. The authors conclude that LLMs can assist with documentation but require developer oversight to ensure accuracy and relevance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** Code documentation and the role of README files are crucial for software maintainability and usability. Automating parts of this process with LLMs is a worthwhile research direction.
    *   **Empirical Approach:** The study uses a real-world NPM dataset and a manual classification scheme, which lends credibility to the findings.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The research questions are well-defined, and the methodology is clearly explained (data collection, prompt design, classification, evaluation with BERTScore).
    *   **Addresses Hallucinations:** The paper touches on the important topic of LLM hallucinations (generating incorrect or misleading information), a major concern when applying LLMs to documentation.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their work, including potential data leakage and the lack of human judgment in the evaluation. This transparency is commendable.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The general idea of using LLMs for code documentation is not entirely new. Several prior studies have explored related tasks, such as generating inline comments, summarizing code repositories, and automating README generation.  This paper focuses specifically on code *snippet descriptions*, but the overall contribution appears incremental.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** Relying solely on BERTScore for evaluating the generated descriptions is a limitation. BERTScore captures semantic similarity but doesn't fully assess the quality, clarity, or accuracy of the descriptions from a human perspective. Human evaluation would have strengthened the findings significantly.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** While the authors mention the importance of prompt design, they don't provide much detail about the specific prompts used or the process of optimizing them. A more thorough discussion of prompt engineering strategies would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other LLMs:** The paper only considered Llama3. A more comprehensive study should compare the performance and classification characteristics of multiple LLMs in a more comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Generalization:** The findings are specific to NPM packages and README files. It's unclear how well they would generalize to other programming languages, documentation formats, or types of software projects.
    *   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** In conjunction with the quantitative evaluation, a qualitative analysis of the different types of errors would have been helpful.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of knowledge on applying LLMs to software engineering tasks.
    *   The focus on code snippet descriptions is a specific and relevant area within the broader field of code documentation.
    *   The findings provide insights into the types of descriptions developers commonly write and the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in generating them.
    *   The discussion of hallucinations and the need for developer oversight is important for guiding future research and practical applications of LLMs in documentation.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **5** to this paper.

*   The problem is relevant and the methodology is sound, including the manual annotation.  However, the novelty is limited, as the use of LLMs for code documentation is an active research area. The paper's contribution is primarily an incremental one, focusing on a specific aspect (code snippet descriptions).
*   The reliance on BERTScore alone is a major weakness in the evaluation. The lack of human evaluation limits the confidence in the results. A qualitative analysis of generated descriptions is also missing.
*   The limited experimentation with prompt engineering and lack of comparison with other LLMs further detract from the paper's overall impact.

Therefore, while the paper offers some valuable insights, the limitations in novelty and evaluation prevent it from being a highly significant contribution to the field.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 5/10

### RE-IMAGINE: Symbolic Benchmark Synthesis for Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15455v1)
- **Authors**: Xinnuo Xu, Rachel Lawrence, Kshitij Dubey, Atharva Pandey, Risa Ueno, Fabian Falck, Aditya V. Nori, Rahul Sharma, Amit Sharma, Javier Gonzalez
- **Abstract**: Recent Large Language Models (LLMs) have reported high accuracy on reasoning benchmarks. However, it is still unclear whether the observed results arise from true reasoning or from statistical recall of the training set. Inspired by the ladder of causation (Pearl, 2009) and its three levels (associations, interventions and counterfactuals), this paper introduces RE-IMAGINE, a framework to characterize a hierarchy of reasoning ability in LLMs, alongside an automated pipeline to generate problem variations at different levels of the hierarchy. By altering problems in an intermediate symbolic representation, RE-IMAGINE generates arbitrarily many problems that are not solvable using memorization alone. Moreover, the framework is general and can work across reasoning domains, including math, code, and logic. We demonstrate our framework on four widely-used benchmarks to evaluate several families of LLMs, and observe reductions in performance when the models are queried with problem variations. These assessments indicate a degree of reliance on statistical recall for past performance, and open the door to further research targeting skills across the reasoning hierarchy.
